Chapter 0
Preface

This thesis documents research which was carried out as part of an NWO
1
1

1

Nederlandse Organisatie voor Wetenschappelijk Onderzoek
research project under my promotors Vincent van Oostrom and Doaitse Swierstra titled ‘Realising Optimal Sharing’. The objective of the project was to investigate whether the theory of ‘optimal evaluation of the λ-calculus’ could be used in practise to increase the execution efficiency for programs written in functional programming languages. As regards the original project goal my research was unsuccessful. This is not due to a lack of results but due to a distraction: when approaching the subject of ‘optimal sharing’ – which is ‘dynamic’ in the sense that it concerns sharing that an evaluator maintains at run time – more fundamental, still unresolved questions emerged concerning ‘static’ sharing – which is the sharing inherent in a given program definition. Therefore the presented results are not about optimal and therefore ‘dynamic’ sharing but solely about ‘static’ sharing.

The research was carried out in close collaboration with Clemens Grabmayer. All of the fundamental ideas and results are due to joint efforts and fruitful – if sometimes fierce – debate. We complemented each other splendidly: I profited much from Clemens Grabmayer’s expertise with formal systems, while I myself could contribute my proficiency with functional programming languages and compiler construction; also I have worked out an implementation of our methods. The substance of this thesis is from three research papers
[
19
,
24
,
22
]
published in the context of my doctoral research with Grabmayer and Rochel as authors, presented here in a more coherent narrative with supplementary explanations and examples. In order to do justice to the Clemens Grabmayer’s substantial contribution, authors who wish to cite this thesis in their work are kindly advised to cite at least one of the papers alongside.

This document is structured according to these three papers:
chapter

0
introduces the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-formalism and our rewriting system for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. There we also pose the problems that are resolved in the following three chapters,
chapter

1
,
chapter

2
, and
chapter

3
, each of which corresponds to one of these papers. Note, that the formalisms in this thesis deviate to varying degrees from their original form in the papers. The changes were required for the formalims to be consistent throughout the chapters.

1
Abstract

In this thesis we investigate the relationship between finite terms in
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
, the λ-calculus with letrec, and the infinite λ-terms they express. We say that a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
expresses
a λ-term if the latter can be obtained as an infinite unfolding of the former. Unfolding is the process of substituting occurrences of function variables by the right-hand side of their definition. We consider the following questions:

(i)

How can we characterise those infinite λ-terms that are
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressible?

(ii)

given two
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms, how can we determine whether they have the same unfolding?

(iii)

given a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term, can we find a more compact version of the term with the same unfolding?

To tackle these questions we introduce and study the following formalisms:

∘

a rewriting system for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms into λ-terms

∘

a rewriting system for ‘observing’ λ-terms by dissecting their term structure

∘

higher-order and first-order graph formalisms together with translations between them as well as translations from and to
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}

We identify a first-order term graph formalism on which bisimulation preserves and reflects the unfolding semantics of
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
and which is closed under functional bisimulation. From this we derive efficient methods to determine whether two terms are equivalent under infinite unfolding and to compute the maximally shared form of a given
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term.

Chapter 0
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
and Unfolding

§ 0.1

(abstract)
.

This thesis concerns itself with terms in the λ-calculus with
\mathsf{letrec}
, specifically with
unfolding
these terms. Unfolding refers to the process of substituting occurrences of
\mathsf{let}
-bound variables by their definition. We define unfolding by means of a rewriting system. We study the properties of that rewriting system and build various formal systems on top of it to derive further results. These results include:

chapter

1
:

a characterisation of the infinite λ-terms that can be expressed finitely as
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms.

chapter

2
:

a graph representation for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressible λ-terms.

chapter

3
:

practical and efficient methods for transforming a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term into a maximally compact form; and deciding whether two
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms have the same unfolding.

§ 0.2

(required background)
.

The reader is expected to have some proficiency with functional programming languages based on the λ-calculus
[
12
,
6
]
(preferably Haskell), which is most likely required to understand the presented results and their relevance. Furthermore, the formal systems used for reasoning in this thesis are mostly rewriting systems. Therefore, at least a basic background in term rewriting
[
51
]
is assumed.

§ 0.3

(chapter overview)
.

This chapter gives an overview over
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
and outlines the perspective from which we will study this calculus. We provide definitions and basic properties of the rewriting system by which we unfold
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. Then we will pose the questions and problems to contextualise the results presented in the following chapters.

1
Introduction

§ 1.1

(
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
as an abstraction of functional programming languages)
.

The λ-calculus is a formal system in computer science and logic for expressing computation. It is the model of computation at the core of functional programming languages. In this thesis we will look at one specific instance of the λ-calculus, namely the untyped λ-calculus extended by the
\mathsf{letrec}
-construct, or in short
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
. In that form, it serves well as a minimalistic abstraction of functional programming languages like Haskell. While Haskell is a typed language, it is typically translated into a simplified form during the compilation process in which type information is discarded (
type erasure
). Thus types can be regarded as auxiliary means for the programmer, and can be neglected when looking at the evaluation semantics of a type-checked program.

§ 1.2

(the
\mathsf{letrec}
-construct)
.

The
\mathsf{letrec}
-construct serves a number of purposes. By allowing us to bind subterms to variables it adds to the simple, untyped λ-calculus the means for:

modularisation:

Subterms with a specific purpose can be given a descriptive name and more easily be treated as entities of their own.

sharing:

Instead of repeating identical subterms at different locations, a subterm can be defined once and be referenced by its function definition multiple times.

cyclicity:

Function definitions can contain references to themselves, allowing for cyclic (and mutually cyclic) bindings.

While all the above can also be achieved by a collection of top-level bindings, the
\mathsf{letrec}
has one distinguishing characteristic, which is that function bindings can be defined at any position in the term. The scope of thus defined function bindings does not range over the entire program but can only be used underneath the position of their definition. This locality of definitions allows for a more structured approach to programming. Also, it can refer to λ-variables bound outside of the binding which results in fewer β-reduction steps during evaluation.

Before we concern ourselves with formal definitions let us first fix some notation and terminology.

Notation 1.3

(
\mathsf{let}
=
\mathsf{letrec}
)
.

Throughout this thesis we write
\mathsf{let}
to denote the
\mathsf{letrec}
-construct, as is done in Haskell.

Terminology 1.4

(
\mathsf{let}
-expression)
.

A term that starts with a
\mathsf{let}
, thus a term that has the form
\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}
, is called a
\mathsf{let}
-expression
.

Terminology 1.5

(binding group)
.

We call the collection of bindings
B
defined in a
\mathsf{let}
-expression
\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}
a
binding group
.

Terminology 1.6

(function binding, function variable,
\mathsf{let}
-bound variable)
.

We call the equations of a
\mathsf{let}
-expression
function bindings
or simply
bindings
. We call the variable on the left-hand side of a binding a
\mathsf{let}
-bound variable
, or a
function variable
.
1
1

1

While a term bound by
\mathsf{let}
to a variable may well be constant (i.e. not a λ-abstraction) we still call such a binding a function binding and the
\mathsf{let}
-bound variable a function variable.

Terminology 1.7

(body)
.

The part
L
of the expression
\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}
is called the
body
of the
\mathsf{let}
-expression.

Example 1.8
.

Consider the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
\mathsf{let}\;{\mathrm{fix}=λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({\mathrm{fix}}\;{f})}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\mathrm{fix}}
. The binding group of the
\mathsf{let}
-expression consists of a single function binding
\mathrm{fix}=λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({\mathrm{fix}}\;{f})}}
which binds the term
λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({\mathrm{fix}}\;{f})}}
to the function variable
\mathrm{fix}
. The body of the
\mathsf{let}
-expression consists of an occurrence of the function variable
\mathrm{fix}
.

Remark 1.9

(Turing completeness, well-typedness, termination)
.

Typed λ-calculi (with finite types) are strongly normalising, which means that every computation in such a calculus terminates. Therefore such calculi are not Turing complete. However, strong normalisation does not hold for typed λ-calculi that allow for cyclic definitions, such as
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
. Therefore the
\mathsf{letrec}
can also be seen as a way to restore Turing completeness for a typed λ-calculus. Other approaches would be fixed-point combinators or top-level bindings. The
\mathsf{letrec}
offers the most convenience from a programmer’s point of view.

§ 1.10

(infinite unfolding)
.

A
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
can be seen as a finite representation of a (possibly) infinite λ-term
M
, which we obtain by repeatedly substituting every occurrence of a function variable by the right-hand side of the corresponding binding. We call
M
the
infinite unfolding
of
L
, and we write
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=M
. A definition for
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
is given later (
definition

7.1
).

Example 1.11

(infinite unfolding of
{\mathrm{fix}}\;{\mathrm{id}}
)
.

Let us consider a naive implementation of the
\mathrm{fix}
-function applied to
λ{x}.\,{x}
, the identity function:

\displaystyle\llbracket{(\mathsf{let}\;{\mathrm{fix}=λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({\mathrm{fix}}\;{f})}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\mathrm{fix}})}\;{(λ{x}.\,{x})}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}

\displaystyle={(λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({(λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({\dots}\;{f})}})}\;{f})}})}\;{(λ{x}.\,{x})}

Notation 1.12

(ellipsis:
\dots
)
.

Note that (as in the example above) we will be using the ellipsis as an informal notation for ‘and so on’ extensively. It occurs both in infinite terms and infinite rewriting sequences. Instead of providing a first-order formalisation of the ellipsis, we trust that the reader will find the context always sufficient to infer the shape of the entire term or rewriting sequence.

§ 1.13

(evaluation and unfolding)
.

While semantics of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-based programming languages can be defined via infinite unfolding, evaluation of programs on a computer cannot operate on infinite terms but must rely on a finite representation. The evaluation of such programs requires in addition to β-reduction a mechanism to unfold
\mathsf{let}
-expressions. This is best modelled in a rewriting system that extends the λ-calculus by unfolding rules.

Example 1.14

(leftmost-outermost evaluation of
{\mathrm{fix}}\;{\mathrm{id}}
)
.

Let us evaluate a small example program to see how unfolding comes into play in the course of evaluating
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms:

{(\mathsf{let}\;{\mathrm{fix}=λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({\mathrm{fix}}\;{f})}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\mathrm{fix}})}\;{(λ{x}.\,{x})}

This term has no (visible) β-redex as the
λ{f}.\,{\dots}
-abstraction is ‘blocked’ by the surrounding
\mathsf{let}
. In order to turn it into a proper β-redex we need to unfold its definition, which means essentially substituting the right-hand side of the binding for both occurrences of the function variable
\mathrm{fix}
:

\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\triangledown}\quad{(λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({\mathsf{let}\;{\mathrm{fix}=λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({\mathrm{fix}}\;{f})}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\mathrm{fix}})}\;{f}}})}\;{(λ{x}.\,{x})}

In the formalisation of unfolding as a rewriting system (see
section

3
) this requires actually a number of steps, hence the many-step reduction
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\triangledown}
. Now that we have a visible β-redex to contract, we can substitute
λ{x}.\,{x}
for
f
:

\mathrel{\to}_{β}\quad{(λ{x}.\,{x})}\;{({(\mathsf{let}\;{\mathrm{fix}=λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({\mathrm{fix}}\;{f})}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\mathrm{fix}})}\;{(λ{x}.\,{x})})}

Next, we apply the identity function and we arrive at the initial term and evaluation continues as above and will thus never terminate.

\mathrel{\to}_{β}\quad{(\mathsf{let}\;{\mathrm{fix}=λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({\mathrm{fix}}\;{f})}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\mathrm{fix}})}\;{(λ{x}.\,{x})}\;\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\triangledown}\;\dots

Remark 1.15

(
\triangledown
)
.

Using the triangle as a symbol for unfolding is inspired by graph rewriting systems like Lambdascope
[
47
]
where sharing is indicated by an explicit sharing node in the shape of a triangle, with multiple incoming edges at the top (shared occurrences) and one outgoing edge at the bottom (to the shared subgraph). An unsharing step would then ‘unzip’ the shared subgraph node by node, with the triangle acting as the zipper foot.

§ 1.16

(mixing unfolding and β-reduction)
.

A simple interpreter for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
proceeds along the lines of
example

1.14
, i.e. by interspersing β-reduction with unfolding steps in a combined rewriting system. Most of the scientific works around unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms are works that study evalutors that include unfolding rules with β-reduction (and possibly α-reduction) in a rewriting system.

§ 1.17

(unfolding as a subject of study)
.

This thesis however focusses entirely on the unfolding portion of the semantics of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
; β-reduction will henceforth play a marginal role at most.
2
2

2

This vaguely suggests possible future research: it could be a promising venture to develop modular semantics for
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
, i.e. ‘β-reduction modulo unfolding’, and express existing evaluators in terms of this semantis in a modular manner.

§ 1.18

(outlook)
.

In the following two sections we will define the term language for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as well as a rewriting system for unfolding terms in
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
. First we give an informal account to introduce the notation that we will be using for examples. Afterwards we provide sound formalisations.

2
λ-terms and
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms – informal

§ 2.1

(overview)
.

This section provides first-order notations for terms in the λ-calculus and the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-calculus. Mind, that these are not formal definitions and are only used to convey an intuition for the issue at hand. Only in
section

5
the definitions are formalised in the CRS (Combinatory Reduction System) framework.

§ 2.2

(set of λ-terms)
.

Let
V
be a set of variable names. The set of λ-terms is ‘coinductively’ defined by the following grammar, where
x∈V
:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\text{(term)}&L&::=&λ{x}.\,{L}&\text{(abstraction)}\\
&&|&{L}\;{L}&\text{(application)}\\
&&|&x&\text{(variable)}\\
\end{array}

§ 2.3

(infinite terms)
.

Mind, that we interpret this grammar coinductively (i.e. as a final coalgebra). Therefore finite as well as infinite terms arise from it. Since unfoldings of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms are typically infinite, in this thesis we will more often than not deal with infinite λ-terms.
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms on the other hand will always be finite.

Example 2.4

(a simple infinite λ-term)
.

See
example

1.11
.

Adding a production for the
\mathsf{letrec}
-construct to the above grammar, we obtain a grammar for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
.

§ 2.5

(set of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms)
.

Let
V
be a set of variable names. The set of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms is inductively defined by the following grammar, where
x,f_{1},\dots,f_{n}∈V
:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\text{(term)}&L&::=&λ{x}.\,{L}&\text{(abstraction)}\\
&&|&{L}\;{L}&\text{(application)}\\
&&|&x&\text{(variable)}\\
&&|&\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}&\text{(letrec)}\\
\text{(binding group)}&B&::=&f_{1}=L,\,\dots,\,f_{n}=L&\text{(bindings)}\\
&&&(f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\penalty\ \text{all distinct})\\
\end{array}

3
Unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms – informal

On this grammar we will now develop a rewriting system to describe unfolding of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms in an informal notation.

§ 3.1

(substitution of function variables)
.

First we need a rule to perform the actual unfolding, i.e. the substitution of a function variable occurrence by the right-hand side of its definition.

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{let}\;{B_{1},\,f=L,\,B_{2}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}&\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}&\mathsf{let}\;{B_{1},\,f=L,\,B_{2}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}\end{array}

This rule is only applicable to a function binding with a function variable as its body.

§ 3.2

(distributing function bindings)
.

In case of a more complex body, we distribute the function binding over its constituents. The following two rules distribute function bindings over applications and abstractions.

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{L_{0}}\;{L_{1}}}&\mathrel{\to}_{@}&{(\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L_{0}})}\;{(\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L_{1}})}\\[4.30554pt]
\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{λ{x}.\,{L}}&\mathrel{\to}_{λ}&λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}}\end{array}

§ 3.3

(merging function bindings)
.

Two nested function bindings can be merged into one:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{1}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}}&\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{letrec}}&\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},B_{1}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}\end{array}

§ 3.4

(name clashes, α-renaming)
.

Note, that the rules above are all in informal notation. The actual definitions (
section

6
) are CRS rewriting rules. Thus, name clashes (as for instance two functions of the same name being defined in
B_{0}
as well as in
B_{1}
in the above rewriting rule) are not a problem that we need to concern ourselves with, as they are dealt with by the CRS formalism. When using first-order notation we will rename variables whenever necessary (or convenient).

§ 3.5

(garbage collection)
.

The above rules would suffice to distribute the function bindings to the corresponding function variable occurrences and unfold them. In order to obtain an unfolded λ-term without any residual function bindings, we include these garbage-collection rules:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{let}\;{f_{1}=L_{1}\dots f_{n}=L_{n}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}&\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}&\mathsf{let}\;{f_{j_{1}}=L_{j_{1}}\dots f_{j_{n^{\prime}}}=L_{j_{n^{\prime}}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}\\
&&\hskip-139.41832pt\text{\small(if $f_{j_{1}},\dots,f_{j_{n^{\prime}}}$ are the function variables reachable from $L$)}\\[4.30554pt]
\mathsf{let}\;{}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}&\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}&L\end{array}

The latter discards empty function bindings, while the former removes all function bindings from a function binding that are not ‘reachable’. We consider a function binding to be ‘reachable’ if the corresponding function variable either occurs in the body of the
\mathsf{let}
-expression or in any other of the function bindings that is ‘reachable’. The side condition, which ensures that only superfluous bindings are removed from the binding group is non-trivial and requires a reachability analysis because there might be mutually recursive unused function bindings.

The above rules define a rewriting system for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms to (possibly infinite) λ-terms.

Example 3.6

(unfolding derivation of
\mathrm{fix}=λ{f}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r}}
)
.

\displaystyle λ{f}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}{}

\displaystyle λ{f}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{f}\;{r}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{@}{}

\displaystyle λ{f}.\,{{(\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f})}\;{(\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r})}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}{}

\displaystyle λ{f}.\,{{(\mathsf{let}\;{}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f})}\;{(\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r})}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}{}

\displaystyle λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{(\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r})}}

and therefore:

\displaystyle λ{f}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\triangledown}{}

\displaystyle λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{(\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r})}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\triangledown}{}

\displaystyle λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({f}\;{(\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r})})}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-6.28612pt\textrightarrow}_{\triangledown}{}

\displaystyle λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({f}\;{({f}\;{\dots})})}}

We say that
\mathrm{fix}

unfolds to

\penalty\ λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({f}\;{({f}\;{\dots})})}}\penalty\
and write

\llbracket\mathrm{fix}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({f}\;{({f}\;{\dots})})}}

§ 3.7

(meaningless bindings)
.

However, not every
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term represents a λ-term. For instance the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
defined as

λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{f}\;{x}}}

has a meaningless function binding
f=f
that does not unfold to a λ-term. The rewriting rules above admit only the cyclic rewriting sequence
L\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}L
. Therefore
L∉{\mathrm{dom}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}}\right)
, which means that the unfolding semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
based on these rules can only be partial. In order to obtain a total unfolding semantics, we include a constant symbol to signified that the term is undefined at the point of its occurrence. As is customary since
[
2
]
we use the ‘black hole’ symbol
\bullet
, which we include in an extended version of the grammars for λ-terms and
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. The unfolding semantics of
L
will then be
λ{x}.\,{{\bullet}\;{x}}
. On the extended grammar we define two additional rules for turning meaningless bindings into black holes:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{let}\;{B_{1},f=g,B_{2}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}&\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{tighten}}&\mathsf{let}\;{{B_{1}}[{f}:={g}],{B_{2}}[{f}:={g}]}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{L}[{f}:={g}]}\\
&&\hskip-113.81102pt\text{\small(if $g$ is defined in $B_{1}$ or $B_{2}$)}\\
\mathsf{let}\;{B_{1},f=f,B_{2}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}&\mathrel{\to}_{\bullet}&\mathsf{let}\;{{B_{1}}[{f}:={\bullet}],{B_{2}}[{f}:={\bullet}]}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{L}[{f}:={\bullet}]}\end{array}

The former rule inlines alias functions, which simplifies meaningless bindings to the form
f=f
, such that they can be turned into a black hole by the latter rule. Thus we obtain an extended unfolding semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}
which (in contrast to
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
) is defined for all
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms.

Example 3.8

(
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term with a meaningless binding)
.

The rules for handling meaningless bindings allow us to reduce
L
from above to a normal form, which means that
L∈{\mathrm{dom}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}}}\right)
, or in particular
\llbracket λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{f}\;{x}}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}=λ{x}.\,{{\bullet}\;{x}}
as is witnessed by the following rewriting sequence:

\displaystyle λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{f}\;{x}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{@}{}

\displaystyle λ{x}.\,{{(\mathsf{let}\;{f=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f})}\;{(\mathsf{let}\;{f=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{x})}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}{}

\displaystyle λ{x}.\,{{(\mathsf{let}\;{f=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f})}\;{(\mathsf{let}\;{}\;\mathsf{in}\;{x})}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}{}

\displaystyle λ{x}.\,{{(\mathsf{let}\;{f=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f})}\;{x}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\bullet}{}

\displaystyle λ{x}.\,{{\mathsf{let}\;{}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\bullet}}\;{x}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}{}

\displaystyle λ{x}.\,{{\bullet}\;{x}}

Example 3.9

(meaninglessness due to mutual recursion)
.

\mathsf{let}\;{f=g,\,g=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}
is meaningless due to mutually recursive functions. With the aid of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{tighten}}
the term can be reduced to a normal form:

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{f=g,\,g=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{tighten}}{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{f=g,\,g=g}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{g=g}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\bullet}{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\bullet}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}{}

\displaystyle\bullet

Example 3.10

(meaninglessness due to nested mutual recursion)
.

Let us consider another
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
defined as
\mathsf{let}\;{f=\mathsf{let}\;{g=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}
, which illustrates that meaninglessness is not always tied to a simple pattern.
L∉{\mathrm{dom}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}}\right)
, but
L∈{\mathrm{dom}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}}}\right)
as is witnessed by the following rewriting sequence:

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{f=\mathsf{let}\;{g=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{f=\mathsf{let}\;{g=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{f=\mathsf{let}\;{}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{f=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\bullet}{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\bullet}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}{}

\displaystyle\bullet

§ 3.11

(
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{{\triangledown\hskip-1.05487pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.2604pt}
)
.

We define rewriting relations
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{{\triangledown\hskip-1.05487pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.2604pt}
for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms, where
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{tighten}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\bullet}
are included only in
\mathrel{\to}_{{\triangledown\hskip-1.05487pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.2604pt}
:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\bigcup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathrel{\to}_{\rho}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {\rho∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{@,λ,\mathrm{letrec},\mathrm{red},\mathrm{nil}}}}\right\}}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{{\triangledown\hskip-1.05487pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.2604pt}

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\bigcup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathrel{\to}_{\rho}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {\rho∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{@,λ,\mathrm{letrec},\mathrm{red},\mathrm{nil},\mathrm{tighten},\bullet}}}\right\}}}}\right\}

§ 3.12

(necessity of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}
)
.

The purpose of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}
together with
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}
is to prevent unbounded growth of binding groups during unfolding. Consider for instance the outermost rewrite sequence on the term
{\mathsf{let}\;{f=\mathsf{let}\;{g={{f}\;{g}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}}
shown in
fig.

1
, where after the fourth rewriting step
g^{\prime}
(the left one) becomes unreachable and could be removed by a
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}
-step.

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{f=\mathsf{let}\;{g={{f}\;{g}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{f=\mathsf{let}\;{g={{f}\;{g}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\mathsf{let}\;{g^{\prime}={{f}\;{g^{\prime}}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{letrec}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{\begin{array}[]{l}f=\mathsf{let}\;{g={{f}\;{g}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}\\
g^{\prime}={{f}\;{g^{\prime}}}\end{array}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{\begin{array}[]{l}f=\mathsf{let}\;{g={{f}\;{g}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}\\
g^{\prime}={{f}\;{g^{\prime}}}\end{array}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{{f}\;{g^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{@}

\displaystyle{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{let}\;{\begin{array}[]{l}f=\mathsf{let}\;{g={{f}\;{g}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}\\
g^{\prime}={{f}\;{g^{\prime}}}\end{array}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}}}\right)}\;{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{let}\;{\begin{array}[]{l}f=\mathsf{let}\;{g={{f}\;{g}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}\\
g^{\prime}={{f}\;{g^{\prime}}}\end{array}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g^{\prime}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{let}\;{\begin{array}[]{l}f=\mathsf{let}\;{g={{f}\;{g}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}\\
g^{\prime}={{f}\;{g^{\prime}}}\end{array}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\mathsf{let}\;{g^{\prime\prime}={{f}\;{g^{\prime\prime}}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g^{\prime\prime}}}}}\right)\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{let}\;{\begin{array}[]{l}f=\mathsf{let}\;{g={{f}\;{g}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}\\
g^{\prime}={{f}\;{g^{\prime}}}\end{array}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g^{\prime}}}}\right)\end{array}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{letrec}}

\displaystyle{\Biggl(\mathsf{let}\;{\begin{array}[]{l}f=\mathsf{let}\;{g={{f}\;{g}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}\\
g^{\prime}={{f}\;{g^{\prime}}}\\
g^{\prime\prime}={{f}\;{g^{\prime\prime}}}\end{array}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g^{\prime\prime}}\Biggr)}\;{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{let}\;{\begin{array}[]{l}f=\mathsf{let}\;{g={{f}\;{g}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g}\\
g^{\prime}={{f}\;{g^{\prime}}}\end{array}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{g^{\prime}}}}\right)}

Figure 1:
Unbounded growth of binding groups indicated by the initial segment of an infinite
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
-rewrite-sequence without
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}
-steps.

While restricting the size of binding groups during unfolding is a sensible constraint on the unfolding process, it is not strictly necessary to define the unfolding of a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term. Alternatively, we could employ a rule as follows:

\mathsf{let}\;{f_{1}=L_{1}\dots f_{n}=L_{n}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}\mathrel{\to}_{\text{free}}L\hskip 12.91663pt\text{\small(if $f_{1},\dots,f_{n}$ do not occur in $L$)}

Note that a
\mathrel{\to}_{\text{free}}
-step can be simulated by a
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}
-step followed by a
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}
-step.
We will, however, at a later point embed the unfolding rules into other rewriting systems of which we wish to perform unfolding in a lazy way such that the number of derivable subterms is bounded. Using the
\mathrel{\to}_{\text{free}}
-rule instead of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}
the size of the bindings groups in
fig.

1
keeps growing and we obtain an infinite number of different subterms.

§ 3.13

(informal notation)
.

We will be using the informal notation as above throughout most of the thesis. For reasoning however, we lean on the theory of higher-order rewriting. In this way we can avoid the ado of an explicit substitution calculus, which would be required for sound reasoning in a first-order formulation.

4
Preliminaries

Notation 4.1

(ℕ, natural numbers)
.

By ℕ we denote the natural numbers including zero. We let
ℕ=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1,\dots}}}\right\}
.

Notation 4.2

(functions, domain, image)
.

For a total function
f:A\textrightarrow B
we denote by
{\mathrm{dom}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f}}\right)
the
domain

A
, and by
{\mathrm{im}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f}}\right)
the
image

B
of
f
.

For a partial function
f:A⇀B
, and
a∈A
we denote by
{f(a)}{\downarrow}
that
f
is defined for
a
. The
domain
of
f
is the set
{\mathrm{dom}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f}}\right):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{a∈A}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {{f(a)}{\downarrow}}}}\right\}
.

Notation 4.3
.

We denote by
{f}\!\mid_{\,D}
the restriction of function
f
to domain
D
.

Notation 4.4

(rewrite relations)
.

Let
\mathrel{\to}\ \subseteq A\times A
be a rewrite relation. We denote by
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}
the
many-step
rewrite relation induced by
\mathrel{\to}
, by which we mean the reflexive and transitive closure of
\mathrel{\to}
. By
\mathrel{\to}^{+}
we denote the
one-or-more-step
rewrite relation of
\mathrel{\to}
, the transitive closure of
\mathrel{\to}
. By
\mathrel{\to}^{=}
we mean the
zero-or-one-step
rewrite relation of
\mathrel{\to}
, the reflexive closure of
\mathrel{\to}
. By
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-6.28612pt\textrightarrow}
we denote the infinite rewrite relation of finitely of infinitely many
\mathrel{\to}
-steps (see
3.25
for more details).
By a normal form of
\mathrel{\to}
we mean an
a∈A
such that there is no
a^{\prime}∈A
with
a\mathrel{\to}a^{\prime}
. By
\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}
we mean the
reduction to normal form
rewrite relation induced by
\mathrel{\to}
. It is equivalent to the restriction of
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}
to a relation with the normal forms of
\mathrel{\to}
as codomain:
\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}\ =\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{a,a^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {a\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}a^{\prime},\text{$a^{\prime}$ normal form of $\mathrel{\to}$ }}}}\right\}
.

5
λ-terms and
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms – CRS formalisation

§ 5.1

(Combinatory Reduction Systems)
.

Many of the formalisations we introduce are based on the framework of Combinatory Reduction Systems (CRSs)
[
36
]
,
[
37
]

[
51
, Section 11.3]
, and, in particular, on infinitary Combinatory Reduction Systems (iCRSs)
[
31
]
.
CRSs are a higher-order term rewriting framework tailor-made for formalising and manipulating expressions in higher-order languages (i.e. languages with binding constructs like λ-abstractions and function bindings). They provide a sound basis for defining our language and for reasoning with
\mathsf{letrec}
-expressions. By formalising a system of unfolding rules as a CRS we conveniently externalise issues like name capturing and α-renaming, which otherwise would have to be handled by a calculus of explicit substitution. Also, we can lean on the rewriting theory of CRSs for the proofs.

Remark 5.2

(infinitary rewriting)
.

We rely on CRSs (and not for instance Higher-Order Rewriting Systems (HRSs)
[
51
]
) as a rewriting framework since to date infinitary rewriting theory has only been developed for CRSs
[
34
,
35
,
31
]
.

§ 5.3

(the ‘calculi’
\boldsymbol{λ}
,
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}
, and
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
)
.

We will use the symbols
\boldsymbol{λ}
,
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}
, and
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
to refer to the λ-calculus, the λ-calculus with black holes, and the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-calculus with
\mathsf{letrec}
. However, we consider the former two calculi not to have any rewriting rules at all, since we concern ourselves only with unfolding, not with β-reduction.

For formulating the rules from
section

3
as a CRS, we provide CRS signatures for
\boldsymbol{λ}
,
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}
and
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
.

Definition 5.4

(CRS signatures for
\boldsymbol{λ}
,
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}
, and
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
)
.

The CRS signature for
\boldsymbol{λ}
consists of the set
\Sigma^{λ}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathsf{app},\mathsf{abs}}}}\right\}
where
\mathsf{app}
is a binary (
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{app}}}\right)=2
) and
\mathsf{abs}
is a unary (
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{abs}}}\right)=1
) function symbol. The CRS signature for
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}
is
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}=\Sigma^{λ}∪\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\bullet}}}\right\}
where
\bullet
is a nullary (
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bullet}}\right)=0
) function symbol. The CRS signature
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
consists of the countably infinite set
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}=\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}∪\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathsf{let}}}}\right\}∪\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathsf{in}_{n}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {n∈ℕ}}}\right\}
of function symbols, with
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{let}}}\right)=1
and
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{in}_{n}}}\right)=n+1
for all
n∈ℕ
.

Definition 5.5

(set of λ-terms)
.

By
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}}}\right)
we denote the set of closed iCRS terms (terms in an infinitary CRS
[
31
]
) over
\Sigma^{λ}
. Likewise, we denote by
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}\right)
the set of closed iCRS terms over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}
.
Note that the set includes finite as well as infinite terms, thus whenever we speak of λ-terms we refer to λ-terms that are either finite or infinite.

We will look more closely at iCRS-terms in
3.22

Definition 5.6

(set of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms)
.

By
{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)
we denote the set of closed CRS terms over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
, with the restrictions

∘

that there is no occurrence of the
\bullet
-symbol

∘

that
\mathsf{let}
and
\mathsf{in}_{n}
can only occur as patterns of the form

\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{f_{1}\dots f_{n}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\dots}}\right)}}}\right)

∘

and that otherwise a CRS abstraction can only occur directly beneath an
\mathsf{abs}
-symbol.

Example 5.7

(
{\mathrm{fix}}\;{\mathrm{id}}
)
.

The naive version of
\mathrm{fix}
applied to the identity function as in
example

1.11
in CRS notation:

{\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\mathrm{fix}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{abs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({[{f}]\hskip 1.0pt{{\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({f,\,{\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathrm{fix},\,f}}}\right)}}}\right)}}}}\right),\mathrm{fix}}}\right)}}}\right),\,{\mathsf{abs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({[{x}]\hskip 1.0pt{x}}}}\right)}}}\right)

The unfolding of
\mathrm{fix}
in CRS notation:

{\mathsf{abs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({[{f}]\hskip 1.0pt{{\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({f,\,{\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{abs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({[{f}]\hskip 1.0pt{{\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({f,\,{\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\dots,\,f}}}\right)}}}\right)}}}}\right),\,f}}}\right)}}}\right)}}}}\right)

Example 5.8

(
\mathrm{fix}
)
.

The (not so naively implemented)
\mathrm{fix}
-function from
example

3.6
in CRS notation:

{\mathsf{abs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({[{f}]\hskip 1.0pt{(\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{r}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({f,\,r}}}\right),r}}\right)}}}\right))}}}}\right)

6
Unfolding Rules – CRS formalisation

Here we give a CRS formalisation of the rules for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms, corresponding to unfolding as described informally in
section

3
.

Definition 6.1

(CRSs
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
and
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms)
.

\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
and
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms are CRSs over the signature
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
. The rules of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
consist of all the rule schemes below, while in
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
the last two rules schemes are excluded (
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{tighten}}
and
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\bullet}
).
We use vector notation to denote sequences of CRS abstractions (
\vec{f}
instead of
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
) and metaterms (
\vec{B}(\vec{f})
instead of
B_{1}(\vec{f}),\dots,B_{n}(\vec{f})
).

\displaystyle\varrho_{\triangledown}^{λ}:\penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{B}(\vec{f}),{\mathsf{abs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({[{x}]\hskip 1.0pt{M(\vec{f},x)}}}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\textrightarrow{\mathsf{abs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({[{x}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{B}(\vec{f}),M(\vec{f},x)}}\right)}}}\right)}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\varrho_{\triangledown}^{@}:\penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{B}(\vec{f}),{\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({M(\vec{f}),\,N(\vec{f})}}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\textrightarrow\mathsf{app}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{B}(\vec{f}),M(\vec{f})}}\right)}}}\right)},\\
\indent{\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{B}(\vec{f}),N(\vec{f})}}\right)}}}\right)}\end{array}}}\right)

\displaystyle\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{letrec}}:\penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{B}(\vec{f}),\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{g}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{C}(\vec{f},\vec{g}),M(\vec{f},\vec{g})}}\right)}}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\textrightarrow\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}\vec{g}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n+m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{B}(\vec{f}),\vec{C}(\vec{f},\vec{g}),M(\vec{f},\vec{g})}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{rec}}:\penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{B}(\vec{f}),f_{i}}}\right)}}}\right)\mathrel{\to}\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{B}(\vec{f}),{B}_{i}(\vec{f})}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{nil}}:\penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{in}_{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)}}\right)\mathrel{\to}M

\displaystyle\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{red}}:\penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\comma_{i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n}}}\right\}}{\hskip-10.76385ptB_{i}(\vec{f}_{i})}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ M(\vec{f^{\prime}})}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\textrightarrow\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}^{\prime}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{|I|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\comma_{i∈I}{B_{i}(\vec{f}^{\prime})}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ M(\vec{f^{\prime}})}}\right)}}}\right)

for some
I\subset\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt{\text{with }\vec{f}^{\prime}=\comma_{i∈I}{f_{i}}\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ and }\vec{f}_{i}=\begin{cases}\vec{f}^{\prime}&i∈I\\
\vec{f}&i∉I\end{cases}}

\displaystyle\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{tighten}}:\penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{B}(\vec{f}),M(\vec{f})}}\right)}}}\right)\text{ where }B_{i}(\vec{f})=f_{j}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\textrightarrow\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{g}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{l}B_{1}(\vec{g}^{\prime}),\dots,{B_{i-1}}(\vec{g}^{\prime}),\\
\indent B_{i+1}(\vec{g}^{\prime}),\dots,{B_{n}}(\vec{g}^{\prime}),M(\vec{g}^{\prime})\end{array}}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt{\begin{array}[]{l}\text{for some }i,j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n}}}\right\}\text{ with }i≠j\\
\text{and }\vec{g}^{\prime}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{g_{1},\dots,g_{i-1},g_{k},g_{i+1},\dots,g_{n-1}}}}\right\rangle\\
\text{where $k=j$ if $j<i$ and $k=j-1$ if $j>i$ }\end{array}}

\displaystyle\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\bullet}:\penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{B}(\vec{f}),M(\vec{f})}}\right)}}}\right)\text{ where }B_{i}(\vec{f})=f_{i}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\textrightarrow\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{g}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{l}B_{1}(\vec{g}^{\prime}),\dots,{B_{i-1}}(\vec{g}^{\prime}),\\
\indent B_{i+1}(\vec{g}^{\prime}),\dots,{B_{n}}(\vec{g}^{\prime}),M(\vec{g}^{\prime})\end{array}}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt{\begin{array}[]{l}\text{for some }i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n}}}\right\}\\
\text{and }\vec{g}^{\prime}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{g_{1},\dots,g_{i-1},\bullet,g_{i+1},\dots,g_{n-1}}}}\right\rangle\end{array}}

The first four rule schemes require little explanation. The rules are generated from having
n
and
m
range over ℕ. The length of the vectors
\vec{f}
,
\vec{g}
,
\vec{B}
, and
\vec{C}
are stipulated by the subscript of the
\mathsf{let}
-symbol in accordance to
definition

5.6
.
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{nil}}
is actually a rule, not a rule scheme.

The formulation of
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{red}}
uses, as an abbreviation, an ad-hoc list-builder notation, which works just as customary mathematical notation for, say, the union by indexing over an ordered set. Compare:

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}}}\right\}}\hskip-10.76385ptA(i)

\displaystyle=A(i_{1})∪\dots∪A(i_{n})

\displaystyle\text{where }i_{1}<\dots<i_{n}\hfill

\displaystyle\comma_{i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}}}\right\}}{\hskip-10.76385ptA(i)}

\displaystyle=A(i_{1}),\dots,A(i_{n})

Moreover, in the rule scheme
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{red}}
not only does
n
range over ℕ; also the index set
I
ranges over all subsets of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n}}}\right\}
. The purpose of the rule scheme is to remove all bindings that are not
required
. A binding is considered required if it is used directly or indirectly by
M
.
I
is (by formulation of the rule scheme) a superset of all required bindings, or to be more precise: for a given term, only if
I
is chosen as a superset of the required bindings, the rule scheme yields an CRS rule applicable to the term. From the set of ‘valid’ choices we consider the single minimal choice for
I
in order to remove
all
unrequired bindings at once. An implementation of this rule scheme would entail a reachability analysis, which is implicit here.

Example 6.2

(
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{red}}
)
.

To understand the rule scheme
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{red}}
, consider the term
L=\mathsf{let}\;{f_{1}=f_{2},\,f_{2}=f_{1}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f_{1}}
or in CRS notation
L=\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{f_{1}f_{2}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f_{2},f_{1},f_{1}}}\right)}}}\right)
. Considering only the four possibilities we have for
I
,
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{red}}
induces the following four CRS rules:

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}:\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{f_{1}f_{2}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B_{1}(f_{1},f_{2}),B_{2}(f_{1},f_{2}),M()}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{in}_{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M()}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1}}}\right\}:\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{f_{1}f_{2}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B_{1}(f_{1}),B_{2}(f_{1},f_{2}),M(f_{1})}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{f_{1}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B_{1}(f_{1}),M(f_{1})}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{2}}}\right\}:\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{f_{1}f_{2}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B_{1}(f_{1},f_{2}),B_{2}(f_{2}),M(f_{2})}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{f_{2}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B_{2}(f_{2}),M(f_{2})}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}:\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{f_{1}f_{2}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B_{1}(f_{1},f_{2}),B_{2}(f_{1},f_{2}),M(f_{1},f_{2})}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{f_{1},f_{2}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B_{2}(f_{1},f_{2}),M(f_{1},f_{2})}}\right)}}}\right)

The first and the third rule are not applicable to
L
, since
M
has an occurrence of
f_{1}
. The rules induced by
I=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1}}}\right\}
and
I=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{2}}}\right\}
are not applicable to
L
, because
B_{1}
has an occurrence of
f_{2}
and
B_{2}
has an occurrence of
f_{1}
. All the bindings here are used by
M
, therefore the only applicable rule is the last one, which does not alter
L
at all.

Now let us consider the term
L^{\prime}=\mathsf{let}\;{f_{1}=f_{2},\,f_{2}=f_{1}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{x}
or in CRS notation
L^{\prime}=\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{f_{1}f_{2}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f_{2},f_{1},x}}\right)}}}\right)
. As before the rules induced by
I=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1}}}\right\}
and
I=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{2}}}\right\}
are not applicable. However this time not only the last but also the first rule is applicable, indicating that none of the bindings are used by
M
and thus all of them can be removed in one
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}
-step.

Definition 6.3

(garbage free)
.

We call a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term that is a normal form w.r.t. to the rules
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{red}}
and
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{\mathrm{nil}}

garbage free
.

Notation 6.4

(
\varrho^{\rho}
,
\varrho_{s}^{\rho}
)
.

Above we used the notation
\varrho_{s}^{\rho}
to denote a rule named
\rho
that belongs to the rewriting system
s
. We will omit the
s
and write
\varrho^{\rho}
to denote a rule
\rho
if it is unambiguous to which rewriting system
\rho
belongs to.

Notation 6.5

(rewriting relations for
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
and
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
)
.

We write
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
(
\mathrel{\to}_{{\triangledown\hskip-1.05487pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.2604pt}
) for the rewrite relation induced by
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
(
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
). And by

\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
,

\mathrel{\to}_{@}
,

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{letrec}}
,

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}
,

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}
,

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}
,

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{tighten}}
, and

\mathrel{\to}_{\bullet}
,
we denote the rewrite relations of both the CRSs that are induced by the rules

\varrho^{λ}
,

\varrho^{@}
,

\varrho^{\mathrm{letrec}}
,

\varrho^{\mathrm{rec}}
,

\varrho^{\mathrm{nil}}
,

\varrho^{\mathrm{red}}
,

\varrho^{\mathrm{tighten}}
, and

\varrho^{\bullet}
,
respectively.

Remark 6.6

(alternative formalisation with permutations)
.

Note that in the rule patterns in
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
we have to ensure that the function binding we want to refer to may be at an arbitrary position among the function bindings of a
\mathsf{let}
-expression. An alternative approach would be to adding a permutation rule by which two adjacent function bindings can be swapped. Then the remaining rules which refer to a function binding could be written such that they always make use of the first function binding of the
\mathsf{let}
-expression. This approach is for instance used in
[
17
]
.

§ 6.7

(informal notation)
.

As mentioned in
3.13
when studying examples we will mostly rely on the easier-to-read informal notation from
section

3
instead of the more cumbersome CRS notation.

7
Unfolding Semantics of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}

Based on the CRSs
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
and
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
we define the unfolding semantics of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as its infinite unique normal form. The well-definedness of the functions below is witnessed by
theorem

7.2
below.

Definition 7.1

(partial and total unfolding semantics)
.

\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
induces the unfolding semantics

\displaystyle\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}:{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)

\displaystyle⇀{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}}}\right)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle↦M\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ L\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-6.28612pt\textrightarrow}^{!}_{\triangledown}M

which is partial, because of meaningless bindings. (see
3.7
)

\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
induces the unfolding semantics

\displaystyle\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}:{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\textrightarrow{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}\right)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle↦M\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ L\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-6.28612pt\textrightarrow}^{!}_{{\triangledown\hskip-1.05487pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.2604pt}M

which is total, as it maps meaningless terms to
\bullet
.

Theorem 7.2
.

\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
and
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}
are well-defined functions.

Proof.

Well-definedness of these mappings is guaranteed by the following properties of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
and
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
:

Lemma

7.6
(infinite normalisation):

the existence of a normal form (only required for
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
)

Lemma

7.5
(well-formedness of the normal forms):

normal forms do indeed adhere to the signatures
\Sigma^{λ}
, and
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}
, respectively.

Lemma

7.7
(uniqueness of normal forms)

∎

Proposition 7.3

(
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
is a specialisation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}
)
.

∀L∈{\mathrm{dom}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}}\right)\penalty\ \llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}

Proof.

For
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
as well as
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term is a normal form if and only if it is a
\mathsf{let}
-expression. Therefore every normal form of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
is a normal form of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}
. Reachability of the normal forms is guaranteed by the fact that the rules of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
are a superset of the rules of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
.
∎

Terminology 7.4
.

We say that a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L

unfolds to

M
if either
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=M
or
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}=M
. Which is meant, should be clear from the context.

We finish the section with the lemmas required for
theorem

7.2
.

Lemma 7.5

(well-formedness of the normal forms)
.

\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
(
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
) has λ-terms (
λ_{\bullet}
-terms) as normal forms.

Proof sketch.

The names of the first four rules are chosen to reflect the kind of term contained by the body of the
\mathsf{letrec}
-expression, which helps to see that the rules are complete in the sense that every
\mathsf{let}
-expression is a redex, thus normal forms do not contain
\mathsf{let}
-expressions. Terms over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
without
\mathsf{let}
-expressions are
λ_{\bullet}
-terms. The arguments also holds analogously for
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
, which contains no rule to give rise to a
\bullet
-symbol.
∎

Lemma 7.6

(infinite normalisation of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
)
.

Every
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term is either weakly normalising w.r.t.
\mathrel{\to}_{{\triangledown\hskip-1.05487pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.2604pt}
or admits a strongly convergent outermost-fair
\mathrel{\to}_{{\triangledown\hskip-1.05487pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.2604pt}
-rewriting-sequence.

For definitions of ‘outermost fair’ and ‘strongly convergent’ we refer to
[
35
]
.

Proof sketch.

We consider outermost-fair
\mathrel{\to}_{{\triangledown\hskip-1.05487pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.2604pt}
-rewrite-sequences on a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
, in which
\varrho^{\mathrm{red}}
,
\varrho^{\mathrm{nil}}
,
\varrho^{\mathrm{tighten}}
, and
\varrho^{\bullet}
are applied eagerly. We show that every such sequence
\tau
is either finite, or that otherwise its rewrite activity tends to infinity. We argue by contradiction: We assume
\tau
performs infinitely many rewriting steps on position
p
. Then there is an infinite subsequence
\xi
of
\tau
which contracts only redexes at
p
. We show that
\xi
cannot exist.

First note that a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term is a
\mathrel{\to}_{{\triangledown\hskip-1.05487pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.2604pt}
-redex if and only if it is a
\mathsf{let}
-expression. Also an outermost
\mathrel{\to}_{{\triangledown\hskip-1.05487pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.2604pt}
-rewriting-sequence cannot create redexes upwards. Therefore, for
\xi
to be infinite all terms in
\xi
must have a
\mathsf{let}
-expression at
p
.

\xi
can contain neither
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
-steps nor
\mathrel{\to}_{@}
-steps, since they would generate a function symbol at
p
(and thus yield a term that is not a
\mathsf{let}
-expression).

For the rest of the proof we argue using the concept of
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth
, the number of
\mathsf{let}
-symbols occurring under
p
.

\xi
cannot contain an infinite number of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}
-steps because it reduces
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth and no other rule increases
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth.

Thus,
\xi
must have an infinite suffix
\pi
which contains no
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
-,
\mathrel{\to}_{@}
-, and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}
-steps, but only steps due to
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{letrec}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{tighten}}
, and
\mathrel{\to}_{\bullet}
.

For
\pi
to be infinite it must contain infinitely many
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}
-steps since the remaining four rules are only finitely often applicable, because they manipulate or remove bindings (which can happen only once per binding), or in the case of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{letrec}}
decrease the
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth.

We will now go on to show that
\pi
cannot have infinitely many
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}
-steps, if
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{tighten}}
-steps and
\mathrel{\to}_{\bullet}
-steps take precedence. Every binding that is employed by the
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}
-step can only be of the form
f=g
or
f=\mathsf{let}\;{\dots}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\dots}
, otherwise a
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
-step or a
\mathrel{\to}_{@}
-step would ensue, which we already excluded. A term with only these two forms of bindings can be reduced to a term with
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth
1
with only bindings of the form
f=g
by applications of
\varrho^{\mathrm{tighten}}
,
\varrho^{\mathrm{red}}
,
\varrho^{\mathrm{nil}}
,
\varrho^{\mathrm{rec}}
,
\varrho^{\mathrm{letrec}}
. If the bindings are cyclic, the
\pi
terminates with an application of
\varrho^{\bullet}
, otherwise the resulting term is a free variable.
∎

Lemma 7.7

(uniqueness of normal forms)
.

If for a term in
{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)
a normal form exists with respect to
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
or
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
, it is unique.

Proof.

This follows from finitary confluence (
proposition

7.8
)
∎

Proposition 7.8
.

\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
and
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
are confluent.

Proof.

Unique infinite normalisation of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
and
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
follows from finitary confluence of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
and
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
. In previous work
[
18
]
we proved confluence for a CRS which is very similar to
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
. In
chapter

6
an adapted version of that proof is provided, also extended by the rules
\varrho^{\mathrm{tighten}}
and
\varrho^{\bullet}
. The proof is based on decreasing diagrams
[
51
, Section 14.2]
and involves a comprehensive critical-pair analysis.
∎

Remark 7.9
.

Note that this confluence result concerns a rewriting system only for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms, and therefore does not conflict with non-confluence observations concerning versions of cyclic λ-calculi which include unfolding rules as well as β-reduction
[
3
]
.

§ 7.10

(
{\mathrm{dom}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}}\right)
)
.

Finally we are going to characterise the domain of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
, thus identify those
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms that are not meaningless but unfold to some λ-term. Meaningless
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms are unproductive in the sense that during all outermost-fair
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
-rewrite-sequences the production of symbols stagnates due to an unproductive cycle.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
L
be a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term be a
\mathsf{let}
-expression. Then exactly one of the following statements hold:

∘

All maximal outermost-fair
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
-rewriting-sequences on
L
solely contain
\mathsf{let}
-expressions.

∘

All maximal outermost-fair
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
-rewriting-sequences on
L
contain finitely many
\mathsf{let}
-expressions.

Definition 7.12

(
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
-productivity)
.

We say that a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
is
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
-productive
if the following statement holds:

∘

L
does not have a
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
-reduct that is the source of an infinite
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
-rewrite-sequence consisting exclusively of outermost steps with respect to
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{rec}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{nil}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{letrec}}
, or
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{red}}
.

Lemma 7.13
.

For every
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms
L
the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

L\mathrel{\to}^{\omega}_{\triangledown}M
for some infinite λ-term
M
.

(ii)

L
is
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
-productive.

(iii)

Every maximal outermost-fair
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
-rewrite-sequence on
L
is strongly convergent.

Proof.

item

ii
⇒
item

iii
, because if
L
is
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
-productive then every outermost occurrence of a
\mathsf{letrec}
in every
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
-reduct will be eventually pushed down to a higher position by either a
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
-step or a
\mathrel{\to}_{@}
-step of any maximal outermost-fair
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
-sequence. Since only
\mathsf{let}
-expressions are
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
-redexes any maximal outermost-fair rewrite sequence starting from
L
converges to an infinite normal form. (i) follows directly from (iii).
Item

i
⇒
item

ii
follows from
lemma

7.11
by contradiction. If
L
is not
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
-productive then it has by definition a
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
-reduct with at least one occurrence of a
\mathsf{letrec}
which cannot be pushed further down by any outermost application of any
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
-rule. By
lemma

7.11
the same holds for every other maximal outermost-fair rewrite sequence. Therefore
L
cannot unfold to a λ-term
M
because
M
may not contain any
\mathsf{letrec}
s.
∎

Proposition 7.14

(
{\mathrm{dom}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}}\right)
)
.

A
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term is in
{\mathrm{dom}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}}\right)
if and only if it admits no cyclic
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
-rewriting-sequence without
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{@}
-steps and
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{λ}
-steps.

Proof.

“⇒” follows from confluence of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
(
proposition

7.8
). For “⇐” we assume that
L
is not infinitarily normalising in
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
. That means that any fair strategy rewrites
M
eventually to a reduct with root-active subterm
O
. The infinite rewriting sequence that only applies rules to the root of
O
can never contain any
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{@}
-steps or
\varrho_{\triangledown}^{λ}
-steps because the reduct would not be a redex. The cyclicity of this rewriting sequence follows from
proposition

6.22
.
∎

§ 7.15

(a single-rule unfolding system in the likeness of μ-unfolding)
.

In
chapter

7
we present an alternative rewriting system for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. It has only a single rule, like the canonical rewriting system for unfolding μ-terms.

§ 7.16

(outlook)
.

Now that we have established what we mean by unfolding, we can phrase the problems we tackle in the three following chapters of this thesis:

chapter

1
:

Here we characterise the set of λ-terms that can be expressed finitely as
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. To this end we introduce rewriting systems for deconstructing λ-terms. These rewriting systems induce a notion of regularity on a decomposed λ-term: if the set of its components is finite it is regular.

chapter

2
:

From the decompositions systems arise graphical representations for λ-terms in a natural way (essentially the reduction graph of a term w.r.t. a decomposition system). We study these graph representations in order to use them to reason about their respective λ-terms.

chapter

3
:

Here we relate these graph formalisms back to
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
and unfolding, by which we obtain concrete practical methods to analyse and manipulate
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms.

Chapter 1
Expressibility in
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}

1
Overview

§ 1.0

(teaser)
.

Why cannot all infinite
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms with a simple repetitive structure like
λ{a}.\,{λ{b}.\,{{(λ{a}.\,{{(λ{b}.\,{{\dots}\;{a}})}\;{b}})}\;{a}}}
be expressed in
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
?

§ 1.1

(subject matter)
.

In this chapter we study the relationship between finite terms in
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
and the λ-terms they express. We investigate λ-terms that are not unfoldings of any
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term and we consider the question: Which are the infinite lambda terms that are
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressible in the sense that they can be obtained as infinite unfoldings of finite
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms? Or in other words: how expressive is the language
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
?

§ 1.2

(methods and formalisms)
.

We introduce a rewrite system for observing λ-terms through repeated experiments carried out at the head of the term, thereby decomposing it into ‘generated subterms’. There are four sorts of decomposition steps:
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
-steps (decomposing a λ-abstraction),
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}
-steps and
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}
-steps (decomposing an application into its function and argument), and a scope-delimiting step. The scope-delimiting step comes in two variants,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
, defining two rewriting systems with rewrite relations
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
. These rewrite relations each induce a notion of ‘λ-transition-graph’, a sort of graphical representation of λ-terms, which can be compared by means of bisimulation. We call a λ-term ‘regular’ (‘strongly regular’) if its set of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
-reachable (
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
-reachable) generated subterms (and therefore its λ-transition-graph) is finite. Furthermore, we analyse the binding structure of λ-terms with the concept of ‘binding–capturing chains’.

§ 1.3

(result)
.

Utilising these concepts, we answer the above question by providing two characterisations of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressibility. For all λ-terms
M
, the following statements are equivalent:
(i):
M
is
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressible;
(ii):
M
is strongly regular;
(iii):
M
is regular, and it only has finite binding–capturing chains.

2
Introduction

In
chapter

0
we have established how
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms serve as a finite representation of (potentially) infinite λ-terms. It is quite obvious, that not every infinite λ-term can be represented finitely, only those λ-terms come into consideration that have some kind of repetitive, or regular, structure. It turns out, however, that not even λ-terms with a regular syntax tree can always be expressed as the infinite unfolding of a term in
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
(see
example

2.2
below).

Terminology 2.1

(
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressible)
.

We say that a λ-term
M
is
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressible
if it has a representation as a (finite)
1
1

1

Recall that
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms are finite by
definition

5.6
.

L
in
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
:

∃L∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ M=\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}

We then also say that
L

expresses

M
.

Note that λ-terms have infinitely many different representations as
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms (see e.g.
example

Example 2.2

(not

.

Consider the infinite λ-term of the form
M=λ{a}.\,{λ{b}.\,{{(λ{c}.\,{{(λ{d}.\,{{\dots}\;{c}})}\;{b}})}\;{a}}}

§ 2.3

(the relevance of scopes)
.

To understand why there is no
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term that unfolds to
M
, it helps to consider the scopes of the abstractions in
M
. Informally speaking, the scope of an abstraction denotes the minimal connected portion of the term which includes the abstraction itself as well as all occurrences of the bound variable that it binds. A precise definition is given later in
definition

7.9
. The scopes of
M
as shown in
fig.

1
are infinitely entangled: the scope of
λ{a}
reaches into the scope of
λ{b}
, the scope of
λ{b}
into the scope of
λ{c}
, and so on. This trait of
M
suggests
M
cannot be the result of ‘unrolling’ a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
, since such a process would map
L
’s scoping structure onto
M
in a regular manner, and result in a term which is ‘tiled’ into finite, non-overlapping scopes with bounded scope-nesting depth. This excludes, intuitively, the formation of the infinite entanglement of successively overlapping scopes that can be observed in
M
.
