§ 2.6

(regularity of first-order terms)
.

Over a first order signature, the simplest kind of infinite terms are those that are regular in the sense that they possess only a finite number of different subterms. They correspond to trees over ranked alphabets that are regular
[
13
]
. Like regular trees, also regular terms can be expressed finitely by systems of recursion equations
[
13
]
or by ‘rational expressions’
[
13
, Definition 4.5.3]
, which correspond to μ-terms (see e.g.
[
15
]
). Hereby finite expressions denote infinite terms either via a mathematical definition (a fixed-point construction, or induction on paths) or as the limit of a rewrite sequence consisting of unfolding steps.

§ 2.7

(regularity of higher-order terms)
.

For higher-order terms such as λ-terms the concept of regularity is less clear-cut from the outset, due to the presence of variable bindings. Frequently, regularity has been used to denote the existence of a first-order representation with named variables that is regular (e.g. in
[
3
,
1
]
). According to that definition
M
from
example

2.2
would be regular: while the syntax tree on the left in
fig.

1
of the term
M
contains infinitely many variables (and therefore is not regular),
M
has another α-equivalent regular syntax tree (on the right) that uses only two variable names.
However, such a definition of regularity has two drawbacks: it does not (as desired) correspond to
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressibility; and it relies on a property of a first-order representation.
We will attempt to find a stronger, more direct definition of regularity for λ-terms that arises as an adaptation of the first-order notion of regularity.

§ 2.8

(subterms of higher-order terms)
.

The first-order notion of regularity relies on the finiteness of the number of subterms. When adapting this notion to higher-order terms, the problem is that there is no immediate, self-evident notion of subterm. Should for instance
y
be considered a subterm of
λ{x}.\,{x}
which is after all equivalent to
λ{y}.\,{y}
? In order to arrive at a viable notion of higher-order subterms we define a CRS for decomposing higher-order terms. The decomposition ‘remembers’ the abstractions encountered and ‘saves’ them as part of the such obtained components which we call ‘generated subterms’.

§ 2.9

(prefixed subterms)
.

Viable notions of subterms for λ-terms in a higher-order formalisation require a stipulation on how to treat variable binding when descending into the body of a λ-abstraction. For this purpose we enrich the syntax of λ-terms with a parenthesised prefix of abstractions (similar to a proof system for weak μ-equality in
[
15
, Figure 12]
). An expression
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x_{1}\dots x_{n}}}}}\right)\;{M}
represents a partially decomposed λ-term: the body
M
typically contains free occurrences of variables which in the original λ-term were bound by λ-abstractions that have been split off by decomposition steps. The role of such abstractions has then been taken over by abstractions in the prefix
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x_{1}\dots x_{n}}}}}\right)
. In this way expressions with abstraction prefixes can be kept closed under decomposition steps.

§ 2.10

(decomposition CRSs)
.

On these prefixed λ-terms, we will define two closely related rewrite systems
Reg
and
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
. Rewrite sequences in
Reg
and in
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
deconstruct λ-terms by steps that typically decompose applications and λ-abstractions that occur just below the abstraction prefix.
Reg
and
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
differ with respect to the steps for removing vacuous prefix bindings they facilitate: while such bindings can always be removed by pertinent steps in
Reg
, the system
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
only enables steps that remove vacuous bindings at the end of the abstraction prefix.

§ 2.11

(scope-delimiting strategies)
.

For each of these systems we consider a family of strategies that make deterministic choices concerning the application of the steps for removing vacuous prefix bindings: we call these strategies ‘scope-delimiting strategies’ for
Reg
, and ‘scope
+
-delimiting strategies’
2
2

2

We usually say ‘extended scope’ when pronouncing scope
+
, see
4.5
for
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
. Scope-delimiting strategies
\mathbb{S}
for
Reg
and scope
+
-delimiting strategies
\mathbb{S}^{+}
for
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
induce rewrite relations
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}
, respectively. These families of rewrite strategies define respective notions of ‘generated subterm’, and they give rise to differently strong concepts of regularity: a λ-term
M
is called regular (strongly regular) if there is a rewrite strategy
\mathbb{S}
for
Reg
(a rewrite strategy
\mathbb{S}^{+}
for
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
) such that the set of from
M

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}}
-reachable (
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}
-reachable) generated subterms is finite.

syntax tree

binding–capturing chains

scopes

scope
+

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{eag}}}
-generated subterms

Figure 4:
Various depictions of the strongly regular infinite λ-term that is expressed by the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
\mathsf{let}\;{f=λ{xy}.\,{{{f}\;{y}}\;{x}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}
.

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}

M

§ 2.13

(result)
.

The generalisations of the concept of regularity to λ-terms suggest the question: do the expressibility results in
[
13
]
for regular first-order trees with respect to systems of recursion equations, rational expressions, or μ-terms also generalise in an appropriate way? We tackle only the case of strong regularity here, and obtain an expressibility result with respect to the
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
, the λ-calculus with
\mathsf{letrec}
. We show that an infinite unfolding is unique if it exists, and it can be obtained as the limit of an infinite rewrite sequence of unfolding steps. We prove that a λ-term is
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressible if and only if it is strongly regular. This result confirms a conjecture
4
4

4

Cf. the last sentence of
[
8
, Section 1.2.4]
: ‘We conjecture that the set of regular lambda-trees is precisely the set of lambda-trees that can be obtained as the unwinding of terms with
\mathsf{letrec}
’. Mind that ‘regular lambda-trees’ there correspond to strongly regular λ-terms in our sense, and that the notion of ‘sub-tree’ of a ‘lambda-tree’ corresponds to our notion of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}
-generated subterm with respect to a scope
+
-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}^{+}
for
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
.
by Blom in
[
8
, Section 1.2.4]
.

§ 2.14

(chapter overview)
.

section

3
introduces formalisms used in this chapter, predominantly concerning abstract rewriting systems and rewriting strategies.
In
section

4
we introduce rewriting systems for decomposing λ-terms into ‘generated subterms’, and we show some properties of these systems in connection to so-called scope-delimiting strategies. Also in this section, we define regularity and strong regularity for λ-terms employing the concepts of generated subterms and scope-delimiting strategies.
In
section

5
we adapt the rewrite systems for decomposing λ-terms and the notions of scope-delimiting strategies to the λ-calculus with
\mathsf{letrec}
.
In
section

6
, we develop proof systems for the notions of regularity and strong regularity, for equality of strongly regular λ-terms, and for the property of a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term to unfold to a λ-term.
In
section

7
we examine the binding structure of λ-terms (binding–capturing chains) and connect to the concepts introduced so far.
In
section

8
we establish the correspondence between strong regularity and
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressibility for λ-terms.
In
section

9
we introduce ‘λ-transition-graphs’ of λ-terms and of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as
labelled transition graphs in which the edges carry one of the four different labels
@_{0}
,
@_{1}
, λ, and
\mathsf{S}
.

Section

10
summarises the above results.

3
Preliminaries

This section gathers known concepts vital to this chapter. Some notions concerning rewriting are recapitulated from
[
51
]
, for others references are given. Some definitions of known concepts are simplified or tailored to our purposes.

Notation 3.1

(
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{w}}}\right|
, length of a word
w
)
.

For words
w
over some alphabet we denote the length of
w
by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{w}}}\right|
.

We will use the following specific version of Kőnig’s Lemma.

§ 3.2

(Kőnig’s Lemma)
.

Let
G=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,E}}}\right\rangle
be an undirected graph with set
V
of vertices and set
E
of edges. Suppose that
G
has infinitely many vertices (
V
is infinite), that it is connected (for all vertices
v,w∈V
there exists a path in
G
from
v
to
w
) and that every vertex has finite degree (it is adjacent to only finitely many other vertices in
G
). Then for every vertex
v∈V
,
G
contains an infinitely long simple path from
v
, that is, a path starting at
v
without repetition of vertices.
5
5

5

This formulation corresponds to the following original formulation by Dénes Kőnig
[
38
, page 80]
“
Satz 3: Jeder unendliche zusammenhängende Graph
G
endlichen Grades besitzt einen einseitig unendlichen Weg, wobei der Anfangspunkt
P_{0}
dieses Weges beliebig vorgeschrieben werden kann.
” in connection with the definition
[
38
, page 10 ]
“Eine unendliche Menge von Kanten
P_{i}P_{i+1}
(
i=0,1,\dots

in inf.
), bzw. der durch sie gebildete Graph, heißt ein
einseitig unendlicher Weg
, falls für
i≠j
stets
P_{i}≠P_{j}
ist.”

1
Rewriting Relations

Notation 3.3

(
R⋅S
, composition of relations
R
and
S
)
.

For relations
R\subseteq A\times B
and
S\subseteq B\times C
we denote by
R⋅S
the
composition of
R
with
S
defined by
R⋅S:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{x,z}}}\right\rangle}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {(∃y∈B)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{x,y}}}\right\rangle∈R∧\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{y,z}}}\right\rangle∈S}}}\right\}\subseteq A\times C
.

Notation 3.4

(
R^{*}
, reflexive transitive closure of a relation
R
)
.

For a relation
R\subseteq A\times B
we denote by
R^{*}
the reflexive transitive closure of
R
under composition, for which it holds that
R^{*}:=\bigcup_{i∈ℕ}R^{i}
where
R^{0}:={\mathrm{id}}_{A}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{x,x}}}\right\rangle}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {x∈A}}}\right\}
and, for all
i∈ℕ
,
R^{i+1}:=R⋅R^{i}
.

2
Abstract Rewriting Systems

We use abstract rewriting systems (ARSs) to reason about CRSs (from which we derive the ARSs) and more specifically about the set of terms that a term can be reduced to in an arbitrary number of reductions. ARSs are essentially binary relations on sets. They are in a sense graph-like structures, and more tangible in comparison to CRSs and therefore easier to manipulate.

§ 3.5

(abstract rewriting systems)
.

An
abstract rewriting system (ARS)
is a quadruple
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,\Phi,{\mathrm{src}},{\mathrm{tgt}}}}}\right\rangle
consisting of a set
O
of
objects
, a set
\Phi
of
steps
, and
{\mathrm{src}},{\mathrm{tgt}}:\Phi\textrightarrow O
, the
source
and
target
functions. For objects
o∈O
we denote by
{\Phi}_{\text{out}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({o}}}\right)
and by
{\Phi}_{\text{in}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({o}}}\right)
the set of steps in
\Phi
that depart (are outgoing steps) from
o
, and that arrive (are incoming steps) at
o
, respectively. We say that an ARS is
finite
if
\Phi
is finite.

§ 3.6

(ARS induced by a CRS/iCRS)
.

Let
C
be a CRS/iCRS over signature
\Sigma
with rules
R
and let
{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
/
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
be the set of terms over
\Sigma
. We call the ARS
\mathcal{A}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,\Phi,{\mathrm{src}},{\mathrm{tgt}}}}}\right\rangle
the
ARS induced by

C
where:

\displaystyle O={\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ /\penalty\ \penalty\ O={\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)

\displaystyle\Phi=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{s,\rho,t}}}\right\rangle}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {s,t∈O,\rho∈R,s\mathrel{\to}_{\rho}t}}}\right\}

\displaystyle{\mathrm{src}}:\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{s,\rho,t}}}\right\rangle↦s

\displaystyle{\mathrm{tgt}}:\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{s,\rho,t}}}\right\rangle↦t

Notation 3.7

(induced ARS steps)
.

While the ARS formalism makes no qualitative distinction between different kinds of steps (
\Phi
being an unstructured set in the definition of ARS), in
3.6
we retain the original information from the CRS
C
as to which rule a step stems from (
\Phi
being a set of triples). This allows us to do more fine-grained reasoning and we will continue to write
o_{1}\mathrel{\to}_{\rho}o_{2}
to indicate that there is a step
\phi∈\Phi
with
{\mathrm{src}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi}}\right)=o_{1}
and
{\mathrm{tgt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi}}\right)=o_{2}
which is due to the application of a CRS rule
\varrho_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rho}
, or even
o_{1}\mathrel{\to}_{\mathcal{A}.\rho}o_{2}
to indicate which ARS we mean specifically. We sometimes also name the step specifically and write
\phi:o_{1}\mathrel{\to}_{\rho}o_{2}
or
\phi:o_{1}\mathrel{\to}_{\mathcal{A}.\rho}o_{2}
.

§ 3.8

(sub-ARS)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O_{1},\Phi_{1},{\mathrm{src}}_{1},{\mathrm{tgt}}_{1}}}}\right\rangle
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O_{2},\Phi_{2},{\mathrm{src}}_{2},{\mathrm{tgt}}_{2}}}}\right\rangle
be ARSs. We say that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is a
sub-ARS
of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
if
O_{1}\subseteq O_{2}
,
\Phi_{1}\subseteq\Phi_{2}
, and
{\mathrm{src}}_{1}
,
{\mathrm{tgt}}_{1}
are the restrictions of
{\mathrm{src}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{tgt}}_{2}
, respectively, to
\Phi_{1}
, which are required to be total functions. This implies that, for all
\phi∈\Phi_{1}
, it holds that
{\mathrm{src}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi}}\right)={\mathrm{src}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi}}\right)∈O_{1}
, and
{\mathrm{tgt}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi}}\right)={\mathrm{tgt}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi}}\right)∈O_{1}
.

§ 3.9

(generated sub-ARS)
.

For an object
o∈O
of an ARS
\mathcal{A}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,\Phi,{\mathrm{src}},{\mathrm{tgt}}}}}\right\rangle
we denote by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(o\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}}}\right):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},{\mathrm{src}}^{\prime},{\mathrm{tgt}}^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle
the
sub-ARS of
A
generated by

o
, where
O^{\prime}
comprises only the objects from
O
that are reachable from
o
by an arbitrary number of steps (or no steps) and with
\Phi^{\prime}
,
{\mathrm{src}}^{\prime}
,
{\mathrm{tgt}}^{\prime}
being the restrictions of
\Phi
,
{\mathrm{src}}
,
{\mathrm{tgt}}
to the objects in
O^{\prime}
and to steps between objects in
O^{\prime}
. We write
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(o\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathcal{A}}}}\right)
to explicitly refer to a specific ARS.

Terminology 3.10

(reduction graph)
.

We call
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(o\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}}}\right)
the
reduction graph
of
o
, and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(o\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathcal{A}}}}\right)
the reduction graph of
o
with respect to
A
.

3
ARS Strategies

Next we will give definitions for history-free and history-aware strategies for ARSs. The latter is based on the notion of ARS labelling which in turn is based on the notion of ARS bisimulations. Note that ARS bisimulation is solely introduced for the sake of defining ARS labellings and is not to be confused with bisimulation between LTSs or TRSs.

§ 3.11

(bisimulation between ARSs)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O_{i},\Phi_{i},{\mathrm{src}}_{i},{\mathrm{tgt}}_{i}}}}\right\rangle
for
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
be ARSs. A relation
\mathrel{\mathbb{B}}\penalty\ \subseteq(O_{1}\times O_{2})∪(\Phi_{1}\times\Phi_{2})
, which relates objects with objects and steps with steps, is called an
ARS bisimulation
between
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
if the following holds:

∘

if
\mathrel{\mathbb{B}}
relates two objects, then
\mathrel{\mathbb{B}}
also relates their outgoing steps:

\displaystyle∀\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{o_{1},o_{2}}}}\right\rangle∈O_{1}\times O_{2}\penalty\ \penalty\ o_{1}\mathrel{\mathbb{B}}o_{2}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt∀\phi_{1}∈{\Phi_{1}}_{\text{out}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({o_{1}}}}\right)\penalty\ ∃\phi_{2}∈{\Phi_{2}}_{\text{out}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({o_{2}}}}\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ \phi_{1}\mathrel{\mathbb{B}}\phi_{2}\penalty\ \penalty\ ∧

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt∀\phi_{2}∈{\Phi_{2}}_{\text{out}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({o_{2}}}}\right)\penalty\ ∃\phi_{1}∈{\Phi_{1}}_{\text{out}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({o_{1}}}}\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ \phi_{2}\mathrel{\mathbb{B}}\phi_{1}

∘

if
\mathrel{\mathbb{B}}
relates two steps, then
\mathrel{\mathbb{B}}
also relates their sources and targets:

\displaystyle∀\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}}}\right\rangle∈\Phi_{1}\times\Phi_{2}\penalty\ \penalty\ \phi_{1}\mathrel{\mathbb{B}}\phi_{2}\Rightarrow\penalty\

\displaystyle{\mathrm{src}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi_{1}}}\right)\>\mathrel{\mathbb{B}}\>{\mathrm{src}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi_{2}}}\right)\penalty\ ∧

\displaystyle{\mathrm{tgt}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi_{1}}}\right)\>\mathrel{\mathbb{B}}\>{\mathrm{tgt}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi_{2}}}\right)

In this work we need ARS-bisimulation only to define ARS-labellings:

§ 3.12

(labellings of ARSs)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,\Phi,{\mathrm{src}},{\mathrm{tgt}}}}}\right\rangle
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},{\mathrm{src}}^{\prime},{\mathrm{tgt}}^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle
be ARSs.

(i)

An ARS bisimulation
\mathbb{L}
between
A
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is called a
labelling of
A
to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

the
\mathbb{L}
-labelled version of

A
, if the converse
{\mathbb{L}}^{\smile}
of
\mathbb{L}
is a function
{\mathbb{L}}^{\smile}:O^{\prime}∪\Phi^{\prime}\textrightarrow O∪\Phi
, and if additionally, for all
o^{\prime}∈O^{\prime}
and
o∈O
with
o\mathrel{\mathbb{L}}o^{\prime}
, the restriction
{{\mathbb{L}}^{\smile}}\!\mid_{\,{\Phi}_{\text{out}}^{\prime}(o^{\prime})}:{\Phi}_{\text{out}}^{\prime}(o^{\prime})\textrightarrow{\Phi}_{\text{out}}(o)
of
{\mathbb{L}}^{\smile}
to the steps departing from
o^{\prime}
is bijective.

(ii)

A
rewrite labelling

L
of
A
to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a pair
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathbb{L},l}}}\right\rangle
consisting of a labelling
\mathbb{L}
of
A
to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
together with an
initial
labelling function
l
mapping objects of
A
to bisimilar objects of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

§ 3.13

(history-free strategy)
.

A
history-free strategy
for an abstract rewriting system
A
is a sub-ARS of
A
that has the same objects, and the same normal forms as
A
.

§ 3.14

(history-aware strategy)
.

A
history-aware strategy
for an abstract rewriting system
A
is a history-free strategy for the
\mathbb{L}
-labelled version of
A
with respect to, and together with, a rewrite labelling
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathbb{L},l}}}\right\rangle
of
A
.

§ 3.15

(history-aware and history-free strategies)
.

While strategies are simply defined as sub-ARSs of an ARS, here one may think of them as restrictions on the applicability of CRS rules. This is because in this work we consider ARSs that are induced by a CRS. A history-free strategy cannot restrict the applicability of a CRS rule to some term differently depending on the history (i.e. the previously applied rules) of that term. In history-aware strategy, on the other hands, terms are coupled with additional information that can be used to record the history of a term. Thus, how the applicability of CRS rules are restricted can differ depending on that record.

By a
strategy
for
A
we will mean a history-free strategy or a history-aware strategy for
A
.

§ 3.16

(projecting history-aware strategies to history-free strategies)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
be a history-aware strategy for
A
, and let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be that
\mathbb{L}
-labelled version of
A
which
\mathbb{S}
is a history-free strategy for. Then
\mathbb{S}
projects to a history-free strategy
\check{\mathbb{S}}
of
A
. The projection is defined by
\mathbb{L}
, which induces a local bijective correspondence between outgoing steps of related sources of
A
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Mind that for deterministic
\mathbb{S}
,
\check{\mathbb{S}}
may become non-deterministic. Furthermore, every rewrite sequence according to
\mathbb{S}
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
projects to a unique rewrite sequence in
A
(which is a rewrite sequence according to
\check{\mathbb{S}}
).

The last mentioned fact makes it possible to speak, for a given rewrite labelling, of rewrite sequences of a history-aware strategy for the objects of the original ARS.

Let
\mathbb{S}
be a history-aware strategy for an ARS
A
, and
o
an object of
A
. Suppose that
\mathbb{S}
is a sub-ARS of the
\mathbb{L}
-labelled version
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
A
for some rewrite labelling
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathbb{L},l}}}\right\rangle
of
A
. Then by a
rewrite sequence of
\mathbb{S}
on
o
(in
A
) we will mean the projection to
A
of a rewrite sequence of
\mathbb{S}
(in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
) on the result
l(o)
of the initial labelling applied to
o
.

4
Labelled Transition Systems

§ 3.17

(labelled transition systems)
.

A
labelled transition system (LTS)
is a triple
\mathcal{L}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{S,L,T}}}\right\rangle
consisting of a set
S
of
states
, a set
L
of
labels
, and a set
T\subseteq S\times L\times S
of
L
-labelled transitions. We write
s_{1}\mathrel{\to}_{a}s_{2}
to denote labelled transitions, indicating that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{s_{1},a,s_{2}}}}\right\rangle∈T
.

§ 3.18

(bisimulation between LTSs)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{S_{1},L,T_{1}}}}\right\rangle
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{S_{2},L,T_{2}}}}\right\rangle
be a LTSs over a common set of labels. A
bisimulation on
L
is a binary relation
R\subseteq S_{1}\times S_{2}
that satisfies, for all
s_{1}∈S_{1}
and
s_{2}∈S_{2}
:

(i)

if
s_{1}\mathrel{R}s_{2}
and
s_{1}\mathrel{\to}_{a}s_{1}^{\prime}
, then there exists
s_{2}^{\prime}∈S_{2}
such that
s_{2}\mathrel{\to}_{a}s_{2}^{\prime}
and
s_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{R}s_{2}^{\prime}
;

(ii)

if
s_{1}\mathrel{R}s_{2}
and
s_{2}\mathrel{\to}_{a}s_{2}^{\prime}
, then there exists
s_{1}^{\prime}∈S_{1}
such that
s_{1}\mathrel{\to}_{a}s_{1}^{\prime}
and
s_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{R}s_{2}^{\prime}
.

Two states
s_{1}∈S_{1}
and
s_{2}∈S_{2}
are
bisimilar
, denoted by
s_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}s_{2}
, if there exists a bisimulation
R
such that
s_{1}\mathrel{R}s_{2}
.

Remark 3.19

(ARSs versus LTSs)
.

Note that labelled transition systems (LTSs) are essentially the same as (indexed) ARSs
[
51
, 1.1]
. Traditionally, research about LTSs is more concerned with the transitions and their labels while research about ARSs is more concerned with set of objects and their reachability. This is reflected in a different definition of bisimulation for the two systems. Only on LTSs the bisimulation is sensitive to transition labels; ARSs do not have explicit labels on their steps. In this work we adhere to the tradition. We use ARSs for the definition of (strong) regularity which depends on whether the set of reachable terms is finite, and we use LTSs to define a graph representation for λ-terms on which we study properties revolving around (functional) bisimulation.

§ 3.20

(labelled transition graphs)
.

A
labelled transition graph (LTG)

G
is a pointed LTS, that is,
G=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{S,L,i,T}}}\right\rangle
where
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{S,L,T}}}\right\rangle
is an LTS, and
i∈S
, which is called the
initial state
.

§ 3.21

(bisimulation between LTGs)
.

Two LTGs
G_{1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{S_{1},L,i_{1},\mathrel{\to}_{1}}}}\right\rangle
and
G_{1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{S_{2},L,i_{2},\mathrel{\to}_{2}}}}\right\rangle
are
bisimilar
if there is a bisimulation on the underlying LTSs that relates the initial states
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
with one another.

5
iCRS Terms

§ 3.22

(iCRS preterms, iCRS terms)
.

When speaking of ‘infinite terms’ for CRSs over some signature we draw on
[
51
, 12.4]
and
[
31
]
where metaterms of iCRSs are defined by means of metric completion. The metric is defined on α-equivalence classes of finite metaterms dependent on the minimal depth at which two finite preterms belonging to the equivalence classes have a ‘conflict’.
iCRS terms
are defined as the objects formed by the metric completion process. They can be represented as equivalence classes of infinite preterms, which we call
iCRS preterms
, with respect to a notion of α-equivalence that again is based on the notion of ‘conflict’ (see also
[
51
, Definition 12.4.1]
). iCRS preterms are infinite ordered dyadic trees in which each node is either labelled by a variable name, and then the node does not have a successor, or by named abstractions
λ{x}
(with some variable name
x
), and then the node has a single successor node, or by an application symbol, and then the node has a right and a left successor node.

Definition 3.23

(α-equivalence for iCRS preterms, Schroer-style proof system)
.

The notion of α-equivalence on iCRS preterms based on the absence of conflicts can be described by provability in the proof system
\mathbf{A}^{\hskip-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{\infty}}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
in
fig.

5
which is an adaptation of a proof system due to Schroer
[
26
]
. The proof system
\mathbf{A}^{\hskip-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{\infty}}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
over signature
\Sigma
consists of the axioms and rules displayed in
fig.

5
and contains a rule
f
for every
f∈\Sigma
.
Provability in
\mathbf{A}^{\hskip-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{\infty}}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
of an equation between preterms is defined as the existence of a possibly infinite,
completed
derivation: for example, by
\prescript{\infty}{}{\vdash}_{\mathbf{A}^{\hskip-1.47221pt\boldsymbol{\infty}}_{\mathcal{S}}}\hskip 1.5pt{s=t}
we mean the existence of a possibly infinite proof tree
\mathcal{D}^{\infty}
with conclusion
s=t
such that maximal threads from the conclusion upwards either have length
\omega
, or have finite length and end at a leaf that carries an axiom. (We will generally use the decorated turnstyle symbol
\prescript{\infty}{}{\vdash}
to indicate provability by a completed, possibly infinite derivation.)

This formulation of α-equivalence for λ-terms will be the key to our formulation of a ‘coinduction principle’ for λ-terms in
theorem

.

§ 3.25

(infinite rewrite relation,
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-6.28612pt\textrightarrow}
)
.

For an iCRS with rewrite relation
\mathrel{\to}
, we denote by
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-6.28612pt\textrightarrow}
the infinite rewrite relation induced by strongly convergent and continuous rewrite sequences of arbitrary (countable) ordinal length. Hereby strong convergence means that, at every limit ordinal, the depth of the rewrite activity in the rewrite sequence’s terms tends to infinity. Continuity means that the terms of the rewrite sequence converge, in the metric space of infinite terms, at every limit ordinal. By
\mathrel{\to}^{\omega}
we will denote the rewrite relation induced by strongly continuous
\mathrel{\to}
-rewrite-sequences of length
\omega
.

4
Regular and strongly regular λ-terms

§ 4.1

(regularity)
.

For infinite first-order trees the concept of regularity is well-known and well-studied
[
13
]
. Regularity of a labelled tree
6
6

6

By a ‘labelled tree’ we here mean a finite or infinite tree whose nodes are labelled by function symbols with a fixed arity. The arity determines the number of (ordered) successor nodes each node has.
is defined as the existence of only finitely many subtrees and implies the existence of a finite graph that unfolds to that tree. In this section we generalise the notion of regularity to trees with a binding mechanism, i.e. to λ-terms specifically. We give a definition for regularity which corresponds to regularity of a term when regarded as a first-order tree, and for strong regularity, which will be shown in the following sections to coincide with
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressibility.

§ 4.2

(decomposition CRSs
Reg
and
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
)
.

We define regularity and strong regularity in terms of rewriting systems that will be called
Reg
and
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
. Rewrite sequences in these systems
inspect
a given term coinductively in the sense that a rewrite sequence corresponds to a decomposition of the term along one of its paths from the root. Both
Reg
and
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
extend a kernel system
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{-}}
comprising three rewrite rules which denote whether the position just passed in the tree is an abstraction or an application and in the second case whether the application is being followed to the left or to the right.

§ 4.3

(prefixed terms)
.

The rewriting systems are defined on λ-terms enriched by what we call an
abstraction prefix
, by which the terms can be kept closed during the deconstruction. This is crucial for the definition of the rewriting system as a CRS. While intuitively it is clear that the λ-term
{M}\;{N}
is composed of the subterms
M
and
N
, abstractions are more problematic. In a first-order setting one could say that
λ{x}.\,{M}
contains
M
as a subterm, but if
x
occurs freely in
M
then
M
would be an open term. That means that the scrutinisation of an abstraction would be able to go from a closed term to an open term, which would run counter to the interpretation of a higher-order term as an α-equivalence class. In the definition of the rewriting systems below this issue is resolved as follows. When inspecting an abstraction, the binder is eliminated but moved from the scrutinised subterm into the prefix. That guarantees that the term as a whole remains closed.

Example 4.4
.

In
λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}
for instance the path from the root to the second occurrence of
x
then corresponds to the rewrite sequence:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}}\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{{x}\;{x}}\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{x}
.

§ 4.5

(scope and scope
+
)
.

The
Reg
and the
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
system extend
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{-}}
by a
scope-delimiting
rule, which signifies that the scope of an abstraction has ended, with both systems being based on different notions of scope.

Reg
relies on what we simply call
scope
of an abstraction: the range from the abstraction up to the positions under which the bound variable does not occur anymore.
We base
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
on a different notion of scope, called scope
+
(pronounced ‘extended scope’), which is strictly nested. The scopes
+
of an abstraction extends its scope by encompassing all scopes
+
that are opened within its range. As a consequence, scopes
+
do no overlap partially (see
fig.

7
) but are strictly nested. In a sense scope
+
is the transitive closure of scope; this becomes obvious in
definition

7.9
where a precise definition of scope and scope
+
is given. In
[
5
]
scopes
+
are called ‘skeletons’.

Figure 7:
The difference between scope and scope
+

§ 4.6

(scope
+
-delimiters identify abstractions)
.

When every scope
+
is closed by the scope
+
-delimiting rule then the sequence of rewrite steps alone (i.e. without the terms themselves) unambiguously determines which abstraction a variable occurrence belongs to.

Example 4.7
.

The rewrite sequence from above would then have one additional scope
+
-delimiting step asserting that the variable at the end of the path is indeed
x
and not
y
:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}}\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{{x}\;{x}}\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{x}\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{x}

§ 4.8

(abstraction prefix and scope
+
)
.

The abstraction prefix not only keeps the term closed but also denotes which scope
+
is still open, which provides the information to decide applicability of the scope
+
-delimiting rule.

Example 4.9
.

The last step closes the scope
+
of
y
, therefore that variable is removed from the prefix.
The rewrite sequence for the path to the occurrence of
y
does not include a scope
+
-delimiting step:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}}\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{y}

§ 4.10

(nameless representation for λ-terms)
.

Ultimately, the
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
rewriting system defines nameless representations for λ-terms related to the de-Bruijn notation for λ-terms. Considering the de-Bruijn representation of the above term
λ{}.\,{λ{}.\,{{{\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{0})}\;{\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{0})}}\;{\mathsf{0}}}}
we find that the position of the
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-steps indeed coincides with the position of the
\mathsf{S}
markers. However, the rewrite system
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
permits more flexibility for the placement of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-steps.

Example 4.11
.

For example the path from
example

4.7
to the second occurrence of
x
can also be witnessed by the following rewrite sequence:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}}\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{{x}\;{x}}\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{{x}\;{x}}\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{x}
.
Here the scope
+
of
y
is closed earlier. This would correspond to
λ{}.\,{λ{}.\,{{\mathsf{S}({\mathsf{0}}\;{\mathsf{0}})}\;{\mathsf{0}}}}
in de-Bruijn notation, or more precisely, in a variant of the de-Bruijn notation which permits the scope/scope
+
-delimiter
\mathsf{S}
to occur anywhere between a variable occurrence and its binding abstraction.

§ 4.12

(scope delimiters in the literature)
.

This variation of the de-Bruijn notation
[
10
]
, in which
\mathsf{S}
-symbols can be used anywhere in the term signify the end of a scope, is due to Paterson
[
7
]
. The idea is also used in
[
47
]
and related to an even more flexible end-of-scope symbol

λ

[
26
]
.

Definition 4.13

(CRS terms with abstraction prefixes)
.

The CRS signature for
\boldsymbol{(λ)}
, the λ-calculus with abstraction prefixes, extends the CRS signature
\Sigma^{λ}
for
\boldsymbol{λ}
(see
definition

5.4
) and consists of the set
\Sigma^{\smash{(λ)}}=\Sigma^{λ}∪\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathsf{pre}_{n}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {n∈ℕ}}}\right\}
of function symbols, where for
n∈ℕ
the function symbols
\mathsf{pre}_{n}
for
prefix λ-abstractions
of length
n
are unary (have arity one). CRS terms with leading prefixes
\mathsf{pre}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{x_{1}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\dots[{x_{n}}]\hskip 1.0pt{M}}}}\right)
will informally be denoted by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x_{1}\dots x_{n}}}}}\right)\;{M}
, abbreviated as
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{M}
.

§ 4.17

(
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
defines nameless representations)
.

Considering the reduction graphs from
fig.

8
without labels on their nodes we see that only from the
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
graph the original term can be reconstructed unambiguously. For example, the path to the rightmost occurrence of
x
has the rewrite sequence in
Reg

\mathrel{\to}_{λ}.\mathrel{\to}_{λ}.\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}.\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}.\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}

which witnesses an occurrence of
y
in place of
x
at the same position. This ambiguity plays a role for the definition of λ-transition-graphs in

9
, and is discussed in that context in
9.14
.

§ 4.18

(
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}}
)
.

Note that
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
is contained in
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}}
, and since the rule
\varrho^{\mathrm{del}}
generalises the rule
\varrho^{\mathsf{S}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
is contained in
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
.

We will specifically need the following statement later on:

Proposition 4.19
.

Every rewrite sequence in
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
corresponds directly to a rewrite sequence in
Reg
by exchanging
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-steps with
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
-steps.

§ 4.20

(abstract prefix symbol)
.

We are only interested in terms which have a single occurrence of the abstraction prefix symbol at the outermost position. Note that the rules in
Reg
and
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
guarantee that every reduct of a term of the form
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{M}
is again a term of this form. Therefore we define:

Definition 4.21

(prefixed λ-terms)
.

By
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
we denote the subset of closed iCRS terms over
\Sigma^{\smash{(λ)}}
with the restrictions:

∘

Every term
M∈{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
has a prefix at its root and nowhere else:
M
is of the form
\mathsf{pre}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{x_{1}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\dots[{x_{n}}]\hskip 1.0pt{M}}}}\right)
and
M
has no occurrences of function symbols
\mathsf{pre}_{i}
for any
i∈ℕ
.

∘

Otherwise a CRS abstraction can only occur directly beneath an
\mathsf{abs}
-symbol.

{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
is more formally specified in
definition

6.3
.

Proposition 4.22
.

{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
is closed under
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
, and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
.

Definition 4.23

(the ARSs
\textit{Reg}^{-}
,
Reg
,
\textit{Reg}^{+}
)
.

We denote by
\textit{Reg}^{-}
,
Reg
and
\textit{Reg}^{+}
the ARSs induced by the iCRSs derived from
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{-}}
,
Reg
,
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
, restricted to terms in
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
.

Proposition 4.24
.

The restrictions of the rewrite relations of
\textit{Reg}^{-}
,
Reg
and
\textit{Reg}^{+}
to
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
, the set of objects of
\textit{Reg}^{-}
,
Reg
, and
\textit{Reg}^{+}
, have the following properties:

(i)

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
is confluent, and terminating.

(ii)

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
one-step commutes with
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
:

\mathrel{\leftarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}⋅\mathrel{\to}_{\rho}\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \mathrel{\to}_{\rho}⋅\mathrel{\leftarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ ∀\rho∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{λ,@_{0},@_{1},\mathsf{S}}}}\right\}

(iii)

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
can be postponed:

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}⋅\mathrel{\to}_{\rho}\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \mathrel{\to}_{\rho}⋅\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ ∀\rho∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{λ,@_{0},@_{1},\mathsf{S}}}}\right\}

(iv)

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
and thus
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
. Furthermore
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}}\penalty\ \subset\penalty\ \mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}\penalty\ \subset\penalty\ \mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
.

(v)

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
is deterministic, hence confluent, and terminating.

(vi)

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
one-step commutes with
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}
, and
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}
:

\mathrel{\leftarrow}_{\mathsf{S}}⋅\mathrel{\to}_{@_{i}}\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \mathrel{\to}_{@_{i}}⋅\mathrel{\leftarrow}_{\mathsf{S}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ ∀i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}

(vii)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{x}
is the sole term in
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
-normal-form.
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
-normal-forms are of the form
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x_{1}\dots x_{n}}}}}\right)\;{x_{n}}
.

(viii)

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
are finitely branching, and – on finite terms – terminating.

Proof.

Most properties, including those concerning commutation of steps, are easy to verify by analysing the behaviour of the rewrite rules in
Reg
on terms of
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
.

For
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}\subset\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
in
item

iv
note that, for example,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{y}

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{y}}}}\right)\;{y}
by a
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
-step, but that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{y}
is a
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-normal-form, and hence also a
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
-normal-form.

Concerning
item

viii
we first argue for finite branchingness of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
on
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}}}\right)
: this property follows from the fact that, on a term
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{M}
with just one abstraction in its prefix, of the constituent rewrite relations
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
only
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
can have branching degree greater than one, which in this case then also is bounded by the length
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\vec{x}}}}\right|
of the abstraction prefix.
For termination of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
on finite terms with just a leading abstraction prefix we can restrict to
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
, due to
item

iv
, and argue as follows: on finite terms in
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
, in every
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
-rewrite-step either the size of the body of the term decreases strictly, or the size of the body stays the same, but the length of the prefix decreases by one. Hence in every rewrite step the measure
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\text{body size},\text{prefix length}}}}\right\rangle
on terms decreases strictly in the (well-founded) lexicographic ordering on
ℕ\times ℕ
.
∎

Corollary 4.25

(
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
are normalising)
.

Note that as a consequence of
proposition

4.24

item

i
and
item

v
, the rewrite relations
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
are normalising on
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}}}\right)
.

Proposition 4.26
.

The following statements hold:

(i)

Let
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{M}
a term in
Reg
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\vec{x}}}}\right|=n∈ℕ
. Then the number of terms
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{N}
in
Reg
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{N}

\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{M}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\vec{y}}}}\right|=n+k∈ℕ
is
\binom{n+k}{n}
.

(ii)

Let
T
be a finite set of terms in
Reg
, and
k∈ℕ
. Then also the set of terms in
Reg
that are the form
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{N}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\vec{y}}}}\right|≤k
and that have a
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}
-reduct in
T
is finite.

Proof.

From
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{{M}(y)}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{y_{1}\dots y_{n+k}}}}}\right)\;{{N}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n+k})}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x_{1}\dots x_{n}}}}}\right)\;{{M}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{{M}(\vec{x})}

it follows that there are

i_{1},\dots,i_{n}∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n+k}}}\right\}
with
i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{n}

such that the term
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{{M}(y)}
is actually of the form

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{y_{1}\dots y_{n+k}}}}}\right)\;{{N}(y_{i_{1}},\dots,y_{i_{n}})}

and furthermore

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{y_{i_{1}}\dots y_{i_{n}}}}}}\right)\;{{N}(y_{i_{1}},\dots,y_{i_{n}})}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x_{1}\dots x_{n}}}}}\right)\;{{M}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}
.
Hence the number of terms

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{{M}(y)}

with
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}
-reduct

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{{M}(\vec{x})}

is equal to the number of choices
i_{1},\dots,i_{n}∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n+k}}}\right\}
such that
i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{n}
.
This establishes statement
item

i
.
Statement
item

ii
is an easy consequence.
∎

Lemma 4.27
.

On
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
, the rewrite relations
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
have the following further properties with respect to
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
,
\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}_{\mathrm{del}}
, and
\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}_{\mathsf{S}}
:

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}⋅\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}}\;

\displaystyle\subseteq\;{(\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}_{\mathrm{del}}⋅\mathrel{\to}^{=}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}})⋅\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}⋅\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{reg}}}\;

\displaystyle\subseteq\;{(\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}_{\mathrm{del}}⋅\mathrel{\to}^{=}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}})^{*}⋅\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}⋅\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}}\;

\displaystyle\subseteq\;{(\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}_{\mathsf{S}}⋅\mathrel{\to}^{=}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}})⋅\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}⋅\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}}\;

\displaystyle\subseteq\;{(\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}_{\mathsf{S}}⋅\mathrel{\to}^{=}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}})^{*}⋅\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}}

Proof.

These commutation properties, which can be viewed as projection properties, can be shown by arguments with diagrams using the commutation properties in
proposition

4.24
.
∎

Remark 4.28
.

The commutation properties in
lemma

4.27
can be refined to state that
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
-steps project to
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
-steps, and
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}
-steps and
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}
-steps project to
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}
-steps and
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}
-steps, accordingly.

As an immediate consequence of
lemma

4.27
we obtain the following lemma, which formulates a connection via projection between rewrite sequences in
Reg
(in
\textit{Reg}^{+}
) and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
-eager
(
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-eager
) rewrite sequences in
Reg
(in
\textit{Reg}^{+}
) that do not contain
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
-steps,
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}
-steps, or
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}
-steps on terms that allow
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
-steps (
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-steps).

Lemma 4.29
.

The following statements hold:

(i)

Every (finite or infinite) rewrite sequence in
Reg
of the form

\tau:\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{0}}}}}\right)\;{M_{0}}\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{1}}}}}\right)\;{M_{1}}\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}\dots\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{k}}}}}\right)\;{M_{k}}\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}\dots

\pi:\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{0}}}}}\right)\;{M_{0}}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}^{\prime}_{0}}}}}\right)\;{M_{0}}

\displaystyle\dots\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}_{\mathrm{del}}⋅\mathrel{\to}^{=}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}^{\prime}_{k}}}}}\right)\;{M_{k}}\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}_{\mathrm{del}}⋅\mathrel{\to}^{=}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}}\penalty\ \dots

in the sense that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i}}

for all

Every (finite or infinite) rewrite sequence in

\displaystyle\dots\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}_{\mathsf{S}}⋅\mathrel{\to}^{=}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}^{\prime}_{k}}}}}\right)\;{N_{k}}\mathrel{\to}^{\scriptstyle!}_{\mathsf{S}}⋅\mathrel{\to}^{=}_{\mathrm{reg^{-}}}\penalty\ \dots

in the sense that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}_{i}}}}}\right)\;{N_{i}}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}^{\prime}_{i}}}}}\right)\;{N_{i}}
for all
i∈ℕ
less or equal to the length of
\tau
.

§ 4.30

(non-determinism of
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
)
.

On terms in
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
, which have just one prefix at the top of the term, there are two different causes for non-determinism of the rewrite relations in
\mathbf{Reg}
and
\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
: First, since the left-hand sides of the rules
\varrho^{@_{0}}
and
\varrho^{@_{1}}
coincide, these rules enable different steps on the same term, producing the left- and respectively the right subterm of the application immediately below the prefix. Second, the rules
\varrho^{\mathrm{del}}
and
\varrho^{\mathsf{S}}
can be applicable in situations where also one of the rules
\varrho^{@_{0}}
,
\varrho^{@_{1}}
, or
\varrho^{λ}
is applicable (see for instance
fig.

8
, in the middle). Whereas the first kind of non-determinism is due to the ‘observer’ having to observe the two different subterms of an application in a λ-term, the second is due to a freedom of the observer as to when to attest the end of a scope/scope
+
in the analysed λ-term.

§ 4.31

(outlook: λ-transition-graphs)
.

In the definition below we define strategies for
Reg
and
\textit{Reg}^{+}
that only allow the former source of non-determinism while forbidding the second kind. The intention is that the reduction graph of a term with respect to such a strategy corresponds to the term’s syntax tree. These reduction graphs can be seen as a nameless graph representation for λ-terms. We will introduce and study such representations (called λ-transition-graphs) in
section

9
and a further adaptation (λ-term-graphs) in
chapter

2
.

Definition 4.32

(scope/scope
+
-delimiting strategy)
.

We call a strategy
\mathbb{S}
for
Reg
(for
\textit{Reg}^{+}
) a
scope-delimiting strategy
(
scope
+
-delimiting strategy
) if the source of a step is non-deterministic (that is, it is the source of more than one step) if and only if it is the source of precisely a
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}
-step and a
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}
-step.

§ 4.33

(alternative formulation of
definition

4.32
)
.

We can define a bit more verbosely: a strategy
\mathbb{S}
for
Reg
(for
\textit{Reg}^{+}
) is called a scope-delimiting (scope
+
-delimiting) strategy if

∘

every source of a step is one of three kinds: the source of a
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
-step, the source of a
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
-step (a
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-step), or the source of both a
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}
-step and a
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}
-step with the restriction that (in all three cases) it is not the source of any other step.

Heeding the fact that sources of
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
-steps are never sources of
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{i}}
-steps, and vice versa, this condition can be relaxed to:

∘

no source of a
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
-step or a
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{i}}
-step for
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
is also the source of a
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
-step (
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-step), and every source of a
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{i}}
-step for
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
is the source of both a
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}
-step and a
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}
-step, but not of any other step.

Definition 4.34

(eager scope/scope
+
-delimiting strategy)
.

The
eager scope-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}
for
Reg
is defined as the restriction of rewrite steps in
Reg
to eager application of the rule
\varrho^{\mathrm{del}}
: applications rules other than
\varrho^{\mathrm{del}}
are only allowed if
\varrho^{\mathrm{del}}
is not applicable. Analogously, the
eager scope
+
-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{eag}}
for
\textit{Reg}^{+}
is defined as the restriction of rewrite steps in
\textit{Reg}^{+}
to eager application of the rule
\varrho^{\mathsf{S}}
.

Definition 4.35

(lazy scope/scope
+
-delimiting strategy)
.

The
lazy scope-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{lazy}}
for
Reg
is defined as the restriction of rewrite steps in
Reg
to lazy application of the rule
\varrho^{\mathrm{del}}
: applications of
\varrho^{\mathrm{del}}
are only allowed when other rules are not applicable. Analogously, the
lazy scope
+
-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{lazy}}
for
\textit{Reg}^{+}
is defined as the restriction of rewrite steps in
\textit{Reg}^{+}
to lazy application of the rule
\varrho^{\mathsf{S}}
.

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}}}}\right)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{lazy}}}}}\right)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{eag}}}}}\right)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{lazy}}}}}\right)

Reduction graphs of
M=λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}
with respect to different

. Again note, that the labels do only show the prefixes associated with each term.

§ 4.36

(history-aware versus history-free scope-delimiting strategies)
.

The history-free strategy obtained by projection from a history-aware scope-delimiting strategy is not in general a scope-delimiting strategy. This is due to the non-determinism which may be introduced by the projection. Consider for example the term
{M}\;{M}
with
M=λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}
and the history-aware strategy constructed by using
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}
on the left component and
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{lazy}}
on the right component of
{M}\;{M}
. See
fig.

10
for the reduction graph. The reduction graph w.r.t. the history-free strategy obtained by projection, however, bears the kind of non-determinism that is not permitted for a scope-delimiting strategy, in the form of a vertex that is the source of both a
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
- and a
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{i}}
-step.

Figure 10:
On the left: reduction graph of
{M}\;{M}
with
M=λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}}
with respect to the history-aware strategy for
Reg
constructed by using
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}
on the left component and
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{lazy}}
on the right component of
{M}\;{M}
. On the right: reduction graph with respect to the history-free strategy obtained by projection. Note: vertex labels only show prefixes.

The following proposition formulates a property of the eager scope-delimiting (scope
+
-delimiting) strategy for
Reg
(in
\textit{Reg}^{+}
) that assigns it a special status: the target of every rewrite sequence with respect to
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}
(with respect to
\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{eag}}
) can be reached, modulo some final
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
-steps (
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-steps), also by a rewrite sequence with respect to an arbitrary scope-delimiting (scope
+
-delimiting) strategy. Furthermore, rewrite sequences with respect to
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}
(with respect to
\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{eag}}
) are able to mimic rewrite sequences with respect to an arbitrary scope-delimiting (scope
+
-delimiting) strategy, up to trailing
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
-steps (
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-steps) applied to the latter.

Definition 4.37
.

Let
\mathrel{\to}_{1}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{2}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{3}
be rewrite relations.
The rewrite relation
\mathrel{\to}_{1}
is called
cofinal for

\mathrel{\to}_{2}
if
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{2}\penalty\ \subseteq{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{1}⋅\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{2}}
.
We say that
\mathrel{\to}_{1}
is
cofinal for
\mathrel{\to}_{2}
with trailing
\mathrel{\to}_{3}
-steps
if
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{2}\penalty\ \subseteq{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{1}⋅\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{3}}
.
Furthermore we say that
\mathrel{\to}_{1}

factors into

\mathrel{\to}_{2}
and
\mathrel{\to}_{3}
if
\mathrel{\to}_{1}\penalty\ \subseteq{\mathrel{\to}_{2}⋅\mathrel{\to}_{3}}
.

Proposition 4.38
.

For all scope-delimiting strategies
\mathbb{S}
on
Reg
and all scope
+
-delimiting strategies
\mathbb{S}^{+}
on
\textit{Reg}^{+}
the following holds:

(i)

\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}}
factors into
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}}
and
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}
.

\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{eag}}}
factors into
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}
and
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathsf{S}}
.

(ii)

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}}
is cofinal for
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}}
with trailing
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
-steps.

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{eag}}}
is cofinal for
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}
with trailing
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-steps.

Definition 4.39

(generated subterms)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
be a scope-delimiting strategy for
Reg
/
\textit{Reg}^{+}
. For every
M∈{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}}}\right)
, the terms in the set
{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
are called the
generated subterms of
M
with respect to
\mathbb{S}
, where
{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
is the set of objects of the generated sub-ARS
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}}}}\right)
, or in other words, the set of
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}}
-reducts of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}
:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}}\penalty\ :\penalty\ {\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}}}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \textrightarrow\penalty\ \powerset({\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right))

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\penalty\ ↦\penalty\ O\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{where $\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,\Phi,{\mathrm{src}},{\mathrm{tgt}}}}}\right\rangle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}}}}\right)$ }

Definition 4.40

(
\mathbb{S}
-regularity)
.

Let
A
be an abstract rewriting system, and
\mathbb{S}
a strategy for
A
. We say that an object
o
in
A
is
\mathbb{S}
-regular in
A
if the set of generated subterms
{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(o}}\right)
of
o
is finite.

Definition 4.41

(regular and strongly regular λ-terms)
.

A λ-term
M
is called
regular
(
strongly regular
) if there exists a scope-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}
for
Reg
(a scope
+
-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}^{+}
for
\textit{Reg}^{+}
) such that
M
is
\mathbb{S}
-regular (is
\mathbb{S}^{+}
-regular).

Corollary 4.42

(regularity and strong regularity)
.

A λ-term
M
is regular (strongly regular) if and only if the set
{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
of generated subterms of
M
with respect to some scope-delimiting strategy (scope
+
-delimiting strategy)
\mathbb{S}
is finite.

Proposition 4.43

(
\text{finiteness}\Rightarrow\text{strong regularity}\Rightarrow\text{regularity}
)
.

(i)

Every strongly regular λ-term is also regular.

(ii)

Finite λ-terms are strongly regular.

Proof.

For statement
item

i
, let
M
be a λ-term, and let
\mathbb{S}^{+}
be a scope
+
-delimiting strategy for
\textit{Reg}^{+}
by which
{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
is finite. Due to
proposition

4.19

\mathbb{S}^{+}
can be modified to yield a scope-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}
for
Reg
by replacing
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
-steps with
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}
-steps. Then there is a stepwise correspondence between
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}
-rewrite-sequences and
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}}
-rewrite-sequences that pass through the same terms. Consequently, the sets of
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}
-reducts and
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}}
-reducts of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}
coincide:
{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)={\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
. It follows that
{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
is finite.

For statement
item

ii
, note that by
proposition

4.24

item

viii
, and Kőnig’s Lemma every finite term in
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
has only finitely many reducts with respect to
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
. It follows that for every finite λ-term
M
and every scope-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}
on
Reg
, or on
\textit{Reg}^{+}
, the number of
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}}
-reducts of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}
is finite.
∎

Proposition 4.44

(eager scope-closure and regularity)
.

For all λ-terms
M
the following statements hold:

(i)

M
is regular if and only if
M
is
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}
-regular.

(ii)

M
is strongly regular if and only if
M
is
\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{eag}}
-regular.

Proof.

We only prove
item

i
, because
item

ii
can be established analogously.
The implication “⇐” follows from the definition of regularity.
For “⇒”, let
M
be a regular λ-term. Then there exists a scope-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}
so that
{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
is finite. Since by
proposition

4.38

item

i
every
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}}
-rewrite-sequence factors into an
(\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathbb{S}}⋅\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{del}})
-rewrite-sequence, it follows that every term in
{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
is the
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}
-reduct of a term in
{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
. As every term in
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
has only finitely many
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{del}}
-reducts, it follows that also
{\mathrm{ST}}_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
is finite.
∎

Example 4.45

(regular and strongly regular terms)
.

The following examples demonstrate the connection between (strong) regularity and, as illustrated in
fig.

11
, the finiteness of the ARSs generated by different
Reg
/
\textit{Reg}^{+}
strategies.

∘

example

2.2
is regular but not strongly regular.

∘

example

2.5
is strongly regular.

To further illustrate the statements made in
example

4.45
let us consider various
Reg
and
\textit{Reg}^{+}
rewrite sequences corresponding to infinite paths through the terms.

Example 4.46
.

For the term
M
from
example

2.2
, we first introduce a finite CRS based notation, as a ‘higher-order recursive program scheme’. We can represent
M
by
λ{a}.\,{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(a)}
together with the CRS rule
\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(X)\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}}λ{x}.\,{{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(x)}\;{X}}
. It holds that
λ{a}.\,{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(a)}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-6.28612pt\textrightarrow}_{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}}M
. Using this notation we can finitely describe the infinite path down the spine of
example

2.2
by the cyclic
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}
-rewrite-sequence:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{λ{a}.\,{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(a)}}\\
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}&\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{a}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(a)}\\
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}}&\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{a}}}}\right)\;{λ{b}.\,{{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(b)}\;{a}}}\\
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}&\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{ab}}}}\right)\;{{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(b)}\;{a}}\\
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}&\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{ab}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(b)}\\
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{del}}&\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{b}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(b)}\\
=&\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{a}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(a)}\end{array}

In
\textit{Reg}^{+}
the rewriting sequence for the same path is invariant over all
scope
+
-delimiting strategies and necessarily infinite:

\hskip-5.0pt\begin{array}[]{llll}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{λ{a}.\,{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(a)}}&\hskip-6.00006pt\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{a}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(a)}&\hskip-6.00006pt\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}}\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{a}}}}\right)\;{λ{b}.\,{{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(b)}\;{a}}}&\hskip-6.00006pt\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{ab}}}}\right)\;{{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(b)}\;{a}}&\hskip-6.00006pt\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{ab}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(b)}&\hskip-6.00006pt\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}}\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{ab}}}}\right)\;{λ{c}.\,{{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(c)}\;{b}}}&\hskip-6.00006pt\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{abc}}}}\right)\;{{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(c)}\;{b}}&\hskip-6.00006pt\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{abc}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(c)}&\hskip-6.00006pt\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}}\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{abc}}}}\right)\;{λ{d}.\,{{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(d)}\;{c}}}&\hskip-6.00006pt\mathrel{\to}_{λ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{abcd}}}}\right)\;{{\mathsf{rec_{\mathit{M}}}(d)}\;{c}}&\hskip-6.00006pt\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}\dots\end{array}

.

M

\mathsf{let}\;{f=λ{xy}.\,{{{f}\;{y}}\;{x}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}

. It is strongly regular since the infinite path through the term can be witnessed by this cyclic

§ 4.48

(eager scope-closure is necessary)
.

The restriction of
proposition

4.44
to the eager scope-delimiting strategy cannot be relaxed to arbitrary scope-delimiting strategies. The term in
example

4.47
for instance is
\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{eag}}
-regular but not
\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\mathrm{lazy}}
-regular (see
fig.

12
).

Definition 4.49

(grounded cycles in
Reg
,
\textit{Reg}^{+}
)
.

Let

\tau:\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{0}}}}}\right)\;{M_{0}}\mathrel{\to}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{1}}}}}\right)\;{M_{1}}\mathrel{\to}\dots

be a finite or infinite rewrite sequence with respect to
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg}}
or
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{reg^{+}}}
.
By a
grounded cycle
in
\tau
we mean
a cycle

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i}}\mathrel{\to}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i+1}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i+1}}\mathrel{\to}\dots\mathrel{\to}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i+k}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i+k}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i}}
,
in
\tau
, where
i∈ℕ
and
k≥1
, with the additional property that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\vec{x}_{i+j}}}}\right|≥\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\vec{x}_{i}}}}\right|
for all
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,\dots,k}}}\right\}
(i.e. the lengths of the abstraction prefixes in the terms of the cycle is greater or equal to the length of the abstraction prefix at the first and final term of the cycle).

Proposition 4.50

(infinite
\mathbb{S}
-rewrite-sequences contain grounded cycles)
.

Let
M
be a λ-term that is
\mathbb{S}
-regular for some scope/scope
+
-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}
. Then every infinite rewrite sequence with respect to
\mathbb{S}
contains a grounded cycle.

Proof.

Since the argument is analogous in both cases, we only treat strongly regular terms. Let
M
be a λ-term that is
\mathbb{S}^{+}
-regular for some scope
+
-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}^{+}
, and let

\tau:M=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{0}}}}}\right)\;{M_{0}}\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{1}}}}}\right)\;{M_{1}}\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}\dots

be an infinite rewrite sequence.
As
M
is
\mathbb{S}^{+}
-regular, the sequence
\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i}}}\}_{i∈ℕ}
of terms on
\tau
contains only finitely many different terms.
Let
l:=\liminf\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\vec{x}_{i}}}}\right|}\}_{i∈ℕ}
, that is, the minimum of abstraction prefix lengths that appears infinitely often on
\tau
. Let
\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i_{j}}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i_{j}}}}\}_{j∈ℕ}
be the subsequence of
\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i}}}\}_{i∈ℕ}
consisting of terms with prefix length
l
, and such that, for all
k≥i_{0}
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\vec{x}_{k}}}}\right|≥l
.
Since also this subsequence contains only finitely many terms, there exist
j_{1},j_{2}∈ℕ
,
j_{1}<j_{2}
such that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i_{j_{1}}}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i_{j_{1}}}}
=
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i_{j_{2}}}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i_{j_{2}}}}
. By the choice of the subsequence it follows that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i_{j_{1}}}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i_{j_{1}}}}\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}\dots\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}_{i_{j_{2}}}}}}}\right)\;{M_{i_{j_{2}}}}

is a grounded cycle in
\tau
.
∎

§ 4.51
.

We round off this section by providing a motivation for the system
\textit{Reg}^{+}
in terms of an operation ‘parse’ that when applied to a λ-term (i) decomposes it into its generated subterms, and (ii) recombines the generated subterms encountered in the decomposition analysis with, in the limit, the original term as the result. With this purpose in mind, we define a CRS
\textit{{Parse}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
.

Definition 4.52

(
\textit{{Parse}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
)
.

Let
\Sigma^{\smash{(λ)}}_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}=\Sigma^{\smash{(λ)}}∪\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{n}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {n∈ℕ}}}\right\}
be the extension of the CRS signature for
\boldsymbol{(λ)}
, where for
n∈ℕ
, the symbols
\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{n}
have arity
n
. By
\textit{{Parse}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
we denote the CRS over
\Sigma^{\smash{(λ)}}_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}
with the following rules:

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}^{@}\hskip-3.01385pt:\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{X}_{n},\mathsf{pre}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{x}_{n}}]\hskip 1.0pt{{\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({Z_{0}(\vec{x}_{n}),\,Z_{1}(\vec{x}_{n})}}}\right)}}}\right)}}\right)\mathrel{\to}

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219pt{\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{X}_{n},\mathsf{pre}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{x}_{n}}]\hskip 1.0pt{Z_{0}(\vec{x}_{n})}}}\right)}}\right),\,\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{X}_{n},\mathsf{pre}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{x}_{n}}]\hskip 1.0pt{Z_{1}(\vec{x}_{n})}}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}^{λ}\hskip-3.01385pt:\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{X}_{n},\mathsf{pre}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{x}_{n}}]\hskip 1.0pt{{\mathsf{abs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({[{y}]\hskip 1.0pt{Z(\vec{x}_{n},y)}}}}\right)}}}\right)}}\right)\mathrel{\to}

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219pt{\mathsf{abs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({[{y}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{X}_{n},y,\mathsf{pre}_{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{x}_{n}}]\hskip 1.0pt{[{y}]\hskip 1.0pt{Z(\vec{x}_{n},y)}}}}\right)}}\right)}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}^{\mathsf{S}}\hskip-3.01385pt:\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left()}}\right)X_{1},\dots,X_{n+1}(\mathsf{pre}_{n+1}([{\vec{x}_{n+1}}]\hskip 1.0pt{Z(\vec{x}_{n})})\mathrel{\to}

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219pt\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\dots,X_{n},{\mathsf{pre}_{n}}([{\vec{x}_{n}}]\hskip 1.0pt{Z(\vec{x}_{n})})}}\right)

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}^{\mathsf{0}}\hskip-3.01385pt:\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\dots,X_{n},\mathsf{pre}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{x}_{n}}]\hskip 1.0pt{x_{n}}}}\right)}}\right)\mathrel{\to}X_{n}

We denote by
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}
the rewrite relation induced by this CRS.

§ 4.53

(
\textit{{Parse}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
contains
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
)
.

Observe that the rules
\varrho^{@_{i}}
for
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
,
\varrho^{λ}
, and
\varrho^{\mathsf{S}}
of the CRS
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
are contained within the rules
\varrho_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}^{@}
,
\varrho_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}^{λ}
,
\varrho_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}^{\mathsf{S}}
, respectively, of the CRS
\textit{{Parse}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
, in the sense that applications of the latter rules include applications of the former. This has as a consequence that repeated
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}
-steps on a term
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}
lead to terms that contain generated subterms of
M
as closed subexpressions. Furthermore
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}
-rewrite-sequences on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}
are possible that move redexes simultaneously deeper and deeper, analysing ever larger parts of
M
, and at the same time recreating a larger and larger λ-term parts (stable prefix contexts) of
M
, the original term.

Proposition 4.54
.

For every term
M∈{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}}}\right)
it holds:

(i)

\textit{{Parse}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
analyses
M
into its generated subterms: If
\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}}}\right)

\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}

M^{\prime}
, then all subexpressions starting with
\mathsf{pre}_{n}
(for some
n∈ℕ
) in
M^{\prime}
are generated subterms of
M
. Moreover, for every generated subterm
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{N}
of
M
, there exists a
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}
-reduction
M^{\prime\prime}
of
M
such that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{N}
is contained in
M^{\prime\prime}
.

(ii)

\textit{{Parse}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
reconstructs
M
:
\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}}}\right)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-6.28612pt\textrightarrow}_{\mathrm{parse^{+}}}M
.

Example 4.55
.

M
be the infinite unfolding of
\mathsf{let}\;{f=λ{xy}.\,{{{f}\;{y}}\;{x}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}

\displaystyle λ{x^{\prime}}.\,{λ{y^{\prime}}.\,{{{\mathsf{parse}^{\mathrm{+}}_{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{{{M}\;{y}}\;{x}}}}}}\right)}\;{y^{\prime}}}\;{x^{\prime}}}}
