23
) by replacing the rule
\mathrm{FIX}
with the rule
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
.

Definition 8.7

(the proof system
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
)
.

The proof system
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
arises from
\mathbf{Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
by replacing the rule
\mathrm{FIX}
by the rule
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
in
fig.

24
, an annotated version of the rule
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
from
definition

6.20
and
fig.

21
. The side-condition on bottommost instances of
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
to be guarded on access path cycles is analogous as explained in
definition

6.20
.

Proposition 8.8

(from
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
- to
\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-derivations)
.

Let
D
be a closed derivation in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{L\,:\,M}
. Then a closed derivation
\check{\mathcal{D}}
in
\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{L}
can be obtained by removing the λ-terms in
D
while keeping the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term-annotations.

Proposition 8.9

(from
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
- to
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-derivations)
.

Every derivation
D
in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
can be transformed into a derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
with the same conclusion and with the same open assumption classes.

Proof.

First note that
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
and
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
differ only by the specific version of assumption-discharging rule in the system,
\mathrm{FIX}
in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
and
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
.
For showing the proposition, let
D
be a derivation in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
.

We define a proof tree
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, (intended to be a derivation in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
) by repeatedly replacing topmost occurrences of
\mathrm{FIX}
at the bottom of subderivations of the form as depicted in
fig.

23
, by simulating subderivations of the form:

(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{c}_{u}\,:\,M})^{u}

[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{c}_{u}\,:\,M}]^{u}

\mathcal{D}_{0}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{{L}[{u}:={\mathsf{c}_{u}}]\,:\,M}

\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}},u

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{(\mathsf{let}\;{u=L}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u})\,:\,M}

until all occurrences of instances of
\mathrm{FIX}
have been replaced by instances of
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
. The result is a proof tree with axioms and rules of
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}

+

\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}^{-}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
, with the same conclusion and the same classes of open assumptions as
D
, but in which rule instances carrying the label
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
might actually be instances of
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}^{-}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
, unless actually proven (as will be done below) to be instances of
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
.

Now first note that, due to the form of the introduced instances of
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
, every formula occurrence in
D
is reachable on an access path of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Second, note that relative access paths
\pi^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
starting at the conclusion of an instance
\iota^{\prime}
of
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
up to a marked assumption that is discharged at
\iota^{\prime}
descend from a thread
\pi
in
D
from the conclusion of an application
\iota
of
\mathrm{FIX}
up to a marked assumption that is discharged at
\iota
. Since by
proposition

8.4
the thread
\pi^{\prime}
passes at least one instance of a rule
(λ)
or
(@)
, this is also the case for
\pi
. As a consequence, all cycles on relative access paths are guarded. Thus
D
is guarded. Hence all occurrences of rule names
\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
rightly label occurrences of this rule, and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is a derivation in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
, which moreover is guarded.
∎

.

in

can be transformed into the following closed derivation in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
:

(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{c}_{u}\,:\,M})^{u}\hskip-30.00005pt

(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{c}_{u}\,:\,M})^{u}

\mathsf{S}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{c}_{u}\,:\,M}

\mathsf{0}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{x\,:\,x}

@

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{{\mathsf{c}_{u}}\;{x}\,:\,{M}\;{x}}

\mathsf{S}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{{\mathsf{c}_{u}}\;{x}\,:\,{M}\;{x}}

\mathsf{0}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{y\,:\,y}

@

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{xy}}}}\right)\;{{{\mathsf{c}_{u}}\;{x}}\;{y}\,:\,{{M}\;{x}}\;{y}}

λ

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{x}}}}\right)\;{λ{y}.\,{{{\mathsf{c}_{u}}\;{x}}\;{y}}\,:\,{{M}\;{x}}\;{y}}

λ

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{λ{xy}.\,{{{\mathsf{c}_{u}}\;{x}}\;{y}}\,:\,λ{xy}.\,{{{M}\;{x}}\;{y}}}

\mathrm{\mathrm{FIX}_{\mathsf{letrec}}},u

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{(\mathsf{let}\;{u=λ{xy}.\,{{{u}\;{x}}\;{y}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u})\,:\,M}

Now we concentrate on the remaining matter of proving that the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
obtained by the annotation process from a closed derivation in
\mathbf{Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
to one in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
does indeed unfold to the λ-term it annotates.
For this, we establish a proof-theoretic transformation from derivations in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
to derivations in
\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\boldsymbol{=}}
.

Lemma 8.11

(from
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
- to
\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\boldsymbol{=}}
-derivations)
.

Let
D
be a closed derivation in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{L\,:\,M}
. Then
{\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}{\downarrow}
, and
D
can be transformed into a closed derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\boldsymbol{=}}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}
=
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}
by:

∘

replacing each formula occurrence
o
of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{P\,:\,N}
in
D
by an occurrence of the formula
\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\tilde{P}}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{N}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, where
B
arises as the union of all outermost binding groups in conclusions of instances of
\mathrm{FIX}
at or below
o
, and where
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{P}
=
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{y}}}}}\right)\;{{\tilde{P}}[{\vec{f}}:={\vec{\mathsf{c}}_{\vec{f}}}]}
and
\vec{f}
is comprised of the function variables occurring in
B
and
\vec{\mathsf{c}}_{\vec{f}}
are distinct constants for
\vec{f}
as chosen by
D
; the unfoldings involved here are always defined.

Proof.

Let
D
be a closed derivation in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{L\,:\,M}
.

By
proposition

8.9
,
D
can be transformed into a closed derivation
\mathcal{D}_{1}
in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
with the same conclusion. Due to
proposition

8.8
, by dropping the infinite terms in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, a derivation
\mathcal{D}_{2}
in
\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{L}
can be obtained. Then it follows from
theorem

6.26
that
{\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}{\downarrow}
, that is, that
L
unfolds to a λ-term.

We have to show that the transformation of
D
into
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
as described in the statement of the lemma is, on the one hand, possible (that is, the unfolding of each prefixed
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term is indeed defined), and on the other hand, that the proof tree
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
obtained by these replacements is indeed a valid derivation in
\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\boldsymbol{=}}
.

We argue for the possibility of these replacements and for their correctness locally, that is by carrying out the replacements from the bottom of
D
upwards, thereby recognising for every replacement step that it is possible, and that it indeed produces a valid inference in
\mathbf{Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
.

As a typical example of the arguments necessary to establish this fact, we consider a derivation
D
in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
with in it an instance of
(λ)
that immediately succeeds an instance of
\mathrm{FIX}
:

[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{c}_{u}\,:\,M_{0}}]^{u}

\mathcal{D}_{0}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{{L_{0}}[{u}:={\mathsf{c}_{u}}]\,:\,M_{0}}

\mathrm{FIX},u

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{u=L_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}\,:\,M_{0}}

λ

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{(λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{u=L_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}})\,:\,λ{y}.\,{M_{0}}}

\mathcal{D}_{00}^{\prime}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{L\,:\,M}

According to the statement of the lemma,
D
is transformed into the following

\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\boldsymbol{=}}
-proof-tree:

\dots(\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},\,u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0},\,B^{\prime}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{M_{0}})^{u}\dots

\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\prime}

\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},\,u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{M_{0}}

\mathrm{FIX},u

\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},\,u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{M_{0}}

λ

\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{λ{y}.\,{M_{0}}}

\mathcal{D}_{00}^{\prime}

\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}

where
B_{0}
arises as the union of all outermost binding groups in conclusions of instances of
\mathrm{FIX}
strictly below the visible instance of
\mathrm{FIX}
, and
B^{\prime}
is the union of all outermost binding groups in conclusions of instances of
\mathrm{FIX}
strictly above the visible instance of
\mathrm{FIX}
and below the indicated marked assumptions (this binding group differs for different marked assumptions of this assumption class), and where
\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}
is the result of replacing in
L_{0}
all occurrences of constants
\mathsf{c}_{f}
by the function variable
f
from which it originates.

Now assuming that the unfolding in the conclusion of the visible instance of
(@)
has been shown to exist, we want to recognise that this instance and the instance of
\mathrm{FIX}
above are valid instances in
\mathbf{Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
. For the instance of
(λ)
we have to show:

\displaystyle{\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow∃λ{\vec{x}y}.\,{N_{0}}\penalty\ \begin{aligned} &{\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},\,u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}{\downarrow}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{N_{0}}∧\\
&\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{λ{y}.\,{N_{0}}}\end{aligned}

This, however, is a straightforward consequence of the following
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
-rewrite-steps:

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown\hskip-0.81949pt.λ}{}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\mathsf{let}\;{u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown\hskip-0.81949pt.\mathrm{letrec}}{}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}}

in view of the fact that, by
lemma

7.7
, unfolding is uniquely normalising (in at most
\omega
steps).
And for the instance of
\mathrm{FIX}
we have to show:

{\underbrace{\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},\,u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}_{=(\star)}}{\downarrow}\Longrightarrow\begin{aligned} &{\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},\,u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}{\downarrow}=(\star)\\
&∧\penalty\ {\llbracket\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},\,u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0},\,B^{\prime}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}{\downarrow}=(\star)\end{aligned}

(actually the statement as in the second line has to be shown for every binding-group
B^{\prime}
that occurs for marked assumptions discharged at the instance of
\mathrm{FIX}
). This implication is a consequence of the
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
-rewrite-steps:

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},\,u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown\hskip-0.81949pt.\mathrm{rec}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},\,u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},\,u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0},\,B^{\prime}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown\hskip-0.81949pt.\mathrm{red}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}y}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B_{0},\,u=\tilde{}L^{\prime}_{0}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{u}}

again in view of the statement of
lemma

7.7
.

The arguments used here are typical, and can be carried out similarly also for showing that axioms (
\mathsf{0}
), and instances of rules
(@)
and
(\mathsf{S})
in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
-derivations give rise to, under the transformation described in the statement of the lemma, valid instances of axioms (
\mathsf{0}
), and instances of
(@)
and
(\mathsf{S})
, respectively, in
\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\boldsymbol{=}}
-derivations.
∎

Theorem 8.12

(
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
and unfolding semantics)
.

If
\vdash_{\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{L\,:\,M}}
holds for a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
and a λ-term
M
, then
L
unfolds to
M
.

Proof.

Suppose that
D
is a closed derivation in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{L\,:\,M}
.
Lemma

8.11
entails that
L
unfolds to a λ-term, and moreover, that
D
can be transformed into a closed derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}_{\boldsymbol{=}}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}}
=
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}
. Then it follows by
theorem

6.19
(applying soundness of
\mathbf{Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
with respect to the property of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms to unfold to a λ-term that
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=M
, and hence that
L\mathrel{\to}^{\omega}_{\triangledown}M
. In this way we have found a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
that expresses
M
.
∎

We now arrive at our main characterisation result.

Theorem 8.13

(
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressibility
\sim
strong regularity)
.

A λ-term is
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressible if and only if it is strongly regular.

Proof.

Let
M
be a λ-term.

The direction “⇒” is the statement of
theorem

6.28
.

For showing the direction “⇐” in the statement of the theorem, we assume that
M
is strongly regular. Then by
theorem

6.15

item

ii
, there exists a closed derivation
D
in
\mathbf{Reg}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}
. Due to
lemma

8.5

item

i
,
D
can be transformed into a derivation
\hat{\mathcal{D}}
in
\mathbf{ann-Reg}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{L\,:\,M}
, for some
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
. Then it follows by
theorem

8.12
that the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
expresses
M
.
∎

As an immediate consequence of
theorem

8.13
and of
corollary

7.22
we obtain the following theorem, a summary of our main results:

Theorem 8.14
.

For all λ-terms
M
the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

M
is
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressible.

(ii)

M
is strongly regular.

(iii)

M
is regular, and it only contains finite binding–capturing chains.

9
λ-transition-graphs

§ 9.1

(Overview)
.

In this section we introduce the concept of λ-transition-graphs.
λ-transition-graphs are a nameless graphical representations of λ-terms that arise naturally from the decomposition systems from this chapter. The λ-transition-graph of
M
is in fact almost identical to
M
’s reduction graph with respect to some scope
+
-delimiting strategy for
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
. The only difference is that the former is a (pointed) LTS and the latter an ARS. As explained earlier in
remark

3.19
these formalisms are essentially same, except for the definition of bisimulation, which for LTSs is sensitive to the transition labels. The main result of this section is a coinduction principle for λ-terms: two λ-terms are equal if and only if they have bisimilar λ-transition-graphs.

§ 9.2

(outlook: λ-term-graphs)
.

The λ-transition-graphs which we study in this section will be further developed in
chapter

2
into ‘λ-term-graphs’, which are first-order term graph and serve as a graphical representation of λ-terms and
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms.

Definition 9.3

(transition system induced by an ARS)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,\Phi,{\mathrm{src}},{\mathrm{tgt}}}}}\right\rangle
be an ARS (or a sub-ARS (or a sub-ARS of a labelled version) of an ARS) that is induced by a CRS with rules
R
(see
3.6
). Note that, every step in
\Phi:O\times R\times O
carries information according to from which rule it stems from. By the
LTS induced by
A
we mean the LTS
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,R,\Phi}}}\right\rangle
in which the steps in
A
according to rule
\rho
are interpreted as transitions with label
\rho
.

Definition 9.4

(transition graph of a λ-term)
.

Let
M
be a λ-term with reduction graph
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathcal{A}}}}\right)
w.r.t. to an ARS
A
and let
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,R,T}}}\right\rangle
be the LTS induced by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathcal{A}}}}\right)
. We call the labelled transition graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,R,M,T}}}\right\rangle
the
transition graph of
M
w.r.t.
A
.

Definition 9.5

(λ-transition-graph)
.

We call a labelled transition graph
G=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{S,L,i,T}}}\right\rangle
a
λ-transition-graph
if:

∘

it is connected

∘

the labels are
L=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{λ,\mathsf{S},@_{0},@_{1}}}}\right\}

∘

there are no infinite paths in
G
consisting solely of
\mathsf{S}
-transitions

∘

every state
s
belongs to one of the following kinds: λ-states,
\mathsf{S}
-states, and @-states, where

–

a λ-state is the source of precisely one λ-transition, and no other transitions:
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{l,t}}}\right\rangle}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {s\mathrel{\to}_{l}t}}}\right\}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{λ,u}}}\right\rangle}}}\right\}\text{ for some $u∈S$ }
.

–

a
\mathsf{S}
-state is the source of precisely one
\mathsf{S}
-transition, and no other transitions:
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{l,t}}}\right\rangle}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {s\mathrel{\to}_{l}t}}}\right\}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathsf{S},u}}}\right\rangle}}}\right\}\text{ for some $u∈S$ }
.

–

a @-state is the source of precisely one
@_{0}
-transition and one
@_{1}
-transition, and no other transitions:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{l,t}}}\right\rangle}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {s\mathrel{\to}_{l}t}}}\right\}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{@_{0},f}}}\right\rangle,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{@_{1},x}}}\right\rangle}}}\right\}\text{ for some $f,x∈S$ }
.

Proposition 9.6

(eager scope-closure yields λ-transition-graphs)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}^{+}
be a scope
+
-delimiting strategy for
\textit{Reg}^{+}
. For every term
M∈{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\right)
the transition graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
is a λ-transition-graph. We call
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)

the λ-transition-graph of
M
with respect to
\mathbb{S}^{+}
.

Proof.

In the transition graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
there cannot be infinitely many successive
\mathsf{S}
-transitions because in the ARS that induces
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
, the rewrite relation
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
is terminating, due to
proposition

4.24

item

v
.
∎

§ 9.7

(λ-transition-graphs of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms)
.

Along the lines of
proposition

9.6
we can also view transition graphs of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as λ-transition-graphs; however, unfolding steps must not be included as transitions (let us call them
silent transitions
). As hinted at in
5.11
, here it is important to restrict scope/scope
+
-delimiting strategies to ones that are deterministic in the application of unfolding rules.

Definition 9.8

(LTS with silent steps)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,R,\Phi}}}\right\rangle
be the LTS induced by ARS
A
and let
R_{0}
be a subset of
R
. Then by
the LTS induced by
A
with silent
R_{0}
-steps
we mean the LTS
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathcal{A}},{R_{0}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,R,T}}}\right\rangle
with

T:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{o,\rho,o^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {\begin{tabular}[]{@{}l@{}}if $o\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{R_{0}}⋅\mathrel{\to}_{\rho}o^{\prime}$ where $\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{R_{0}}$ are steps w.r.t.\ rules\\
in $R_{0}$, and $\mathrel{\to}_{\rho}$ is a step w.r.t.\ a rule $\rho∈R\setminus R_{0}$\end{tabular}}}}\right\}

in which the steps in
A
according to rules in
R_{0}
are interpreted as silent transitions.

Definition 9.9

(LTG with silent steps)
.

Let
o
be an object of the ARS
A
and
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(o\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}}}\right)},{R_{0}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,R,T}}}\right\rangle
an LTS with silent
R_{0}
-steps. We call
\mathcal{G}_{{\mathcal{A}},{R_{0}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({o}}}\right):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{O,R,o,T}}}\right\rangle
the
transition graph of
o
with silent
R_{0}
-steps
.

Proposition 9.10
.

Let
\mathbb{S}^{+}
be a scope
+
-delimiting strategy for
\textit{Reg\/}^{+}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
. For every term
L∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\smash{\boldsymbol{(λ_{\mathsf{letrec}})}}}}\right)
, the transition graph
\mathcal{G}_{{\mathbb{S}^{+}},{\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({L}}}\right)
of
L
is a λ-transition-graph.

Definition 9.11

(λ-transition-graph of a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term)
.

Let
L∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\smash{\boldsymbol{(λ_{\mathsf{letrec}})}}}}\right)
be a (prefixed)
\mathbb{S}^{+}
-productive
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term. For a scope
+
-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}^{+}
of
\textit{Reg\/}^{+}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
such that
L
is
\mathbb{S}^{+}
-productive, we call the transition graph
\mathcal{G}_{{\mathbb{S}^{+}},{\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({L}}}\right)

the λ-transition-graph of
L
with respect to
\mathbb{S}^{+}
. We also speak of λ-transition-graphs of terms
L∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)
or
M∈{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}}}\right)
by which we refer to the λ-transition-graphs of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{L}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}
, respectively.

Theorem 9.12

(coinduction principle for
\boldsymbol{λ}
)
.

For all λ-terms
M
and
N
the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

M=N
.

(ii)

\vdash_{{\mathbf{EQ}}^{\boldsymbol{\infty}}}{M=N}
.

(iii)

M
and
N
have bisimilar λ-transition-graphs.

Proof.

In view of
proposition

6.17

item

ii
, the logical equivalence between
item

i
and
item

ii
, and the fact that
item

i
⇒
item

iii
clearly holds, it suffices to show that
item

iii
⇒
item

ii
holds.

For this, suppose that
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}_{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N}}\right)
are bisimilar for some scope
+
-delimiting strategies
\mathbb{S}^{+}_{1}
and
\mathbb{S}^{+}_{2}
for
Reg
. But now bisimilarity of these transition graphs guarantees that a derivation
D
in
{\mathbf{EQ}}^{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}
=
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{N}
can be constructed such that all threads in
D
correspond to
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}_{1}}
-rewrite-sequences on
M
and to
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathbb{S}^{+}_{2}}
-rewrite-sequences on
M
, respectively. If the construction process is organised in a depth-fair manner (for example, all non-axiom leafs at depth
n
are extended by appropriate rule instances, before extensions are carried out at depth greater than
n
), then in the limit a completed derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\infty}
with conclusion
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{M}
=
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)\;{N}
is obtained. This establishes
\vdash_{{\mathbf{EQ}}^{\boldsymbol{\infty}}}{M=N}
.
∎

Conjecture 9.13

(coinduction principle for
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
)
.

For all
L_{1},L_{2}∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)
it holds that
L_{1}=L_{2}
if and only if
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
have bisimilar λ-transition-graphs.

Proof sketch.

In
chapter

3
we develop a coinduction principle for
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
(
theorem

6.17
) based on λ-term-graphs. λ-term-graphs are introduced in
chapter

2
and are closely related to λ-transition-graphs. The conjecture can in all likelihood be validated by
theorem

6.17
after relating λ-transition-graphs to λ-term-graphs in a formal manner.
∎

§ 9.14

(only
\textit{{Reg}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
defines nameless representations)
.

The coinduction principle above holds for transition graphs derived from terms w.r.t. an eager scope
+
-delimiting strategy for
\textit{Reg}^{+}
. It cannot be extended to
Reg
, which is witnessed by the following counterexample.

Consider the transition graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M}}\right)
of the term
M=λ{xy}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}
w.r.t. the eager scope-delimiting strategy
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{eag}}
for
Reg
. The corresponding
Reg
-reduction graph is depicted on the left in
fig.

9
. Each of the following terms yields the exact same transition graph w.r.t. to an appropriately-chosen scope-delimiting strategy for
Reg
. For the two terms in the middle the eager scope-delimiting strategy can be chosen.

λ{xy}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{x}}\hskip 28.45274ptλ{xy}.\,{{{x}\;{x}}\;{y}}\hskip 28.45274ptλ{xy}.\,{{{y}\;{y}}\;{x}}\hskip 28.45274ptλ{xy}.\,{{{y}\;{y}}\;{y}}

§ 9.15

(readback for λ-transition-graphs)
.

The understanding of λ-transition-graphs as nameless representations of λ-terms implies that from a such graphs the corresponding λ-term can be extracted. We define a function for this purpose by means of a CRS which implements the assembly of a λ-term from the infinite unfolding of a λ-transition-graph. The function is closely related to the
\textit{{Parse}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
in the sense that
\textit{{Parse}}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
does both destruct and reconstruct the scrutinised term while
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}
only implements the reconstruction.

Definition 9.16

(readback for λ-transition-graphs)
.

\displaystyle{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}:{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathsf{0},λ,@,\mathsf{S}}}}\right\}}}\right)

\displaystyle{}⇀{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}}}\right)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle{}↦{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(t}}\right):=\penalty\ \parbox[t]{130.0pt}{infinite normal form of ${\mathrm{{\textsf{rw}}_{0}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(t}}\right)$ w.r.t.\ the following CRS:}

\displaystyle{{\textsf{rw}}_{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\dots,X_{n},λ(t_{0})}}\right)

\displaystyle{}\;\mathrel{\to}\;{\mathsf{abs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({[{x}]\hskip 1.0pt{{{\textsf{rw}}_{n+1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\dots,X_{n},x,t_{0}}}\right)}}}}\right)

\displaystyle{{\textsf{rw}}_{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{X},@(t_{0}){t_{1}}}}\right)

\displaystyle{}\;\mathrel{\to}\;{\mathsf{app}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{{\textsf{rw}}_{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{X},t_{0}}}\right),\,{{\textsf{rw}}_{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{X},t_{1}}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle{{\textsf{rw}}_{n+1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{X},x,\mathsf{S}(t_{0})}}\right)

\displaystyle{}\;\mathrel{\to}\;{{\textsf{rw}}_{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{X},t_{0}}}\right)

\displaystyle{{\textsf{rw}}_{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\dots,X_{n},\mathsf{0}}}\right)

\displaystyle{}\;\mathrel{\to}\;X_{n}

The function is partial because
{\textsf{rw}}_{n}
is unproductive for infinite
\mathsf{S}
-chains. That restriction comes forth accordingly in the definition of λ-transition-graphs (
definition

9.5
). The function is thus complete on the subset of
{\mathrm{{Ter}^{\infty}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathsf{0},λ,@,\mathsf{S}}}}\right\}}}\right)
that is obtained from unfolding a λ-transition-graph.

10
Summary

§ 10.1

(A CRS for decomposing λ-terms)
.

To characterise the set of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressible λ-terms we established a framework of formalisms for ‘observing’ λ-terms coinductively. First we introduced prefixed λ-terms that enrich λ-terms by an abstraction prefix. On the prefixed terms we defined the CRS
\textit{Reg}^{+}
in which a rewrite sequence corresponds to a deconstruction of a term along one of its paths. In that sense a prefixed term
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{x}}}}}\right)\;{M}
can be understood as a ‘suspended decomposition’ which has not advanced into subterm
M
yet. Such a decomposition describes a path through the term by observations of the form
\mathrel{\to}_{λ}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{0}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{@_{1}}
,
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
, where the
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathsf{S}}
delimits the scope
+
of an abstraction.

§ 10.2

(scope/scope
+
-delimiting strategies)
.

Since there is some freedom as to where scope
+
-delimiters can be placed, we defined scope
+
-delimiting strategies to formalise specific possible choices eliminating that freedom and thereby making the observations deterministic except for the forking into the left or the right subterm of an application. By means of scope
+
-delimiting strategies we formulated two important concepts: strong regularity and λ-transition graphs.

§ 10.3

(strong regularity)
.

The intuitive understanding of strong regularity is the property of a λ-term
M
that from
M
every ‘sufficiently eager’ scope
+
-delimiting strategy can only generate a finite number of terms. We showed that
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expressibility coincides with strong regularity.

§ 10.4

(λ-transition-graphs)
.

Every scope
+
-delimiting strategy defines for each term a λ-transition-graph which can be viewed as a nameless graphical representation very similar to its term graph in de-Bruijn notation with the difference that
\mathsf{S}
-nodes are not restricted to occur only near leafs but can be shared by variables (see
4.12
). We established a coinduction principle for λ-transition-graphs that states that two λ-terms are equal if and only if their λ-transition graphs w.r.t. to a scope
+
-delimiting strategy are bisimilar. The eager scope
+
-delimiting strategy yields finite λ-transition-graphs for strongly regular λ-terms.

§ 10.5

(
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
)
.

We then adapted the concepts of the CRS for observing terms, scope
+
-delimiting strategies, and λ-transition-graphs and applied them to
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
proving similar results as for
\boldsymbol{λ}
.

§ 10.6

(proof systems for strong regularity)
.

We provided a proof system that is sound and complete for the notion of strong regularity and which admits finite proofs for strongly regular λ-terms. We define an annotated version of the proof system which not unlike an attribute-grammar definition implements the extraction of a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
from a proof for term
M
in that system, such that
L
unfolds to the
M
. We show that every scope
+
-delimiting strategy induces a proof and that from a proof a corresponding history-aware strategy can be deduced, which suggests a similar correspondence between λ-transition-graphs and proofs.

Chapter 2
Term Graph Representations for Strongly Regular λ-Terms

1
Overview

§ 1.0

(teaser)
.

Can’t we just do bisimulation on
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms? See also
1.0
.

§ 1.1

(subject matter)
.

In this chapter we study various classes of higher-order and first-order term graphs (intended as representations for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms). We focus on the relation between ‘λ-higher-order term graphs’ (λ-ho-term-graphs), which are first-order term graphs endowed with a well-behaved scope function, and their representations as ‘λ-term-graphs’, which are plain first-order term graphs with scope-delimiter vertices that meet certain scoping requirements. Specifically we tackle the question: Which class of first-order term graphs admits a faithful embedding of λ-ho-term-graphs in the sense that (i) the homomorphism-based sharing-order on λ-ho-term-graphs is preserved and reflected, and (ii) the image of the embedding corresponds closely to a natural class (of λ-term-graphs) that is closed under functional bisimulation?

§ 1.2

(motivation)
.

We study these graph formalisms in isolation – that is to say without formally connecting them to
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. But we do so with a long term goal in mind: to find a graph formalism that is suitable to adequately represent
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. Once we have found such a graph formalism, in
chapter

3
we relate it back to
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
, by which we gain further insights concerning unfolding semantics of
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
and also to obtain concrete practical methods to analyse and manipulate
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms.

§ 1.3

(methods and formalisms)
.

The term graph formalims we study arise naturally from the term decomposition systems from the previous chapter. They are closely related to the λ-transition-graphs, and we derive different classes of term graphs from the term decomposition systems by a similar approach. We systematically examine which classes of λ-term-graphs satisfy the properties in
1.1
.

§ 1.4

(results)
.

We identify a particular class of first-order term graphs with these properties. Term graphs of this class are built not only from application, abstraction, and variable vertices, but also scope-delimiter vertices. They have the characteristic feature that the latter two kinds of vertices have backlinks to the corresponding abstraction. This result puts a handle on the concept of subterm sharing for higher-order term graphs, both theoretically and algorithmically: We obtain an easily implementable method for obtaining the maximally shared form of λ-ho-term-graphs. Also, we open up the possibility to transfer properties from first-order term graphs to λ-ho-term-graphs. In fact we prove in this way that the sharing-order on a set of bisimilar λ-ho-term-graphs forms a complete lattice
section

10
.

§ 1.5

(outlook)
.

In
chapter

3
we use these insights to develop practical applications w.r.t
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
:

∘

an efficient test for whether two
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms have the same unfolding

∘

a partial order for the amount of subterm sharing in a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term leading to

–

a notion of maximal sharing for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}

–

an efficient mechanism to compute the maximally shared form of a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term which generalises common subexpression elimination

2
Preliminaries

§ 2.1

(term graphs)
.

The graph formalisms that we study in this chapter all based on term graphs (in contrast to transition graphs as in the last chapter), which is a natural choice, considering the three types of expressions (abstraction, application, variable occurrence) that make up λ-terms. Here is some notation and terminology revolving around term graphs.

Definition 2.2

(term graph)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a signature with arity function
{\mathrm{ar}}:\Sigma\textrightarrow ℕ
. A
term graph over
\Sigma
(or a
\Sigma
-term-graph
) is a tuple
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r}}}\right\rangle
where
V
is a set of
vertices
,
{\mathrm{lab}}:V\textrightarrow\Sigma
the
(vertex) label function
,
{\mathrm{args}}:V\textrightarrow V^{*}
the
argument function
that maps every vertex
v
to the word
{\mathrm{args}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
consisting of the
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathrm{lab}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}}\right)
successor vertices of
v
(hence it holds that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{{\mathrm{args}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}}}\right|={\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathrm{lab}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}}\right)
), and
r
, the
root
, is a vertex in
V
. Note that term graphs may have infinitely many vertices.

Definition 2.3

(root connected term graphs)
.

We say that such a term graph is
root connected
if every vertex is reachable from the root by a path that arises by repeatedly going from a vertex to one of its successors. We denote by
{\mathrm{TG}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
and by
{\mathrm{TG^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
the class of all root-connected term graphs over
\Sigma
, and the class of all term graphs over
\Sigma
, respectively. By ‘term graphs’ we will from now on, always mean root-connected term graphs, except in a few situations in which we explicitly state otherwise.

Definition 2.4

(successor relations)
.

Let
G
be a term graph over signature
\Sigma
. As useful notation for picking out the
i
-th vertex, from among the ordered successors
{\mathrm{args}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
of a vertex
v
of
G
,
we define for each
i∈ℕ
the indexed edge relation
{\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{i}}\subseteq{V\times V}
, and additionally the (not indexed) edge relation
{\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}}\subseteq V\times V
, by stipulating for all
v,v^{\prime}∈V
:

\displaystyle v\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{i}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\penalty\ :⇔\penalty\ ∃v_{0},\dots,v_{n}∈V\penalty\ \penalty\ {\mathrm{args}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=v_{0}\dots v_{n}∧v^{\prime}=v_{i}

\displaystyle v\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\penalty\ :⇔\penalty\ ∃i∈ℕ\penalty\ \penalty\ v\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{i}v^{\prime}

We write
v\mathrel{\prescript{\smash{l}}{}{\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}}}_{i}v^{\prime}
if
v\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{i}v^{\prime}∧{\mathrm{lab}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=l
holds for
v,v^{\prime}∈V
,
i∈ℕ
,
l∈\Sigma
, to indicate the label at the source of an edge.

Definition 2.5

(paths)
.

Let
v_{0},\dots,v_{n}∈V
. A
path
in
G
is a tuple
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{v_{0},l_{1},v_{1},l_{2},v_{2},l_{3},\dots,l_{n-1},v_{n-1},l_{n},v_{n}}}}\right\rangle
and
n,l_{1},\dots,l_{n}∈ℕ
such that
v_{0}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{l_{1}}v_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{l_{2}}v_{2}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{l_{3}}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{l_{n}}v_{n}
holds; paths will usually be denoted in the latter form, using indexed edge relations.

Definition 2.6

(access paths)
.

An
access path
of a vertex
v
of a term graph
G
is a path that starts at the root of
G
, ends in
v
, and does not visit any vertex twice. Note that every vertex
v
has at least one access path: since every vertex in a term graph is reachable from the root (see
definition

2.3
), there is a path
\pi
from
r
to
v
; then an access path of
v
can be obtained from
\pi
by repeatedly cutting out
cycles
, that is, parts of the path between one and the same vertex.

In the following, let
G_{1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V_{1},{\mathrm{lab}}_{1},{\mathrm{args}}_{1},r_{1}}}}\right\rangle
,
G_{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V_{2},{\mathrm{lab}}_{2},{\mathrm{args}}_{2},r_{2}}}}\right\rangle
be term graphs over signature
\Sigma
.

Definition 2.7

(homomorphism, functional bisimulation)
.

A
homomorphism
, also called a
functional bisimulation
, from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
is a morphism from the structure
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V_{1},{\mathrm{lab}}_{1},{\mathrm{args}}_{1},r_{1}}}}\right\rangle
to the structure
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V_{2},{\mathrm{lab}}_{2},{\mathrm{args}}_{2},r_{2}}}}\right\rangle
, i.e., a function
h:V_{1}\textrightarrow V_{2}
such that, for all
v∈V_{1}
it holds:

\displaystyle h(r_{1})

\displaystyle=r_{2}

(roots)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{lab}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{lab}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h(v)}}\right)

(labels)

\displaystyle h^{*}({\mathrm{args}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right))

\displaystyle={\mathrm{args}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h(v)}}\right)

(arguments)

where
h^{*}
is the homomorphic extension (also: pointwise lifted version) of
h
to words over
V_{1}
, i.e.
h^{*}:V_{1}^{*}\textrightarrow V_{2}^{*}
,
v_{1}\dots v_{n}↦h(v_{1})\dots h(v_{n})
. In this case we write
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{h}G_{2}
, or
G_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{h}G_{1}
. And we write
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G_{2}
, or for that matter
G_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G_{1}
, if there is a homomorphism from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
.

Let
f∈\Sigma
. An
f
-homomorphism
from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
is a homomorphism
h
from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
that ‘shares’ (i.e. maps to the same vertex) only vertices with the label
f
, i.e.
h
has the property that
h(v_{1})=h(v_{2})\;⇒\;{\mathrm{lab}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)={\mathrm{lab}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)=f
holds for all
v_{1}≠v_{2}∈V_{1}
. If
h
is an
f
-homomorphism from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
, then we write
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}^{f}_{h}G_{2}
or
G_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}^{f}_{h}G_{1}
, or dropping
h
,
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}^{f}G_{2}
or
G_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}^{f}G_{1}
.

Terminology 2.8
.

The terms ‘homomorphism’ and ‘functional bisimulation’ will we used interchangeably throughout this document.

Definition 2.9

(isomorphism)
.

An
isomorphism
between
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
is a bijective homomorphism
i:V_{1}\textrightarrow V_{2}
from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
(it follows from the homomorphism conditions
definition

2.7
that also the inverse function
i^{-1}:V_{1}\textrightarrow V_{2}
is a homomorphism). If there is an isomorphism between
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, we write
G_{1}\mathrel{\sim}G_{2}
, and say that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are
isomorphic
. The relation
\mathrel{\sim}
is an equivalence relation on
{\mathrm{TG}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
. For every term graph
G
over
\Sigma
we denote the isomorphism equivalence class
{[{G}]}_{\mathrel{\sim}}
by (the boldface letter)
G
.

Definition 2.10

(bisimulation, bisimilarity)
.

For
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
, let
G_{i}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V_{i},{\mathrm{lab}}_{i},{\mathrm{args}}_{i},r_{i}}}}\right\rangle
be term graphs over signature
\Sigma
. A
bisimulation
between
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
is a relation
R\subseteq V_{1}\times V_{2}
such that the following conditions hold, for all
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{v,v^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle∈R
:

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{r_{1},r_{2}}}}\right\rangle∈R

(roots)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{lab}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)={\mathrm{lab}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\prime}}}\right)

(labels)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\mathrm{args}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right),{\mathrm{args}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\prime}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle∈R^{*}

(arguments)

where the extension
R^{*}\subseteq{V_{1}}^{*}\times{V_{2}}^{*}
of
R
to a relation between words over
V_{1}
and words over
V_{2}
is defined as:

R^{*}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{v_{1}\dots v_{n},w_{1}\dots w_{n}}}}\right\rangle}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {\begin{array}[]{l}n∈ℕ,∀i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n}}}\right\}\penalty\ v_{i}∈V_{1},w_{i}∈V_{2}\\
\text{such that\penalty\ }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{v_{i},w_{i}}}}\right\rangle∈R\text{ for all $1≤i≤n$ }\end{array}}}}\right\}

We write
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G_{2}
if there is a bisimulation between
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, and say that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are
bisimilar
. Bisimilarity
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
is an equivalence relation on classes
{\mathrm{TG}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
of term graphs over a signature
\Sigma
.

An
f
-bisimulation
between
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
is a bisimulation between
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
such that its restriction to vertices with labels different from
f
is a bijective function. If there is an
f
-bisimulation between
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
we say that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are
f
-bisimilar and write
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}^{f}
to indicate
f
-bisimilarity
.

The following proposition is a simple but useful reformulation of the definition
of homomorphism (
definition

2.7
).

Proposition 2.11

(homomorphism)
.

Let
G_{i}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V_{i},{\mathrm{lab}}_{i},{\mathrm{args}}_{i},r_{i}}}}\right\rangle
, for
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
be term graphs over signature
\Sigma
. Let
h:V_{1}\textrightarrow V_{2}
be a function. Then
h
is a homomorphism from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
, if and only if, for all
v,v_{1}∈V_{1}
,
v_{2}∈V_{2}
, and
k∈ℕ
, the following four statements hold:

\displaystyle h(r_{1})=r_{2}

(roots)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{lab}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)={\mathrm{lab}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h(v)}}\right)

(labels)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} h(v_{1})=v_{2}\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ &∀v^{\prime}_{1}∈V_{1}\penalty\ v_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k}v^{\prime}_{1}\\
⇒\penalty\ &∃v^{\prime}_{2}∈V_{2}\penalty\ v_{2}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k}v^{\prime}_{2}\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ h(v^{\prime}_{1})=v^{\prime}_{2}\end{aligned}

(args-forward)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} h(v_{1})=v_{2}\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ &∀v^{\prime}_{2}∈V_{2}\penalty\ v_{2}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k}v^{\prime}_{2}\\
⇒\penalty\ &∃v^{\prime}_{1}∈V_{1}\penalty\ v_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k}v^{\prime}_{1}\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ h(v^{\prime}_{1})=v^{\prime}_{2}\end{aligned}

(args-backward)

Proposition 2.12

(functional bisimulation and paths)
.

For
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
let
G_{i}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V_{i},{\mathrm{lab}}_{i},{\mathrm{args}}_{i},r_{i}}}}\right\rangle
be term graphs over signature
\Sigma
. Let
h:V_{1}\textrightarrow V_{2}
be a homomorphism from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
. Then the following statements hold:

(i)

Every path
\pi:v_{0}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{1}}v_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{2}}v_{2}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{3}}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{n-1}}v_{n-1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{n}}v_{n}
in
G_{1}
has an image
h(\pi)
under
h
in
G_{2}
in the sense that
h(\pi):h(v_{0})\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{1}}h(v_{1})\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{2}}h(v_{2})\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{3}}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{n-1}}h(v_{n-1})\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{n}}h(v_{n})
.

(ii)

For every
v_{0}∈V_{0}
and
v^{\prime}_{0}∈V_{1}
with
h(v_{0})=v^{\prime}_{0}
it holds that every path
\pi^{\prime}:v^{\prime}_{0}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{1}}v^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{1}}v^{\prime}_{2}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{2}}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{n-1}}v^{\prime}_{n-1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{n-1}}v^{\prime}_{n}
in
G_{2}
has a pre-image under
h
in
G_{1}
that starts in
v_{0}
: a unique path
\pi:v_{0}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{1}}v_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{2}}v_{2}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{3}}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{n-1}}v_{n-1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{n}}v_{n}
in
G_{1}
such that
\pi^{\prime}=h(\pi)
holds in the sense of
item

i
.

(iii)

h(V_{1})=V_{2}
, that is,
h
is surjective.

Proof.

Statement
item

i
can be shown by induction on the length of
\pi
, using the
eq.

args-forward
property of
h
from
proposition

2.11
. Analogously, statement
item

ii
can be established by induction on the length of
\pi^{\prime}
, using the
eq.

args-backward
property of
h
from
proposition

2.11
. Statement
item

iii
follows by applying, for given
v^{\prime}∈V_{2}
, the statement of
item

ii
to an access path
\pi^{\prime}
of
v^{\prime}
in
G_{2}
(which exists because term graphs are defined to be root-connected).
∎

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
G
,
G_{1}
, and
G_{2}
be term graphs over a signature
\Sigma
.

(i)

If
G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{h}G
, then
h={\mathrm{id}}_{V}
,
where
{\mathrm{id}}_{V}
is the identity function on the set
V
of vertices of
G
.

(ii)

If
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{h}G_{2}
and
G_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{g}G_{1}
hold,
then
h
and
g
are invertible,
h^{-1}=g
,
and consequently
G_{1}\mathrel{\sim}G_{2}
.

Proof.

For statement
item

i
, suppose that
G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{h}G
holds for some homomorphism
h
from
G
to itself. The fact that
h(v)={\mathrm{id}}_{V}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
holds for all vertices
v
of
G
can be established by induction on the length of the shortest access path of
v
in
G
. Note that we make use here of the root-connectedness of
G
(assumed implicitly, see
section

2
) in the form of the assumption that every vertex can be reached by an access path. In order to show statement
item

ii
, note that
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{h}G_{2}
and
G_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{g}G_{1}
entail
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{{h}∘{g}}G_{1}
for homomorphisms
h
and
g
from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
. From this
{h}∘{g}={\mathrm{id}}_{V_{1}}
, where
V_{1}
the set of vertices of
G_{1}
, follows by
item

i
. Since analogously
{h}∘{g}={\mathrm{id}}_{V_{2}}
follows, where
V_{2}
is the set of vertices of
G_{2}
, the further claims follow.
∎

§ 2.14

(sharing order)
.

The homomorphism relation
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
is a preorder on term graphs over a given signature
\Sigma
. It induces a partial order on the isomorphism equivalence classes of term graphs over
\Sigma
, where anti-symmetry is implied by item
proposition

2.13

item

ii
of the following proposition. We will refer to
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
as the
sharing preorder
, and to the induced relation on isomorphism equivalence classes as the
sharing order
.

Notation 2.15
.

Note that, deviating from some literature
[
51
]
, we use the order relation
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
in the same direction as
≤\,
: if
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G_{2}
, then
G_{2}
is greater or equal to
G_{1}
with respect to the ordering
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
indicating that sharing is typically increased from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
.

Notation 2.16

(equivalence classes of term graphs)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq{\mathrm{TG}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
be a class of term graphs over signature
\Sigma
. For
G∈{\mathrm{TG}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
we will use the notation

{[{\textbf{{G}}}]}_{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}^{\mathcal{K}}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\textbf{{G}}^{\prime}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {G^{\prime}∈\mathcal{K},G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G^{\prime}}}}\right\}

to denote the bisimulation equivalence class of
G
(the
\mathrel{\sim}
-equivalence-class of
G
) with respect to (the
\mathrel{\sim}
-equivalence-classes in)
K
. And we will write

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\textbf{{G}}}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right)^{\mathcal{K}}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\textbf{{G}}^{\prime}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {G^{\prime}∈\mathcal{K},G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G^{\prime}}}}\right\}

to denote the class of all
\mathrel{\sim}
-equivalence classes in
K
that are reachable from
G
via functional bisimulation. For
\mathcal{K}={\mathrm{TG}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
we drop the superscript
K
, and simply write
{[{\textbf{{G}}}]}_{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\textbf{{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}\right)
.

§ 2.17

(complete lattice)
.

A partially ordered set
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{A,≤}}}\right\rangle
is a
complete lattice
if every subset of
A
possesses a least upper bound and a greatest lower bound.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
\Sigma
be a signature, and
G
be a term graph over
\Sigma
. The bisimulation equivalence class
{[{\textbf{{G}}}]}_{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}
of the isomorphism equivalence class
G
of
G
is ordered by functional bisimulation
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
such that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\textbf{{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}\right),\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right\rangle
is a complete lattice.

Remark 2.19
.

The statement of
proposition

2.18
is a restriction to sets of
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
-successors of the statements
[
2
, Theorem 3.19]
and
[
51
, Theorem 13.2.20]
, which confer the complete-lattice property for entire bisimulation equivalence classes (of
\mathrel{\sim}
-equivalence classes) of term graphs.

Definition 2.20

(closedness under functional bisimulation)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq{\mathrm{TG}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
be a subclass of the term graphs over some signature
\Sigma
. We say that
K
is
closed under functional bisimulation
(
closed under bisimulation
), if for all term graphs
G,G^{\prime}∈{\mathrm{TG}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma}}\right)
, whenever
G∈\mathcal{K}
and
G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G^{\prime}
(
G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G^{\prime}
), then also
G^{\prime}∈\mathcal{K}
.

3
Introduction

§ 3.1

(premise)
.

In this chapter we seek to explore graph formalisms to adequately represent
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. In developing these graph representations we draw heavily on ideas from the previous chapter such as λ-transition-graphs as a representation for λ-terms (
section

9
) and the general concepts of including scope
+
‘exctended scope’ in the formalisms. The inclusion of scope
+
is intended to reflect the scoping rules in
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
, i.e. that variables bound by an abstraction or by
\mathsf{let}
can only occur underneath their point of definition. Here we consider term graphs built from three kinds of vertices representating applications, abstractions, and variable occurrences, respectively.

§ 3.2

(three classes of graph formalisms)
.

In particular we study the following three classes of term graphs:

λ-higher-order-term-graphs (
section

4
)

are extensions of first-order term graphs by adding a scope function that assigns a set of vertices, its scope, to every abstraction vertex. There are two variants, one with and one without an edge (a
backlink
) from each variable occurrence to its corresponding abstraction vertex. The class with backlinks is similar to
higher-order term graphs
as defined by Blom in
[
8
]
, and is in fact an adaptation of that concept to the λ-calculus.

abstraction-prefix based λ-higher-order-term-graphs (
section

5
)

abbreviated as λ-ap-ho-term-graphs do not have a scope function but assign, to each vertex
v
, an abstraction prefix consisting of a word of abstraction vertices that includes those abstractions for which
v
is in their scope (it actually lists all abstractions for which
v
is in their
extended scope
, see
definition

7.9
). Abstraction prefixes are aggregated information about the scopes entered so far (much as abstraction prefixes in the decomposition CRSs from the previous chapter; see
2.9
,
4.3
,
definition

4.13
).

λ-term-graphs with scope delimiters (
section

7
)

are plain first-order term graphs intended to represent both classes of higher-order term graphs above, and by extension
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. Instead of relying upon added structures for describing scopes, they use scope-delimiter vertices to signify the end of scopes (much as the scope-delimiting steps in the decomposition CRSs from the previous chapter; see
4.5
). Variable occurrences as well as scope delimiters may or may not have backlinks to their corresponding abstraction vertices.

§ 3.3

(desired properties)
.

We develop these graph formalisms with a number of properties in mind, from which we expect to ensure that the representations are meaningful and useful:

∘

The term graphs should represent some
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term, and in this sense are not be ‘meaningless’.

∘

Each of these classes induces a notion of functional bisimulation and bisimulation, which preserve the unfolding semantics of the term graphs (and therefore also the unfolding semantics of the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term they represent).

∘

Each of these classes induces a sharing order, which reflects the sharing present in the represented
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term.

∘

We are particularly interested in classes of term graphs that are closed under functional bisimulation, which ensures that when increasing sharing in a term graph it still remains meaningful.

§ 3.4

(relating the graph formalisms)
.

It is important to stress that in this chapter we are not going to prove any of properties above that involve in any way
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms, particularly the former three. Instead we take a leap of faith and trust our intuition (and the authority of
[
8
]
) that the λ-higher-order-term-graphs are indeed sound representation of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms in that sense. As mentioned before focus on the graph formalisms themselves, and relate them amongst another. Particularly we establish a bijective correspondence between the λ-higher-order-term-graphs and λ-ap-ho-term-graphs, and a correspondence between λ-ap-ho-term-graphs and λ-term-graphs with scope delimiters that is ‘almost bijective’ (bijective up to the sharing of scope delimiter vertices). We show that all of these correspondences preserve and reflect the sharing order.
It is only in
chapter

3
that make a connection back to
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. In particular, we supply a translation of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms to λ-term-graphs with scope delimiters and back and show that the above desired properties do indeed hold. By the correspondences these properties extend also to the higher-order graph formalisms, which validate our conjecture about λ-higher-order-term-graphs.

4
λ-higher-order-Term-Graphs

Definition 4.1

(signatures
\Sigma^{λ}
,
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{0}}
,
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}
)
.

By
\Sigma^{λ}
we denote the signature
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{@,λ}}}\right\}
with
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(@}}\right)=2
, and
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)=1
. By
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i}}
, for
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
, we denote the extension
\Sigma^{λ}∪\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathsf{0}}}}\right\}
of
\Sigma^{λ}
where
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)=i
. Whether the variable vertices have an outgoing edge depends on the value of
i
. The intention is to consider two variants of term graphs, one with and one without variable backlinks to their corresponding abstraction vertex.

Definition 4.2

(term graphs classes
{\mathcal{T}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
)
.

The classes of term graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{0}}
and
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}
are denoted by
{\mathcal{T}}_{0}:={\mathrm{TG}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{0}}}}\right)
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}:={\mathrm{TG}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}}}\right)
, respectively.

Notation 4.3

(vertex subsets)
.

Let
G=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r}}}\right\rangle
be a term graph over signature
\Sigma
. For
l∈\Sigma
we denote by
{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(l}}\right)
the set of
l
-vertices of
G
, that is, the subset of
V
consisting of all vertices with label
l
; more formally,
{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(l}}\right):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{v∈V}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {{\mathrm{lab}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=l}}}\right\}
.

A ‘λ-higher-order-term-graph’ consists of a
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i}}
-term-graph together with a scope function that maps abstraction vertices to their scopes
+
, which are subsets of the graph’s vertices.

Terminology 4.4

(scope
:=
scope
+
)
.

Henceforth when we write ‘scope’ we mean scope
+
.

Definition 4.5

(scope function for
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i}}
-term-graphs)
.

Let
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
and
G=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r}}}\right\rangle
be a
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i}}
-term-graph. A function
{\mathrm{Sc}}:{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)\textrightarrow\powerset(V)
from λ-vertices of
G
to vertex sets of
G
is called a
scope function
for
G
. Such a function is called
correct
if for all
k∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
, all vertices
v,w∈V
, and all abstraction vertices
x,y∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)
the following holds:

\displaystyle⇒\penalty\ r∉{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)

(root)

\displaystyle⇒\penalty\ x∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)

(self)

\displaystyle x∈{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y}}\right)

\displaystyle⇒\penalty\ {\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\subseteq{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y}}\right)

(nest)

\displaystyle v\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k}w\;∧\;w∈{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)

\displaystyle⇒\penalty\ v∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)

(closed)

\displaystyle v∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)

\displaystyle⇒\penalty\ ∃x∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)\penalty\ v∈{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)

(scope
0
)

\displaystyle v∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)\;∧\;v\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w

\displaystyle⇒\penalty\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &w∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)\;∧\\
&v∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)⇔w∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\end{aligned}}}\right.

(scope
1
)

where
{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right):={\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\setminus\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{v}}}\right\}
. Note that if
i=0
, then
eq.

scope1
is trivially true and hence superfluous, and if
i=1
, then
eq.

scope0
is redundant, because it follows from
eq.

scope1
.

We say that
G

admits
a correct scope function if such a function exists for
G
.

Definition 4.6

(λ-ho-term-graph)
.

Let
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
. A
λ-ho-term-graph
(short for
λ-higher-order-term-graph
) over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i}}
, is a tuple
\mathcal{G}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r,{\mathrm{Sc}}}}}\right\rangle
where
G_{\mathcal{G}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r}}}\right\rangle
is a
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i}}
-term-graph, called the term graph
underlying

G
, and
{\mathrm{Sc}}
is a correct scope function for
G_{\mathcal{G}}
. The classes of λ-ho-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{0}}
and
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}
will be denoted by
\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{0}
and
\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{1}
.

Example 4.7
.

Note that there is some freedom on how big the scopes are chosen. The minimal choice corresponds to scope
+
in
definition

7.9
. See
fig.

1
for two different λ-ho-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i}}
both of which represent the same term in the λ-calculus with
\mathsf{letrec}
, namely
\mathsf{let}\;{f=λ{x}.\,{{(λ{y}.\,{{y}\;{({x}\;{g})}})}\;{(λ{z}.\,{{g}\;{f}})}},\;g=λ{u}.\,{u}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}
.

\mathcal{G}-0
:

\mathcal{G}-1
:

Figure 1:
\mathcal{G}_{0}
and
\mathcal{G}_{1}
are λ-ho-term-graphs in
\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{i}
where the dotted backlink edges are present for
i=1
, but absent for
i=0
. The underlying term graphs of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
and
\mathcal{G}_{1}
are identical but their scope functions (signified by the shaded areas) differ. While in
\mathcal{G}_{0}
scopes are chosen as small as possible (eager scope-closure), in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
some scopes are closed only later in the graph.

The following lemma states some basic properties of the scope function in λ-ho-term-graphs. Most importantly, scopes in λ-ho-term-graphs are properly nested, in analogy with scopes
+
in finite λ-terms.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
, and let
\mathcal{G}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r,{\mathrm{Sc}}}}}\right\rangle
be a λ-ho-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i}}
. For
v∈V
and
x∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)
we say that
x
is a
binder for

v
if
v∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
, and we denote by
{\mathrm{bds}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{x\in{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {v∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)}}}\right\}
the set of binders of
v
. Then the following statements hold for all
w∈V
and
v,v_{1},v_{2}∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)
:

(i)

If
w∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
, then
v
is visited on every access path of
w
, and all vertices on access paths of
w
after
v
are in
{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
. Hence (since
G_{\mathcal{G}}
is a term graph, every vertex has an access path)
{\mathrm{bds}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)
is finite.

(ii)

If
{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)∩{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)≠\emptyset
for
v_{1}≠v_{2}
, then
{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)\subseteq{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)
or
{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)\subseteq{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)
. As a consequence, if
{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)∩{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)≠\emptyset
, then
{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)\subset{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)
or
{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)={\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)
or
{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)\subset{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)
.

(iii)

If
{\mathrm{bds}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)≠\emptyset
, then
{\mathrm{bds}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{v_{0},\dots,v_{n}}}}\right\}
for
v_{0},\dots,v_{n}∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)
and
{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{n}}}\right)\subset{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{n-1}}}\right)\dots\subset{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{0}}}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
, and let
\mathcal{G}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r,{\mathrm{Sc}}}}}\right\rangle
be a λ-ho-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i}}
.

For showing
item

i
, let
w∈V
and
v∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)
be such that
w∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
. Suppose that
\pi:r=w_{0}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{1}}w_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{2}}w_{2}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{3}}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{k_{n}}w_{n}=w
is an access path of
w
. If
w=v
, then nothing remains to be shown. Otherwise
w_{n}=w∈{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
, and, if
n>0
, then by
eq.

closed
it follows that
w_{n-1}∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
. This argument can be repeated to find subsequently smaller
i
with
w_{i}∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
and
w_{i+1},\dots,w_{n}∈{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
. We can proceed as long as
w_{i}∈{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
. But since, due to
eq.

root
,
w_{0}=r∉{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
, eventually we must encounter an
i_{0}
such that such that
w_{i_{0}+1},\dots,w_{n}∈{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
and
w_{i_{0}}∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\setminus{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
. This implies
w_{i_{0}}=v
, showing that
v
is visited on
\pi
.

For showing
item

ii
, let
w∈V
and
v_{1},v_{2}∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)
,
v_{1}≠v_{2}
be such that
w∈{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)∩{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)
. Let
\pi
be an access path of
w
. Then it follows by
item

i
that both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are visited on
\pi
, and that, depending on whether
v_{1}
or
v_{2}
is visited first on
\pi
, either
v_{2}∈{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)
or
v_{1}∈{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)
. Then due to
eq.

nest
it follows that either

{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)\subseteq{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)
holds or
{\mathrm{Sc}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)\subseteq{\mathrm{{Sc}^{-}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)
.

Finally, statement
item

iii
is an easy consequence of statement
item

ii
.
∎

Remark 4.9

(Comparison to ‘higher-order term graphs’
[
8
]
)
.

The notion of λ-ho-term-graph is an adaptation of the notion of ‘higher-order term graph’ by Blom
[
8
, Definition 3.2.2]
for the purpose of representing finite or infinite λ-terms or cyclic (i.e. strongly regular) λ-terms. In particular, λ-ho-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}
correspond closely to higher-order term graphs over signature
\Sigma^{λ}
. But they differ in the following respects:

abstractions:

Higher-order term graphs in
[
8
]
are graph representations of finite or infinite terms in Combinatory Reduction Systems (CRSs). They typically contain abstraction vertices with label
\Box
that represent CRS abstractions. In contrast, λ-ho-term-graphs have abstraction vertices with label λ that denote λ-abstractions.

signature:

Whereas higher-order term graphs in
[
8
]
are based on an arbitrary CRS signature, λ-ho-term-graphs only contain the application symbol @ and the variable-occurrence symbol
\mathsf{0}
in addition to the abstraction symbol λ.

variable backlinks and variable occurrence vertices:

In
[
8
]

there are no explicit vertices that represent variable occurrences. Instead, variable occurrences are represented by backlink edges to abstraction vertices. Actually, in the formalisation chosen in
[
8
, Definition 3.2.1]
, a backlink edge does not directly target the abstraction vertex
v
it refers to, but ends at a special variant vertex
\bar{v}
of
v
. (Every such variant abstraction vertex
\bar{v}
could be looked upon as a variable vertex that is shared by all edges that represent occurrences of the variable bound by the abstraction vertex
v
.)

In λ-ho-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}
a variable occurrence is represented by a variable vertex that as outgoing edge has a backlink to the abstraction vertex that binds the occurrence.

conditions on the scope function:

While the conditions
eq.

root
,
eq.

self
,
eq.

nest
, and
eq.

closed
on the scope function in higher-order term graphs in
[
8
, Definition 3.2.2]
correspond directly to the respective conditions in
definition

4.6
, the difference between the condition (scope) there and
eq.

scope1
in
definition

4.6
reflects the difference described in the previous item.

free variables:

Whereas the higher-order term graphs in
[
8
]
cater for the presence of free variables, free variables have been excluded from the basic format of λ-ho-term-graphs.

In the following, let
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
and let
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
be λ-ho-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i}}
with
\mathcal{G}_{k}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V_{k},{\mathrm{lab}}_{k},{\mathrm{args}}_{k},r_{k},{\mathrm{Sc}}_{k}}}}\right\rangle
for
k∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
.

Definition 4.10

(homomorphism)
.

A
homomorphism
from
\mathcal{G}_{1}
to
\mathcal{G}_{2}
