G^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}^{\mathsf{S}}G
, and consequently
G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}^{\mathsf{S}}G^{\prime}
. We can say that
G
and
G^{\prime}
only differ in their ‘degree of
\mathsf{S}
-sharing’. Since
{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{i,j}
ignores
\mathsf{S}
-vertices and their sharing, the degree of
\mathsf{S}
-sharing cannot be reflected in the corresponding λ-ap-ho-term-graph. This observation leads us to look for a weaker (than bijective) correspondence between λ-ap-ho-term-graph and λ-term-graphs: the weakening consists of equating
\mathsf{S}
-bisimilar λ-term-graphs as in the following proposition.

Proposition 7.25
.

Let
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
and
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
. The mapping
{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{i,j}
in
proposition

7.19
maps two λ-term-graphs that are
\mathsf{S}
-bisimilar to the same λ-ap-ho-term-graph. That is, for all λ-term-graphs
G_{1},G_{2}
over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i},\mathsf{S}_{j}}
it holds:

G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}^{\mathsf{S}}G_{2}\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ {\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{i,j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{1}}}\right)\mathrel{\sim}{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{i,j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{2}}}\right)

Remark 7.26

(λ-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{i},\mathsf{S}_{j}}
up to
\mathsf{S}
-bisimilarity)
.

In the original paper
[
24
, 5.15]
, we use this insight to develop a variation on
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}
, where the correctness condition from
definition

7.5
is relaxed to non-delimiter vertices. Consequently an
\mathsf{S}
-vertex is allowed to be a delimiter for two different scopes (as
G
in
example

7.23
). We show that these λ-term-graphs are always
\mathsf{S}
-bisimilar to graphs in
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}
and that there is a bijective correspondence with λ-ap-ho-term-graphs if we consider equivalence classes of these λ-term-graphs up to isomorphism and
\mathsf{S}
-bisimilarity. We omit this part this thesis. Instead we lean on the not quite bijective correspondence from
proposition

7.25
as is.

§ 7.27

(outlook)
.

Be reminded that we seek to implement bisimulation and functional bisimulation on higher-order term graphs via (functional) bisimulation on λ-term-graphs (which are first-order term graphs). Now that we have a correspondence result that relates λ-term-graphs and higher-order term graphs, we investigate how λ-term-graphs behave under bisimulation and functional bisimulation, specifically which classes of λ-term-graphs are closed under (functional) bisimulation.

8
Not closed under (functional) bisimulation

§ 8.1

(overview)
.

In this section we collect negative results concerning closedness under bisimulation and functional bisimulation for the classes of λ-term-graphs as introduced in the previous section.

Proposition 8.2
.

None of the classes
\mathcal{T}_{1}^{λ}
and
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}
, for
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
and
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
, of λ-term-graphs are closed under bisimulation.

§ 8.3
.

This proposition is an immediate consequence of the following proposition, which can be viewed as a refinement, because it formulates non-closedness of classes of λ-term-graphs under specialisations of bisimulation, namely for functional bisimulation (under which some classes are not closed), and for converse functional bisimulation (under which none of the classes considered here is closed).

Proposition 8.4
.

None of the classes
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}
of λ-term-graphs for
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
and
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
are closed under functional bisimulation, or under converse functional bisimulation. Additionally, the class
\mathcal{T}_{1}^{λ}
of λ-term-graphs is not closed under converse functional bisimulation.

Proof.

We proof the following statements by giving counterexamples:

(i)

None of the classes
\mathcal{T}_{0,j}^{λ}
for
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
are closed under
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
, or under
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
.

(ii)

None of the classes
\mathcal{T}_{1}^{λ}
and
\mathcal{T}_{1,j}^{λ}
for
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
of λ-term-graphs are closed under converse functional bisimulation
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
.

(iii)

The class
\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{λ}
of λ-term-graphs is not closed
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
.

(iv)

The class
\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}
of λ-term-graphs is not closed under
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
.

The counterexamples:

(i)

Let
\Delta
be one of the signatures
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{0},\mathsf{S}_{j}}

j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
. Consider the following term graphs over
\Delta
:

G-2
:

G-1
:

G-0
:

It holds that
G_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G_{0}
. But while
G_{2}
and
G_{0}
admit correct abstraction-prefix functions over
\Delta
(since the implied scopes (drawn shaded) are nested), this is not the case for
G_{1}
(overlapping scopes). Therefore, the class of λ-term-graphs over
\Delta
is closed neither under functional bisimulation nor under converse functional bisimulation.

(ii)

Let
\Delta
be one of the signatures
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}
and
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{j}}
. Consider the following term graphs over
\Delta
:

G-1
:

G-0
:

It holds that
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G_{0}
. But while
G_{0}
admits a correct abstraction-prefix function, this is not the case for
G_{1}
(overlapping scopes). Therefore, the class of λ-term-graphs over
\Delta
is not closed under converse functional bisimulation.

(iii)

Consider the following term graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{1}}
:

G-1
:

G-0
:

It holds that
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G_{0}
. However, while
G_{1}
admits a correct abstraction-prefix function, this is not the case for
G_{0}
(overlapping scopes). Therefore the class of λ-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{1}}
is not closed under functional bisimulation.

(iv)

Consider the following term graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
:

G-1
:

G-0
:

It holds that
G_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G_{0}
. However, while
G_{1}
admits a correct abstraction-prefix function, this is not the case for
G_{0}
(overlapping scopes). Therefore the class of λ-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
is not closed under functional bisimulation.

∎

As an easy consequence of
proposition

8.2
, and of
proposition

8.4

item

i
and
item

ii
, together with the examples used in the proof, we obtain the following two propositions.

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
. None of the classes
\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{i}
of λ-ho-term-graphs, or
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\smash{(λ)}}
of λ-ap-ho-term-graphs are closed under bisimulation on the underlying term graphs.

Proposition 8.6
.

The following statements hold:

(i)

Neither
\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{0}
nor
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\smash{(λ)}}
is closed under functional bisimulation or converse functional bisimulation on the underlying term graphs.

(ii)

Neither
\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{1}
nor
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\smash{(λ)}}
is closed under converse functional bisimulation on the underlying term graphs.

Remark 8.7
.

Note that
proposition

8.6
,
item

i
is a strengthening of the statement of
proposition

6.5
earlier.

9
Closed under functional bisimulation

§ 9.1

(recapitulation)
.

The negative results gathered in the last section leave the impression that our enterprise in a quite poor state: For the classes of λ-term-graphs we introduced,
proposition

8.4
leaves
\mathcal{T}_{1}^{λ}
as the only class of λ-term-graphs which still might be closed under functional bisimulation. Actually,
\mathcal{T}_{1}^{λ}

is
closed (we do not prove this here), but that does not help us any further, because the correspondences in
theorem

7.20
do not apply to this class, and worse still,
proposition

6.6
rules out simple correspondences for
\mathcal{T}_{1}^{λ}
. So in this case we are left without any satisfying correspondences to higher-order term graphs that we have for the other classes of λ-term-graphs; those however are not closed under functional bisimulation.

§ 9.2

(eager scope-closure)
.

But in this section we establish that the class
\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}
is very useful after all: we find that its restriction to λ-term-graphs with eager scope-closure (
definition

9.4
below) is in fact closed under functional bisimulation.

Example 9.3

(eager-scope λ-term-graphs)
.

Let us look at two
\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}
-term-graphs from earlier and see whether we can fix closedness under functional bisimulation in that instance by making them eager-scope. Consider the λ-term-graph
G_{1}
from the
proof
of
proposition

8.4

item

iv
. The scopes of the two topmost abstractions are not closed on the paths to variable occurrences belonging to the bottommost abstractions, although both scopes could have been closed immediately before the bottommost abstractions. When this is actually done, and the following variation
\tilde{G}_{1}
of
G_{1}
with eager scope-closure is obtained, then the problem disappears:

\tilde{G}-1
:

\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}

\tilde{G}-0
:

\tilde{G}_{0}
has again a correct abstraction-prefix function and is therefore a λ-term-graph.

For λ-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{1}}
and
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
we define the eager-scope property.

Definition 9.4

(eager-scope λ-term-graphs)
.

Let
G=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r}}}\right\rangle
be a λ-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{j}}
for
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
with abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P}}:V\textrightarrow V^{*}
. We say that
G
is an
eager-scope λ-term-graph
, or that
G

is eager-scope
if:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\begin{aligned} &∀v,w∈V\penalty\ ∀p∈V^{*}\\
&\indent{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=p\,w\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ v∉{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{S}}}\right)\\
&\indent\indent⇒\penalty\ \begin{aligned} &∃n∈ℕ\penalty\ ∃v_{1},\dots,v_{n}∈V\\
&\indent v\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{n}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{0}w\penalty\ ∧\\
&\indent v_{n}∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ ∀i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n}}}\right\}\penalty\ {\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)≤{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{i}}}\right)\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\penalty\ \penalty\ }}\right\}

(4)

In words: from every non-delimiter vertex
v
with non-empty abstraction-prefix
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
ending with
w
there is a path to
w
via vertices with abstraction-prefixes that extend
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
(a path within the scope of
w
) and via a variable vertex just before reaching
w
.

By
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,j}^{λ}}
we denote the subclass of
\mathcal{T}_{1,j}^{λ}
of all eager-scope λ-term-graphs.

§ 9.5

(fully backlinked λ-term-graphs)
.

We will find that not only the class of eager λ-term-graphs is closed under functional bisimulation, but also a super-class thereof, called ‘fully backlinked’ λ-term-graphs.

Definition 9.6

(fully backlinked λ-term-graphs)
.

Let
G=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r}}}\right\rangle
be a λ-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{j}}
for
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
with abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P}}:V\textrightarrow V^{*}
. We say that
G
is
fully backlinked
if:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\begin{aligned} &∀v,w∈V\penalty\ ∀p∈V^{*}\\
&\indent{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=p\,w\penalty\ ⇒\penalty\ \begin{aligned} &∃n∈ℕ\penalty\ ∃v_{1},\dots,v_{n}∈V\\
&\indent v\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{n}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w\\
&\indent∧\penalty\ ∀i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n}}}\right\}\penalty\ {\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)≤{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{i}}}\right)\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\penalty\ \penalty\ }}\right\}

(5)

In words: from every vertices
v
with non-empty abstraction prefix
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
that ends with
w
, there is a path from
v
to
w
via vertices with abstraction-prefixes that extend
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
(a path within the scope of
w
).

By
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{fbl}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,j}^{λ}}
we denote the subclass of
\mathcal{T}_{1,j}^{λ}
that consists of all fully backlinked λ-term-graphs.

Proposition 9.7

(
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}\subseteq\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{fbl}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
)
.

Every eager-scope λ-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
is fully backlinked.

Proof.

Let
G
be an λ-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
with abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P}}
and vertex set
V
. Suppose that
G
is an eager-scope λ-term-graph. This means that the condition
eq.

4
from
definition

9.4
holds for
G
. Now note that, for all
w,v∈V
and
p∈V^{*}
, if
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)=p\,v
and
w∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{S}}}\right)
holds, then the correctness condition
eq.

S
1

on the abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P}}
entails
w\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{1}v
. It follows that
G
also satisfies the condition
eq.

5
from
definition

9.6
, and therefore, that
G
is fully backlinked.
∎

§ 9.8

(backlinks and fully backlinkedness)
.

The intuition for the property of a λ-term-graph
G
to be ‘fully backlinked’ is that, for every vertex
w
of
G
with a non-empty abstraction-prefix
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)=p\,v
, it is possible to get back to the final abstraction vertex
v
in the abstraction-prefix of
w
by a directed path via vertices in the scope of
v
and via a last edge that is a backlink from a variable or a delimiter vertex to the abstraction vertex
v
. Therefore the presence of both sorts of backlink in λ-term-graphs is crucial for this concept. Indeed, at least backlinks for variables have to be present so that the property to be fully backlinked can make sense for a λ-term-graph: all λ-term-graphs with variable vertices but without variable backlinks (for example, consider a representation of the λ-term
λ{x}.\,{x}
by such a term graph) are not fully backlinked.

§ 9.9

(eager-scope λ-term-graphs without variable backlinks)
.

The situation is different for the eager-scope property. While the presence of backlinks was used for the definition of ‘eager-scope λ-term-graphs’ in
eq.

4
, the assumption that all variable vertices have backlinks is not essential there. In fact the condition
eq.

4
can be generalised to apply also to λ-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{0},\mathsf{S}_{1}}
and
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{0},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
as follows:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\begin{aligned} &∀v,w∈V\penalty\ ∀p∈V^{*}\\
&\indent{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=p\,w\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ v∉{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{S}}}\right)\\
&\indent\indent⇒\penalty\ \begin{aligned} &∃n∈ℕ\penalty\ ∃v_{1},\dots,v_{n}∈V\penalty\ \\
&\indent v\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{n}\\
&\indent∧\penalty\ v_{n}∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ {\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{n}}}\right)=p\,w\\
&\indent∧\penalty\ ∀i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n}}}\right\}\penalty\ {\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)≤{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{i}}}\right)\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\penalty\ \penalty\ }}\right\}

(6)

For λ-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{1}}
and
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
the conditions
eq.

4
and
eq.

6
coincide: For the implication
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:def:eager:scope:without:var:backlinks}⇒\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:def:eager:scope}
note that the correctness condition
eq.

0
0

on the abstraction-prefix functions of these λ-term-graphs yields that the statements
v_{n}∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)
and
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{n}}}\right)={\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=p\,w
imply that
v_{n}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{0}w
. For the implication
\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:def:eager:scope}}⇒\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:def:eager:scope:without:var:backlinks}}
observe that
v_{n}∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)
,
v_{n}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{0}w
, and
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{n}}}\right)≤p\,w
implies
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{n}}}\right)={\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=p\,w
in view of the condition
eq.

0
0

and
lemma

7.8
.

Proposition 9.10
.

Functional bisimulation on λ-term-graphs in
\mathcal{T}_{1,j}^{λ}
with
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
preserves and reflects the properties ‘eager scope’ and ‘fully backlinked’.

Proof.

We only show preservation under homomorphic image of the eager-scope property for λ-term-graphs, since preservation of the property ‘fully backlinked’ can be shown analogously and involves less technicalities. Also, reflection of these properties under functional bisimulation can be demonstrated similarly.

Let
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
. Let
G_{1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V_{1},{\mathrm{lab}}_{1},{\mathrm{args}}_{1},r_{1}}}}\right\rangle
and
G_{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V_{2},{\mathrm{lab}}_{2},{\mathrm{args}}_{2},r_{2}}}}\right\rangle
be λ-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{j}}
with (correct) abstraction-prefix functions
{\mathrm{P}}_{1}
and
{\mathrm{P}}_{2}
, respectively. Let
h:V_{1}\textrightarrow V_{2}
be a homomorphism from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
, and suppose that
G_{1}
is eager-scope. We show that also
G_{2}
is an eager-scope λ-term-graph.

For this, let
w^{\prime}∈V_{2}
such that
w^{\prime}∉{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{S}}}\right)
and
{\mathrm{P}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{\prime}}}\right)=p^{\prime}\,v^{\prime}
for some
v^{\prime}∈V_{2}
and
p^{\prime}∈(V_{2})^{*}
. Since
h
is surjective by
proposition

2.12

item

iii
, there exists a vertex
w∈V_{1}
such that
h(w)=w^{\prime}
. Now note that, due to
proposition

7.15
,
{\mathrm{P}}_{2}
is the homomorphic image of
{\mathrm{P}}_{1}
. It follows that
h^{*}({\mathrm{P}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right))={\mathrm{P}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h(w)}}\right)={\mathrm{P}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{\prime}}}\right)=p^{\prime}\,v^{\prime}
. Hence there exist
v∈V_{1}
and
p∈(V_{1})^{*}
such that
{\mathrm{P}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)=p\,v
and
h^{*}(p)=p^{\prime}
and
h(v)=v^{\prime}
. Since
G_{1}
is an eager-scope λ-term-graph, there exists a path in
G_{1}
of the form:

\pi:\penalty\ w=w_{0}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w_{n}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}_{0}v

such that
w_{n}∈{\mathrm{V}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)
and
{\mathrm{P}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w_{i}}}\right)≥p\,v
for all
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,\dots,n}}}\right\}
. As
h
is a homomorphism, it follows from
proposition

2.12

item

i
that
\pi
has an image
h(\pi)
in
G_{2}
of the form:

h(\pi):\penalty\ w^{\prime}=h(w)=h(w_{0})\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}^{\prime}h(w_{1})\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}^{\prime}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}^{\prime}h(w_{n})\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}^{\prime}_{0}h(v)=v^{\prime}

where
\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}^{\prime}
is the directed-edge relation in
G_{2}
. Using again that
h
is a homomorphism, it follows that
h(w_{n})∈{\mathrm{V}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)
. Due to the fact that
{\mathrm{P}}_{2}
is the homomorphic image of
{\mathrm{P}}_{1}
it follows that for all
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,\dots,n}}}\right\}
it holds:
{\mathrm{P}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h(w_{i})}}\right)=h^{*}({\mathrm{P}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w_{i}}}\right))≥h^{*}(p\,v)=h^{*}(p)\,h(v)=p^{\prime}\,v^{\prime}
. Hence we have shown that for
w^{\prime}_{i}:=h(w_{i})∈V_{2}
with
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,\dots,n}}}\right\}
it holds that
w^{\prime}=w^{\prime}_{0}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}^{\prime}w^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}^{\prime}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}^{\prime}w^{\prime}_{n}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}^{\prime}_{0}v^{\prime}
such that
w^{\prime}_{n}∈{\mathrm{V}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)
and
{\mathrm{P}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{\prime}_{i}}}\right)≥p^{\prime}\,w^{\prime}
for all
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,\dots,n}}}\right\}
. In this way we have shown that also
G_{2}
is eager-scope.

For showing that the eager-scope property is reflected by a homomorphism
h
from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
the fact that paths in
G_{2}
have pre-images under
h
in
G
(
proposition

2.12

item

ii
) can be used.
∎

§ 9.11

(generalised conditions for eager-scope and fully backlinked)
.

The following proposition states that the defining conditions for a λ-term-graph to be eager-scope (
definition

9.4
) or fully backlinked (
definition

9.6
) can be generalised. The generalised condition for eager-scopedness requires that for every non-delimiter vertex
v
with
{\mathrm{P}}{v}=p\,w\,q
there exists a path from
v
to
w
within the scope of
w
that only transits variable-vertex backlinks, but not delimiter-vertex backlinks. In the generalised condition for fully-backlinkedness the conclusion, in which the path may also proceed via delimiter-vertex backlinks, holds for all vertices.

Proposition 9.12

(generalised conditions for eager-scope and fully backlinked)
.

Let
G
be a λ-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
, and let
{\mathrm{P}}
be its abstraction-prefix function.

(i)

G
is an eager-scope λ-term-graph if and only if:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\begin{aligned} &∀v,w∈V\penalty\ ∀p,q∈V^{*}\\
&\indent{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=p\,w\,q\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ v∉{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{S}}}\right)\\
&\indent\indent⇒\penalty\ \begin{aligned} &∃n∈ℕ\penalty\ ∃v_{1},\dots,v_{n}∈V\\
&\indent v\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{n}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w\penalty\ ∧\\
&\indent v_{n}∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ ∀0≤i≤n\penalty\ p\,w≤{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{i}}}\right)\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\penalty\ \penalty\ }}\right\}

(7)

(ii)

The λ-term-graph
G
is fully backlinked if and only if:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\begin{aligned} &∀v,w∈V\penalty\ ∀p,q∈V^{*}\\
&\indent{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=p\,w\,q\penalty\ ⇒\penalty\ \begin{aligned} &∃n∈ℕ\penalty\ ∃v_{1},\dots,v_{n}∈V\\
&\indent v\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{n}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w\\
&\indent∧\penalty\ ∀0≤i≤n\penalty\ p\,w≤{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{i}}}\right)\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\penalty\ \penalty\ }}\right\}

(8)

Proof.

We will only prove the statement
item

ii
, since statement
item

i
can be proven in much the same way.

Let
G
be an arbitrary λ-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{j}}
with
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
. If
eq.

8
holds for
G
, then so does its special case
eq.

5
, and it follows that
G
is fully backlinked.

For showing the converse we assume that
G
is fully backlinked. Then
eq.

5
holds. We show
eq.

8
, which is universally quantified over
q∈V^{*}
, by induction on the length
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{q}}}\right|
of
q
.

In the base case we have
q=\epsilon
, and the statement to be establish follows immediately from
eq.

5
.

For the induction step, let
p,q∈V^{*}
and
v,w∈V
be such that
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=p\,w\,q
with
q=w^{\prime}\,q_{0}
for
w^{\prime}∈V
and
q_{0}∈V^{*}
. Due to
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{q_{0}}}}\right|<\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{q}}}\right|
, the induction hypothesis can be applied to
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=(p\,w)\,w^{\prime}\,q_{0}
, yielding a path
v\rightarrowtail^{*}v^{\prime}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w^{\prime}
, for some
v^{\prime}∈V
, that in its part between
v
and
v^{\prime}
visits vertices with
p\,w\,w^{\prime}
as prefix of their abstraction prefixes. Due to
p\,w\,w^{\prime}≤{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\prime}}}\right)
it follows that
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{\prime}}}\right)=p\,w
by
lemma

7.8
, (i) (which entails that
p\,w\,w^{\prime}={\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\prime}}}\right)
and that
v^{\prime}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w^{\prime}
must be a backlink). From this the condition
eq.

5
yields a path
w^{\prime}\rightarrowtail^{*}v^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w
, for some
v^{\prime\prime}∈V
, that on the way from
w^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
visits vertices with
p\,w
as prefix of their abstraction prefix. Combining these two paths, we obtain a path
v\rightarrowtail^{*}v^{\prime}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w^{\prime}\rightarrowtail^{*}v^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w
that before it reaches
v^{\prime\prime}
visits only vertices with
p\,w
as prefix of their abstraction prefixes. This establishes the statement to show for the induction step.
∎

§ 9.13
.

In order to show that eager-scope and fully backlinked λ-term-graphs are closed under functional bisimulation, we first establish the following lemma: if two vertices of such a λ-term-graph have the same image under a homomorphism
h
, then so do their abstraction prefixes.

Lemma 9.14
.

Let
G∈\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}
be a fully backlinked λ-term-graph with vertex set
V
and abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P}}
. Let
G^{\prime}∈{\mathcal{T}}_{{1},{2}}
be a term graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
such that
G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{h}G^{\prime}
for a homomorphism
h
. Then it holds:

∀v_{1},v_{2}∈V\penalty\ \;h(v_{1})=h(v_{2})\penalty\ ⇒\penalty\ h^{*}({\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right))=h^{*}({\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right))

(9)

where
h^{*}
is the homomorphic extension of
h
to words over
V
.

To prove this lemma, as a stepping stone, we first prove the following technical lemma. To this end we use the generalised condition for fully backlinkedness from
proposition

9.12
to relate the abstraction-prefixes of vertices on paths departing from
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.

Lemma 9.15
.

Let
G=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r}}}\right\rangle∈\mathcal{T}_{1,j}^{λ}
for
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
with abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P}}
. Let
G^{\prime}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V^{\prime},{\mathrm{lab}}^{\prime},{\mathrm{args}}^{\prime},r^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle∈{\mathcal{T}}_{{1},{j}}
(i.e.
G^{\prime}
is not necessarily a λ-term-graph) such that
G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{h}G^{\prime}
for a homomorphism
h
. Let
v_{1},v_{2}∈V
be such that
h(v_{1})=h(v_{2})
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)=p_{1}\,w_{1}\,q_{1}
with
w_{1}∈V
and
p_{1},q_{1}∈V^{*}
and that
\pi_{1}
is a path from
v_{1}
to
w_{1}
in
G
of the form

\pi_{1}:v_{1}=v_{1,0}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{1,1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{1,n-1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{1,n}=w_{1}

such that furthermore
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1,n-1}}}\right)=p_{1}\,w_{1}
holds.

Then there are
w_{2}∈V
and
p_{2},q_{2}∈V^{*}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{q_{2}}}}\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{q_{1}}}}\right|
such that
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)=p_{2}\,w_{2}\,q_{2}
, and a path
\pi_{2}
in
G
from
v_{2}
to
w_{1}
of the form

\pi_{2}:v_{2}=v_{2,0}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{2,1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{2,n-1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{2,n}=w_{2}

such that
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2,n-1}}}\right)=p_{2}\,w_{2}
, and that
h(v_{1,j})=h(v_{2,j})
for all
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,\dots,n}}}\right\}
, and in particular
h(w_{1})=h(w_{2})
.

Proof.

By
lemma

7.8
(i), from
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1,n-1}}}\right)=p_{1}\,w_{1}
it follows that
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w_{1}}}\right)=p_{1}
. Hence the final transition in
\pi_{1}
must be either via the backlink of a variable vertex or a delimiter vertex and is therefore either of the form
v_{1,n-1}\mathrel{\prescript{\smash{\mathsf{0}}}{}{\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}}}_{0}v_{1,n}
or of the form
v_{1,n-1}\mathrel{\prescript{\smash{\mathsf{S}}}{}{\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}}}_{1}v_{1,n}
.

Furthermore by
proposition

2.12

item

i
, it follows that there is a path
h(\pi_{1})
in
G^{\prime}
from
h(v_{1})
to
h(w_{1})
of the form:

h(\pi_{1}):h(v_{1})=h(v_{1,0})\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}h(v_{1,1})\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}h(v_{1,n-1})\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}h(v_{1,n})=h(w_{1})

From this path,
proposition

2.12

item

ii
, yields a path
\pi_{2}
in
G
from
v_{2}
of the form:

\pi_{2}:v_{2}=v_{2,0}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{2,1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{2,n-1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{2,n}=w_{2}

such that
h(\pi_{2})=h(\pi_{1})
. Therefore it holds
h(v_{1,j})=h(v_{2,j})
for all
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n}}}\right\}
, and in particular
h(w_{1})=h(w_{2})
. Since
h
is a homomorphism also
{\mathrm{lab}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1,j}}}\right)={\mathrm{lab}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2,j}}}\right)
follows for all
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,\dots,n}}}\right\}
. Therefore, and due to
lemma

7.8

item

iv
, the abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P}}
quantitatively behaves the same when stepping through
\pi_{2}
as when stepping through
\pi_{1}
. It follows that
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)=p_{2}\,w\,q_{2}
,
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2,n-1}}}\right)=p_{2}\,w
, and
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w_{2}}}\right)=p_{2}
for some
w∈V
, and
p_{2},q_{2}∈V^{*}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{q_{2}}}}\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{q_{1}}}}\right|
. Due to
{\mathrm{lab}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2,n-1}}}\right)={\mathrm{lab}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1,n-1}}}\right)
, and since the final transition
v_{1,n-1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w_{1}
in
\pi_{1}
is one along a backlink of a variable or delimiter vertex, this also holds for the final transition
v_{2,n-1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w_{2}
in
\pi_{2}
. Then it follows by the correctness condition
eq.

0
1

or
eq.

S
1

, respectively, that
w=w_{2}
, and therefore that
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)=p_{2}\,w_{2}\,q_{2}
, and
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2,n-1}}}\right)=p_{2}\,w_{2}
.
∎

Relying on this lemma, we can now give a rather straightforward proof of
lemma

9.14
.

Proof of
lemma

9.14
.

Let
G
,
G^{\prime}
be as assumed in the lemma, and let
h
be a homomorphism that witnesses
G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{h}G^{\prime}
. We first show:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\begin{aligned} &∀v_{1},v_{2}∈V\penalty\ ∀w_{1}∈V\penalty\ ∀p_{1},q_{1}∈V^{*}\\
&\indent h(v_{1})=h(v_{2})\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ {\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)=p_{1}\,w_{1}\,q_{1}\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\\
&\indent\indent∃w_{2}∈V\penalty\ ∃p_{2},q_{2}∈V^{*}\penalty\ \begin{aligned} &{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)=p_{2}\,w_{2}\,q_{2}\penalty\ ∧\\
&h(w_{1})=h(w_{2})\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{q_{2}}}}\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{q_{1}}}}\right|\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\penalty\ \penalty\ }}\right\}

(10)

For this, let
v_{1},v_{2}∈V
be such that
h(v_{1})=h(v_{2})
and
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)=p_{1}\,w_{1}\,q_{1}
for
p_{1},q_{1}∈V^{*}
and
w_{1}∈V
. Now since
G
is fully backlinked, there is a path

\pi_{1}:v_{1}=v_{1,0}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}^{*}v_{1,n-1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}v_{1,n}=w_{1}
in
G
with
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1,n-1}}}\right)=p_{1}\,w_{1}
.
Then
lemma

9.15
yields the existence of
p_{1},q_{1}∈V^{*}
and
w_{1}∈V
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{q_{2}}}}\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{q_{1}}}}\right|
such that
h(w_{1})=h(w_{2})
, and

{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)=p_{2}\,w_{2}\,q_{2}
.
This establishes
eq.

10
.

As an direct consequence of
eq.

10
we obtain:

\displaystyle∀v_{1},v_{2}∈V\penalty\ ∀r_{1}∈V^{*}\penalty

\displaystyle h(v_{1})=h(v_{2})\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ {\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)=r_{1}\penalty\ \Longrightarrow

\displaystyle∃s_{2},r_{2}∈V^{*}\penalty\ {\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)=s_{2}\,r_{2}\;∧\;h^{*}(r_{1})=h^{*}(r_{2})

But since in this statement the roles of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
can be exchanged,
it follows that

h(v_{1})=h(v_{2})

always entails

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right)}}}\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right)}}}\right|
,
and hence

h^{*}({\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}}}\right))=h^{*}({\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{2}}}\right))
.
This establishes
eq.

9
.
∎

Lemma

9.14
is the crucial stepping stone for the proof of the main theorem of this chapter.

Theorem 9.16

(preservation of the properties ‘eager scope’ and ‘fully backlinked’ under functional bisimulation on
\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}
)
.

Let
G
be a λ-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
and suppose that
h
is a homomorphism from
G
to a term graph
G^{\prime}∈{\mathcal{T}}_{{1},{2}}
. Then the following two statements hold:

(i)

If
G
is fully backlinked, then also
G^{\prime}
is a λ-term-graph, which is fully backlinked.

(ii)

If
G
is eager-scope, then also
G^{\prime}
is a λ-term-graph, which is eager scope.

Proof.

Let
G=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r}}}\right\rangle∈\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}
and
G^{\prime}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V^{\prime},{\mathrm{lab}}^{\prime},{\mathrm{args}}^{\prime},r^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle∈{\mathcal{T}}_{{1},{2}}
, and
h
a homomorphism from
G
to
G^{\prime}
.

For showing statement
item

i
of the theorem, we assume that
G
is fully backlinked. On
G^{\prime}
we define the following abstraction-prefix function:

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}\penalty\ :

\displaystyle V^{\prime}

\displaystyle\textrightarrow(V^{\prime})^{*},

(11)

\displaystyle w^{\prime}

\displaystyle↦h^{*}({\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right))\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{for some $w∈V$ with $h(w)=w^{\prime}$ }

This function is well-defined because: for every
w^{\prime}∈V^{\prime}
there exists a
w∈V
with
w^{\prime}=h(w)
, due to
proposition

2.12

item

iii
; and due to
lemma

9.14
the
{\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{\prime}}}\right)
is the same for all
w∈V
with
w^{\prime}=h(w)
.

For the thus defined abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
it holds:

∀w∈V\penalty\ \;\,h^{*}({\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right))={\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h(w)}}\right)

and hence
{\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
is the homomorphic image of
{\mathrm{P}}
under
h
in the sense of
definition

7.12
.

It remains to show that
{\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
is a correct abstraction-prefix function for
G^{\prime}
, and that
G^{\prime}
is fully backlinked. Both properties follow from statements established earlier by using that
G^{\prime}
and
{\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
are the homomorphic images under
h
of
G
and
{\mathrm{P}}
, respectively: That
{\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
is a correct abstraction-prefix function for
G^{\prime}
follows from
lemma

7.16

item

ii
which entails that the homomorphic image of a correct abstraction-prefix function is correct. And that
G^{\prime}
is fully backlinked follows from
proposition

9.10
, which states that the homomorphic image of a fully backlinked λ-term-graph is again fully backlinked.

In this way we have established statement
item

i
of the theorem.

For showing statement
item

ii
, let
G
be an eager-scope λ-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
. By
proposition

9.7
it follows that
G
is also fully backlinked. Therefore the just established statement
item

i
of the theorem is applicable, and it yields that
G^{\prime}
is a λ-term-graph. Since by
proposition

9.10
also the eager-scope property is preserved by homomorphism, it follows that
G^{\prime}
is eager-scope, too. This establishes statement
item

ii
of the theorem.
∎

Corollary 9.17
.

\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{fbl}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
and
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
are closed under functional bisimulation.

§ 9.18

(backlinks are required)
.

Note that statements analogous to
theorem

9.16
and
corollary

9.17
do not hold for λ-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{1}}
: The classes
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{fbl}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{λ}}
and
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{λ}}
are not closed under functional bisimulation. This is witnessed by the counterexample in the
proof
of
proposition

8.4

item

iii
, which maps an eager-scope (and hence fully-backlinked) λ-term-graph to a term graph that is not a λ-term-graph.

Similarly the statements of
theorem

9.16
and
corollary

9.17
do not carry over to λ-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{0},\mathsf{S}_{1}}
and
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{0},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
that are eager-scope in the sense of
9.9
. This is witnessed by the eager-scope term graphs in the
proof
of
proposition

8.4

item

i
and
item

ii
.

Another direct consequence of
theorem

9.16
is the following corollary. Recall the notations from
definition

2.9
and
2.16
.

Corollary 9.19
.

The following statements holds:

(i)

For every fully backlinked λ-term-graph
G
over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
it holds:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\textbf{{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\textbf{{G}}}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right)^{\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{fbl}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\textbf{{G}}}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right)^{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}

(ii)

For every eager-scope λ-term-graph
G
over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
it holds:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\textbf{{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\textbf{{G}}}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right)^{\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\textbf{{G}}}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right)^{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}

This corollary will be central to proving in
section

10
the complete-lattice property for
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
-successors of (
\mathrel{\sim}
-equivalence classes of) λ-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
and λ-ap-ho-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}
.

Remark 9.20

(an alternative remedy)
.

To recapitulate, we identify subclasses of
\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}
that are closed under functional bisimulation. These subclasses are
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{fbl}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
and the subclass
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
thereof. The reason why some λ-term-graphs are not fully backlinked is revealed by the counterexample of
proposition

8.4

item

iv
: in the scopes of the two topmost λ-abstractions in
G_{1}
, the two subgraphs representing
λ{x}.\,{x}
are ‘dangling’ since the scopes of the topmost λ-abstractions are not closed; therefore the mentioned subgraphs can be shared in the homomorphic image
G_{0}
, thus leading to overlapping scopes.

In the original paper
[
24
, 7.12]
, we explore an approach to mend this blemish of
\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}
by changing its definition. We ensure that
all
λ-term-graphs are fully backlinked. This is facilitated by allowing trailing
\mathsf{S}
-vertices as successors of
\mathsf{0}
-vertices. Thereby scopes that remain open at a variable occurrence are still closed afterwards. This solution is omitted in this thesis.

10
Transfer of the complete-lattice property to λ-ho-term-graphs

§ 10.1

(overview)
.

In this section we establish that sets of
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
-successors of (the isomorphism equivalence class of) a given λ-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
form a complete lattice under the sharing order. For this we use the fact that this is the case for first-order term graphs in general (see
proposition

2.18
), and we apply the results developed so far. Subsequently we transfer this complete-lattice property to the higher-order λ-ap-ho-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}
via the correspondences established in
section

7
.

The following proposition is a specialisation
proposition

2.18
.

Proposition 10.2

(complete-lattice property of
{\mathcal{T}}_{{i},{j}}
)
.

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\textbf{{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}\right),\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right\rangle
is a complete lattice for every term graph
G
over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
with
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
and
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
.

Combining this proposition with the result from
section

9
that the classes of fully backlinked and eager-scope λ-term-graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
are closed under functional bisimulation (
corollary

9.17
) and with
corollary

9.19
we obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 10.3

(complete-lattice property of
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{fbl}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
and
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
)
.

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\textbf{{G}}}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right)^{\smash{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}},\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right\rangle
is a complete lattice for all
G∈\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{fbl}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}∪\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
.

§ 10.4

(transfer to eager-scope higher-order term graphs)
.

Considering this theorem we cannot expect all higher-order term graphs to form a complete lattice, but only those subclasses that correspond to fully backlinked or eager-scope λ-term-graphs. However, the ‘fully-backlinked’ property does not have a natural equivalent on the higher-order term graphs, since it is based on the existence of paths that depend on backlinks departing from delimiter vertices, which the higher-order term graphs do not have. The eager-scope property on the other hand has an obvious counterpart in the higher-order term graphs. Its definition below is analogous to the eager-scope property of λ-term-graphs as described in
9.9
.

Definition 10.5

(eager-scope λ-ap-ho-term-graphs)
.

Let
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
and let
\mathcal{G}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r,{\mathrm{P}}}}}\right\rangle
be a λ-ap-ho-term-graph over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}
. We say that
G
is an
eager-scope λ-term-graph
, or that
G
is
eager-scope
, if:

\displaystyle∀w,v∈V\penalty\ ∀p∈V^{*}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)=p\,v\penalty\ ⇒

\displaystyle∃n∈ℕ\penalty\ ∃w_{1},\dots,w_{n}∈V

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &w\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w_{1}\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}\dots\mathrel{\rightarrowtail}w_{n}\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ w_{n}∈{\mathrm{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)\penalty\ ∧\penalty\ {\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w_{n}}}\right)=p\,v\\
&∧\penalty\ ∀i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,\dots,n-1}}}\right\}\penalty\ \penalty\ p\,v≤{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w_{i}}}\right)\end{aligned}

Or in other words, if for every vertex
w
of
G
with a non-empty abstraction-prefix
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)
ending with
v
there exists a path from
w
via vertices with abstraction-prefixes that start with
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)
(i.e. a path within the scope of
v
) to a variable vertex
w_{n}
that is bound by the abstraction vertex
v
. (Note that if
k=1
, then
v
is directly reachable from
w_{n}
via its backlink.)

By
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\smash{(λ)}}}
we denote the subclass of
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\smash{(λ)}}
that consists of all eager-scope λ-ap-ho-term-graphs.

Proposition 10.6

(uniqueness of eager-scope λ-ap-ho-term-graphs)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{i}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r,{\mathrm{P}}_{i}}}}\right\rangle
with
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
be λ-ap-ho-term-graphs with the same underlying term graph. If
\mathcal{G}_{1}
is eager-scope, then
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{{\mathrm{P}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)}}}\right|≤\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{{\mathrm{P}}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w)}}\right)}}}\right|
for all
w∈V
. If, in addition, also
\mathcal{G}_{2}
is eager-scope, then
{\mathrm{P}}_{1}={\mathrm{P}}_{2}
. Hence eager-scope λ-ap-ho-term-graphs over the same underlying term graph are unique.

Proof sketch.

If a λ-ap-ho-term-graph is not eager-scope, then it contains a vertex
w
with abstraction-prefix
v_{1}\dots v_{n}
from which
v_{n}
is only reachable (if at all) by leaving the scope of
v_{n}
. It can be shown that in this case another abstraction-prefix function with shorter prefixes exists in which
v_{n}
does not occur in the prefix of
w
.
∎

As stated in the proposition below, the eager-scope property for λ-ap-ho-term-graphs and for λ-term-graphs correspond to each other via the correspondence mappings from
proposition

7.18
and
proposition

7.19
.

Proposition 10.7

(
{\mathrm{G}}_{i,j}
and
{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{i,j}
preserve and reflect the eager-scope property)
.

Let
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
, and
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
. The correspondences
{\mathrm{G}}_{i,j}
and
{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{i,j}
between preserve and reflect the eager-scope property i.e., for all
\mathcal{G}∈\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\smash{(λ)}}
, and for all
G∈\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}
it holds:

G
is eager-scope

\displaystyle⇔\penalty\ \text{${\mathrm{G}}_{i,j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{G}}}\right)$ is eager-scope}

{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{i,j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
is eager-scope

\displaystyle⇔\penalty\ \text{$G$ is eager-scope}

Consequently, the restriction of the domains of
{\mathrm{G}}_{i,j}
and
{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{i,j}
to eager-scope term graphs – and also of
{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{i,j}
and
{\mathrm{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}_{i,j}
– are functions of following types:

\displaystyle{{\mathrm{G}}_{i,j}}\!\mid_{\,\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\smash{(λ)}}}}

\displaystyle:\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\smash{(λ)}}}\textrightarrow\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}}

\displaystyle{{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{i,j}}\!\mid_{\,\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}}}

\displaystyle:\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}}\textrightarrow\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\smash{(λ)}}}

\displaystyle{{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{i,j}}\!\mid_{\,\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}}_{{i}}^{\smash{\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}}}

\displaystyle:\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}}_{{i}}^{\smash{\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}}\textrightarrow\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}}_{{i},{j}}^{\smash{\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\!}

\displaystyle{{\mathrm{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}_{i,j}}\!\mid_{\,\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}}_{{i},{j}}^{\smash{\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\!}}

\displaystyle:\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}}_{{i},{j}}^{\smash{\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\!}\textrightarrow\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}}_{{i}}^{\smash{\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}}

Furthermore, we can specialise
theorem

7.20
concerning the correspondences on the isomorphism equivalence classes (in particular
item

i
and
item

iii
) as follows. Recall
2.16
.

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
, and
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
and let
\mathcal{G}∈\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\smash{(λ)}}
be a λ-ap-ho-term-graph. Then

\displaystyle{{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{i,j}}\!\mid_{\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\penalty\ :\penalty\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}\right)\penalty\ \textrightarrow\penalty\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{i,j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}\right)}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right)^{\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}}

\displaystyle{{\mathrm{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}_{i,j}}\!\mid_{\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{i,j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}\right)}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}}}\right)^{\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ :\penalty\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{i,j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}\right)}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right)^{\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}}\penalty\ \textrightarrow\penalty\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}\right)

and it holds that
{{\mathrm{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}_{i,j}}\!\mid_{\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{i,j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}\right)}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}}}\right)^{\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}}}
is a left-inverse of
{{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{i,j}}\!\mid_{\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}}\right)}
:

{{\mathrm{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}_{i,j}}\!\mid_{\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{i,j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}\right)}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}}}\right)^{\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}}}\penalty\ ∘\penalty\ {{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{i,j}}\!\mid_{\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}}\right)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ {\mathrm{id}}_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}}\right)}

The final tool for transfering the complete-lattice property to higher-order term graphs is the following general lemma about partial orders. It states that the complete-lattice property of partial orders is reflected by order homomorphisms with left-inverses.

Lemma 10.9

(reflection of the complete-lattice property under order homomorphisms with left-inverses)
.

Let
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{A,≤_{A}}}}\right\rangle
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{B,≤_{B}}}}\right\rangle
be partial orders. Suppose that
h:A\textrightarrow B
, and
i:B\textrightarrow A
are order homomorphisms such that
i
is a left-inverse of
h
, i.e.:
{i}∘{h}={\mathrm{id}}_{A}
. Then if
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{B,≤_{B}}}}\right\rangle
is a complete lattice, then so is
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{A,≤_{A}}}}\right\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
S\subseteq A
be arbitrary. We have to show the existence of a least upper bound (l.u.b.)
\bigsqcup{S}
and a greatest lower bound (g.l.b.)
\bigsqcap{S}
of
S
in
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{A,≤_{A}}}}\right\rangle
. We only show the existence of the l.u.b., because the argument for the g.l.b. is analogous. Since
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{B,≤_{B}}}}\right\rangle
is a complete lattice, we know that a l.u.b.
\bigsqcup{h(S)}
exists. We will show that
\bigsqcup{S}=i(\bigsqcup{h(S)})
.

i(\bigsqcup{h(S)})
is an upper bound of
S
: Let
s∈S
be arbitrary. Then
h(s)∈h(S)
, and
h(s)≤_{B}\bigsqcup{h(S)}
. Since
i
is an order homomorphism, and a left-inverse of
h
, it follows that
s=i(h(s))≤_{A}i(\bigsqcup{h(S)})
.

i(\bigsqcup{h(S)})
is less or equal to all upper bounds of
S
: Let
u∈A
be an upper bound of
S
. As
h
is an order homomorphism, it follows that
h(u)
is an upper bound of
h(S)
. Consequently,
\bigsqcup{h(S)}≤_{B}h(u)
. Again, since
i
is an order homomorphism, and a left-inverse of
h
, this entails
i(\bigsqcup{h(S)})≤_{A}i(h(u))=u
.
∎

Now we can formulate and prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 10.10
.

For every
\mathcal{G}∈\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{1}}
it holds that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}\right),\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right\rangle
is a complete lattice.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}∈\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{1}}
Then by
proposition

10.7
it follows that
{\mathrm{G}}_{1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{G}}}\right)∈\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
and also that
{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}\right)∈\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}}_{{1},{2}}^{\smash{\boldsymbol{(λ)}}}\!}
. Now
theorem

10.3
yields that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}\right)}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right)^{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
w.r.t.
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
is a complete lattice. Furthermore note that
{{\mathrm{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}_{1,2}}\!\mid_{\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}\right)}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}}}\right)^{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}}
is a left-inverse of
{{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{1,2}}\!\mid_{\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}}\right)}
due to
lemma

10.8
. Hence
lemma

10.9
can be used to show that the complete-lattice property of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}}\right)}{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right)^{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
w.r.t.
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}
is reflected by
{{\mathrm{\textbf{G}}}_{1,2}}\!\mid_{\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.3014pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.3014pt\hbox{\kern-0.3014pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.3014pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.3014pt}}}}\right)}
, yielding that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}\right),\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}}}\right\rangle
is a complete lattice.
∎

11
Summary

§ 11.1

(summary)
.

We defined higher-order term graph representations for strongly regular λ-terms:

∘

λ-ho-term-graphs
\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{i}
, an adaptation of Blom’s ‘higher-order term graphs’
[
8
]
, which possess a scope function that maps every abstraction vertex
v
to the set of vertices that are in the scope of
v
.

∘

λ-ap-ho-term-graphs
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\smash{(λ)}}
, which instead of a scope function carry an abstraction-prefix function that assigns to every vertex information about the scoping structure. Abstraction prefixes are closely related to the notion of ‘generated subterms’ for λ-terms (
definition

4.39
). The correctness conditions here are simpler and more intuitive than for λ-ho-term-graphs.

These classes are defined for
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
, according to whether variable occurrences have backlinks
to abstractions or not.
Our main statements about these classes are:

∘

a bijective correspondence between
\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{i}
and
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\smash{(λ)}}
via mappings
{\mathrm{A}}_{i}
and
{\mathrm{B}}_{i}
that preserve and reflect the sharing order (
theorem

5.11
);

∘

the naive approach to implementing functional bisimulation on theses classes (ignoring all scoping information and using only the underlying first-order term graphs) fails (
proposition

6.6
).

The latter was the motivation to consider first-order term graphs with scope delimiters:

∘

λ-term-graphs
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{λ}
(with
i∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{0,1}}}\right\}
for whether there are variable backlinks and
j∈\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{1,2}}}\right\}
for whether there are delimiter backlinks), which are plain first-order term graphs, but which require as a correctness condition the existence of an abstraction-prefix function.

The most important results linking these classes with λ-ap-ho-term-graphs are:

∘

an ‘almost bijective’ correspondence between
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\smash{(λ)}}
via mappings
{\mathrm{G}}_{i,j}
and
{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{i,j}
that preserve and reflect the sharing order (
theorem

7.20
);

∘

the subclass
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
of eager-scope λ-term-graphs is closed under functional bisimulation (
corollary

9.17
).

I
I
I
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{1}}
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\smash{(λ)}}}
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
I
I
I
{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
{\mathrm{G}}_{1,2}
{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{1,2}
{\mathrm{B}}_{1}
G
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
G
\mathcal{G}_{0}
\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\prime}
G_{0}
{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
{\mathrm{G}}_{1,2}
{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{1,2}
{\mathrm{B}}_{1}

Figure 6:
The correspondences (top) permit an implementation of functional bisimulation on higher-order term graphs using functional bisimulation on first-order term graphs (bottom).

The correspondences together with the closedness result allow us to handle functional bisimulation between eager-scope higher-order term graphs in a straightforward manner by implementing them via functional bisimulation between first-order term graphs as shown in
fig.

6
.

§ 11.2

(outlook: maximal sharing)
.

The findings from this chapter are a toehold for the concept of maximal sharing, which we develop in the following chapter. In particular the complete-property of the classes
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{1}}
,
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}}
, and
{\mathcal{T}}_{{1},{2}}
implies that for every graph in these classes there is a unique maximally compact version of that element – its bisimulation collapse – which we call its maximally shared form. For an eager λ-ho-term-graph
G
the maximally shared form can be computed as:

{{{\text{\small\textbar}}\hskip-3.14302pt\downarrow}_{\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{H}^{λ}_{1}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathcal{G}}}}\right)=({\mathrm{B}}_{1}∘{{{\mathrm{\mathcal{G}}}_{1,2}}∘{{{{\text{\small\textbar}}\hskip-3.14302pt\downarrow}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{{1},{2}}}}∘{{{\mathrm{G}}_{1,2}}∘{{\mathrm{A}}_{1}}}}})(\mathcal{G})

For implementing
{{\text{\small\textbar}}\hskip-3.14302pt\downarrow}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{{1},{2}}}
fast algorithms are available.

Analogous to
remark

9.20
this can be generalised to term graphs without eager scope-closure. For our intent of getting a grip on maximal sharing in
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
, however, only eager scope-closure is practically relevant, because it facilitates the highest degree of sharing.

Chapter 3
Maximal Sharing in
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}

1
Overview

§ 1.0

(teaser)
.

Can we transform
λ{f}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{({f}\;{r})}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r}}
into the more efficient form
λ{f}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r}}
and can we prove that these terms are equivalent?

§ 1.1

(subject matter)
.

Increasing sharing in programs is generally desirable. It results in more compact code and avoids duplication of reduction work at run-time, thereby speeding up execution. We show how a maximal degree of sharing can be obtained for programs expressed as terms in the λ-calculus with
\mathsf{letrec}
. We introduce a notion of ‘maximal compactness’ for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms among all terms with the same unfolding. We translate
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms into a term graph representation which respects the unfolding semantics in the sense that bisimilarity preserves and reflects unfolding equivalence on the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. Compactness of the term graphs can then be compared and increased by means of functional bisimulation.

§ 1.2

(methods and formalisms)
.

We lean heavily on the results from the previous chapters. While in
chapter

1
we completely focused on the λ-terms expressed by
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms, in
chapter

2
we exclusively investigated possible suitable graph representations for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. In this chapter we put things together and connect the graph representations to the unfolding semantics. We provide a translation into higher-order and first-order term graphs and we show that it respects the unfolding semantics in the sense that bisimulation preserves and reflects the unfolding semantics.

§ 1.3

(results)
.

We obtain practical and efficient methods for the following two problems: transforming a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term into a maximally compact form; and deciding whether two
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms have the same unfolding.

The transformation of a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
into maximally compact form
L_{0}
proceeds in three steps:

(i)

translate
L
into a term graph
G=\llbracket L\rrbracket
;

(ii)

compute the maximally shared form of
G
as its bisimulation collapse
G_{0}
;

(iii)

read back a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L_{0}
from
G_{0}
with the property
\llbracket L_{0}\rrbracket=G_{0}
.

The transformation is sound in the sense that
L_{0}
and
L
have the same λ-term as their unfolding.

The procedure for deciding whether two given
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are unfolding-equivalent computes their term graph interpretations
\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket
,
and checks whether these term graphs are bisimilar.

We also provide an implementation.

2
Preliminaries

Definition 2.1

(bisimulation collapse)
.

Let
G=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{V,{\mathrm{lab}},{\mathrm{args}},r}}}\right\rangle
be a term graph. A
bisimulation collapse
of
G
is a maximal element in the class
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{G^{\prime}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G^{\prime}}}}\right\}
up to
\mathrel{\sim}
, that is, a term graph
G^{\prime}_{0}
with
G\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G^{\prime}_{0}
such that if
G^{\prime}_{0}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}G^{\prime\prime}_{0}

for some term graph
G^{\prime\prime}_{0}
, then
G^{\prime\prime}_{0}\mathrel{\sim}G^{\prime}_{0}

. Every two bisimulation collapses of
G
are isomorphic. This justifies the common abbreviation of saying that ‘the bisimulation collapse’ of
G
is unique up to isomorphism.

3
Introduction

§ 3.1

(sharing by
\mathsf{letrec}
)
.

Explicit sharing in pure functional programming languages is typically expressed by means of the
\mathsf{letrec}
-construct, which facilitates cyclic definitions (see also
1.2
).
For the programmer the
\mathsf{letrec}
-construct offers the possibility to write a program more compactly by utilising subterm sharing.
\mathsf{letrec}
-expressions bind subterms to variables; these variables then denote occurrences of the respective subterms and can be used anywhere inside of the
\mathsf{letrec}
-expression (also recursively).
In this way, instead of repeating a subterm multiple times,
a single definition can be given which is then referenced from multiple positions.

Example 3.2

(horizontal sharing)
.

Consider the λ-term
{(λ{x}.\,{x})}\;{(λ{x}.\,{x})}
with two occurrences of the subterm
λ{x}.\,{x}
. These occurrences can be shared as done in the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
\mathsf{let}\;{id=λ{x}.\,{x}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{id}\;{id}}
. Obviously it holds:
\llbracket\mathsf{let}\;{id=λ{x}.\,{x}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{id}\;{id}}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}={(λ{x}.\,{x})}\;{(λ{x}.\,{x})}

As
\mathsf{let}
-expressions permit cyclic definitions, sharing can not only occur horizontally but also vertically.

Example 3.3

(vertical sharing)
.

Consider the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms
L
and
P
and the λ-term
M
from
example

2.5
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} L&:=λ{f}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{r}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r}}\\
P&:=λ{f}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{r={f}\;{({f}\;{r})}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{r}}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle:=λ{f}.\,{{f}\;{({f}\;{(\dots)})}}

Both
L
and
P
have
M
as their infinite unfolding:
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=M
. Note that
L
represents
M
in a more compact way than
P
. It is intuitively clear that there is no
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term that represents
M
more compactly than
L
. So
L
can be called a ‘maximally shared form’ of
P
(and of
M
).

Remark 3.4

(twisted sharing)
.

Besides horizontal and vertical sharing also a hybrid form of sharing can occur, a sort of superimposition of both kinds, which is called ‘twisted sharing’ in
[
8
, Definition 4.1.7]
.

§ 3.5

(dynamic vs. static sharing)
.

In the context of functional programming ‘sharing’ can refer to two different – albeit related – notions. Here, we call them
static
and
dynamic
sharing, while in the literature about static and dynamic sharing, this distinction is usually not made explicitly.

Static sharing
simply refers to a trait of some (or graph) languages in which a term (graph) with multiple occurrences of the same subterm (subgraph) can also be written more compactly, where the subterm (subgraph) is written out only once and referenced from multiple points. Static sharing is possible in most programing languages and most optimising compilers perform common subexpression elimination at compile time to increase sharing.
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
and the graph formalisms from the previous chapter with their unfolding semantics are of course typical examples of languages with static sharing. This thesis focuses (almost exclusively) on static sharing.

Dynamic sharing
refers to the degree of ‘static‘ sharing an evaluator is able to maintain during evaluation. This is an important issue because unsharing is an integral part of evaluation. A shared function typically behaves differently in different contexts (i.e. with different input), therefore it is impossible to evaluate the entire function only once if the result is required for two different inputs. However some portion of the computation may very well be shared. The degree of sharing of an evaluator refers to how clever it performs unsharing, and therefore how much of the computation can be shared. Terms as ‘call-by-need’, ‘full laziness’
[
53
]
, ‘complete laziness’, and ‘optimal evaluation’
[
39
,
4
]
all refer to dynamic sharing. An overview can be found in
[
52
, 3.4]
. While our maximal sharing is a priori a method for increasing static, not dynamic, sharing, we do envisage applying it as part of an evaluator, collapsing the program’s graph representation periodically at run-time (see
section

10
).

§ 3.6

(‘maximal sharing’ in ATERM)
.

The term ‘maximal sharing’ stems from work on the ATERM library
[
9
]
. It describes a technique for minimising memory usage when representing a set of terms in a first-order term rewrite system (TRS). The terms are kept in an aggregate directed acyclic graph by which their syntax trees are shared as much as possible. Thereby terms are created only if they are entirely new; otherwise they are referenced by pointers to roots of sub-dags. Our use of the expression ‘maximal sharing’ is inspired by that work, but our results generalise that approach in the following ways:

∘

Instead of first-order terms we consider terms in higher-order languages.

∘

Since
\mathsf{letrec}
can express cyclic sharing, we interpret terms as cyclic graphs instead of just dags.

∘

We increase sharing by bisimulation collapse instead of by identifying isomorphic sub-dags.

§ 3.7

(common subexpression elimination)
.

ATERM only checks for equality of subexpressions. Therefore it only introduces horizontal sharing (for a definition see
[
8
]
) and implements a form of
common subexpression elimination (CSE)

[
33
, 14.7.2]
. Our approach is stronger than CSE: while
example

3.2
can be handled by CSE, this is not the case for
example

3.3
. In contrast to CSE, our approach increases also vertical and twisted sharing (see
remark

3.4
).

4
Overview: Methods and Formalisms

Here we will quickly introduce mathematical symbols for the central formalisms (which are later properly defined) so that we can sketch a complete picture of the algorithms we develop in this chapter.

§ 4.1

(graph formalisms)
.

The methods that we introduce in this chapter rely heavily on the term graph formalisms developed in
chapter

2
. As a main result of
chapter

2
we have identified suitable classes of term graphs for representing regular λ-terms. In particular we will use eager-scope λ-ap-ho-term-graphs
1
1

1

Note that λ-ho-term-graphs would be just as suitable due to
theorem

5.11
with variable backlinks and eager-scope λ-term-graphs with variable and scope-delimiter backlinks, however over a slightly modified signature which includes black holes. In this chapter we will use an abbreviated notation for these classes. λ-ap-ho-term-graphs (originally
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\smash{(λ)}}
) amended with black holes are now denoted by
H
. λ-term-graphs (originally
\mathcal{T}_{1,2}^{λ}
) amended with black holes are now denoted by
T
.

§ 4.2

(graph semantics)
.

We provide a higher-order graph semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
for interpreting
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as eager-scope λ-ap-ho-term-graphs. Together with the correspondence from
theorem

7.20
it induces a first-order graph semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
. Specifically we use the mapping
{\mathrm{G}}_{1,2}
from λ-ap-ho-term-graphs to λ-term-graphs from
proposition

7.18
, which (amended to handling black holes) we call
{\mathrm{\mathcal{HT}}}
in this chapter.

(readback)

up to graph isomorphism.
