§ 4.5

(correctness and practicality)
.

The correctness of these methods hinges on the fact that the translation and the readback satisfy the following properties:

(P1)

λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms
L
and
P
have the same infinite unfolding if and only if the term graphs
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
and
\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
are bisimilar.

(P2)

The class
T
of λ-term-graphs is closed under functional bisimulation.

(P3)

The readback
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}
is a right inverse of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
up to isomorphism
\mathrel{\sim}
, that is, for all term graphs
G∈\mathcal{T}
it holds:
\llbracket{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}\mathrel{\sim}G
.

Furthermore, practicality of these methods depends on the property:

(P4)

Translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
and readback
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}
are efficiently computable.

§ 4.6

(applications)
.

Our approach holds promise for a number of practical applications:

∘

Increasing the efficiency of the execution of programs by transforming them into their maximally shared form at compile-time.

∘

Increasing the efficiency of the execution of programs by periodically compactifying the program at run time.

∘

Improving systems for recognising program equivalence.

∘

Providing feedback to the programmer, along the lines: ‘This code has identical fragments and can be written more compactly.’

These and a number of other potential applications are discussed in more detail in
section

10
.

5
Interpretion of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as λ-ap-ho-term-graphs

§ 5.1

(natural first-order semantics)
.

First we will quickly look at a naive translation of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms into first-order term graphs over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}
which we call the
natural first-order semantics
of
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
and see that it does not work. We will not give a formal definition of that translation but only the following informal description: consider the syntax tree of a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term and resolve variable occurrences as
\mathsf{0}
-vertices with variable backlinks; furthermore resolve occurrences of function variables as an edge to the root of the corresponding function’s subgraph in the term graph translation.

Example 5.2

(incorrectness of the natural first-order semantics)
.

For the terms
L
,
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
below, it holds that
\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}≠\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
:

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle\penalty\displaystyle=\penalty\ \mathsf{let}\;{f=λ{x}.\,{{(λ{y}.\,{{f}\;{y}})}\;{x}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\penalty\displaystyle=\penalty\ \mathsf{let}\;{f=λ{x}.\,{{f}\;{x}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle\penalty\displaystyle=\penalty\ \mathsf{let}\;{f=λ{x}.\,{{(λ{y}.\,{{f}\;{x}})}\;{x}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}

Their term graph interpretation under the natural first-order semantics
G_{1}
,
G
, and
G_{2}
are however all bisimilar:

\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}

\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}

G_{1}

G

G_{2}

This violates
item

1
as under this semantics bisimilarity does not guarantee unfolding equivalence. Therefore the natural first-order semantics is incorrect.

Let us consider the translation of the above example into λ-ap-ho-term-graphs, before we give a formal definition thereof.

Example 5.3

(the λ-ap-ho-term-graphs of the terms in
example

5.2
)
.

\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}

\not{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}

\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}

\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}

\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}

We see that the abstraction prefixes prevent
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
to be bisimilar, and that
item

1
is satisfied in this instance.

§ 5.4

(cf. bisimulation on the underlying term graphs)
.

The shortcoming of the natural first-order semantics is reflected in
proposition

6.5
which states that higher-order term graphs are not closed under functional bisimulation on their underlying term graphs.

§ 5.5

(λ-ap-ho-term-graphs with black holes)
.

Before we finally define the higher-order term graph semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms, we first have to slightly adapt the graph formalism. The reason is that in
chapter

2
we introduced higher-order term graphs as a representation for strongly regular λ-terms, while here we want them to represent
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. We know these two classes to coincide (
theorem

6.28
), but only if we exclude meaningless
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms (see
3.7
). Here, however, we want to be able to handle all
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. Therefore in this chapter we need to consider an amended definition of λ-ap-ho-term-graphs over an extended signature
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}
which includes black hole vertices.

Definition 5.6

(signature for λ-ap-ho-term-graphs with black holes)
.

By
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}
we denote the signature
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1}}
extended by a black-hole symbol
\bullet
with arity
0
, i.e.
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{@,λ,\mathsf{0},\bullet}}}\right\}
with
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(@}}\right)=2
,
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)=1
,
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)=1
, and
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bullet}}\right)=0
.

We also have to amend the correctness conditions
definition

5.3
for the abstraction-prefix function by a black-hole case.

Definition 5.7

(abstraction-prefix function for
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}
-term-graphs)
.

As
definition

5.3
but over signature
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}
and with the following condition added:

\displaystyle\penalty\ {\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bullet}}\right)=\epsilon

(black hole)

Definition 5.8

(λ-ap-ho-term-graph with black holes)
.

As
definition

5.4
, but over signature
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}
and using
definition

5.7
.

Terminology 5.9

(λ-ap-ho-term-graph := λ-ap-ho-term-graph with black holes)
.

Henceforth, if we speak of λ-ap-ho-term-graphs, we refer to this specific variant with black holes and variable backlinks. All the relevant properties that hold for ordinary λ-ap-ho-term-graphs carry over; accounting for the black holes is generally easy.

Figure 3:
Translation rules
R
for interpreting
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as λ-ap-ho-term-graphs. See
section

5
for explanations.

§ 5.10

(translating
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms into λ-ap-ho-term-graphs)
.

In order to interpret a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
as a λ-ap-ho-term-graph, the translation rules
R
from
fig.

3
are applied to a ‘translation box’

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

. It contains
L
furnished with a prefix consisting of a dummy variable
*
annotated with an empty set
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}
of function variables. The translation process proceeds by induction on the syntactical structure
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-expression. Ultimately, a term graph
G
over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}
is produced, together with a correct abstraction-prefix function for
G
.

For reading the rules
R
in
fig.

3
correctly, take notice of the explanations below. For illustration of their application, please refer to
chapter

4
where several
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms are translated into λ-ap-ho-term-graphs.

∘

A translation box

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}}}}\right)\;{L}

contains a prefixed, partially decomposed
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
. The prefix contains a vector
\vec{p}
of annotated λ-abstractions that have already been translated and whose scope typically extends into
L
. Every variable in the prefix is annotated with a set of function variables that are defined at its level. There is a special dummy variable
*
as the very first entry of the prefix that carries function variables for top-level function definitions, i.e. definitions that do not reside under any enclosing λ-abstraction. The λ-rule strips off an abstraction from the body of the expression, and pushes the abstraction variable into the prefix, which initially contains an empty set of function variables.

∘

Names of abstraction vertices are indicated to the right, and abstraction-prefixes to the left of the created vertices. In the following example the λ-abstraction vertex
v
has the abstraction-prefix
\vec{p}
:
λ
\scriptstyle v
\scriptstyle(\vec{p})

∘

In order to refer to the vertices in the prefix we use the following notation:
{\mathrm{vs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{p}}}\right)=v_{1}\,\dots\,v_{n}
given that
\vec{p}=\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\vec{f}_{0}}}}\right\}\,x_{1}^{v_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\vec{f}_{1}}}}\right\}\,\dots\,x_{n}^{v_{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\vec{f}_{n}}}}\right\}
.

∘

Vertices drawn with dashed lines have been created earlier during the translation, and are referenced by new edges in the current translation step.

∘

{\mathrm{FV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L}}\right)
is the set of free variables in
L
.

∘

The
\mathsf{let}
-rule for translating
\mathsf{let}
-expressions creates a box for the body as well as for each of its function definitions. For each function definition an
indirection vertex
is created. These vertices guarantee the well-definedness of the process when it translates meaningless bindings such as
f=f
, or
g=h,\,h=g
, which would otherwise give rise to loops without vertices; the result would not be a term graph. Indirection vertices are eliminated by an erasure process at the end: Every indirection vertex that does not point to itself is removed, redirecting all incoming edges to its successor vertex. Finally every loop on a single indirection vertex is replaced by a
black hole
vertex with an empty abstraction prefix that represents a meaningless binding.

∘

The
\mathsf{let}
-rule is non-deterministic as there is some freedom on choosing the prefix-lengths used for the translation of each function definition. Say, a function
f
does not use the rightmost variable
x
in the current abstraction prefix. Then this freedom allows the translation to either remove
x
from the prefix within the translation of
f
’s definition, or alternatively at every use site of
f
outside of
f
’s translation. This freedom is limited by the scoping condition at the bottom of the rule: function definitions may only depend on variables and functions that occur in their respective prefix. In this context also note, that the choice of the prefix-lengths used for some function
f
also determines the position of
f
within the prefixes used in the translation of the other functions (and the body of the
\mathsf{let}
-expression).

Definition 5.11

(
R
-generated term graphs)
.

We say that a term graph
G
over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}
and an abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P}}
is
R
-generated from
a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
if
G
and
{\mathrm{P}}
are obtained by applying the rules
R
from
fig.

3
to

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

.

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,x^{v}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

λ

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}}}}\right)\;{λ{x}.\,{L}}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}}}}\right)\;{L_{0}}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}}}}\right)\;{L_{1}}

@

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}}}}\right)\;{{L_{0}}\;{L_{1}}}

\mathrm{rec}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,x^{v}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\dots,f^{w},\dots}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{f}

\mathsf{0}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({x_{0}^{v_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\vec{f}_{0}}}}\right\}\dots x_{n}^{v_{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\vec{f}_{n}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{x_{n}}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}}}}\right)\;{L}

\mathsf{S}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{\small(if $x∉{\mathrm{FV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L}}\right)$ and $f_{i}∉{\mathrm{FV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L}}\right)$)}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,x^{v}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f_{1}^{v_{1}},\dots,f_{n}^{v_{n}}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({x_{0}^{v_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\vec{f}^{\prime}_{0}}}}\right\}\dots x_{l_{0}}^{v_{l_{0}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\vec{f}^{\prime}_{l_{0}}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L_{0}}

\dots

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({x_{0}^{v_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\vec{f}^{\prime}_{0}}}}\right\}\dots x_{l_{k}}^{v_{l_{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\vec{f}^{\prime}_{l_{k}}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L_{k}}

\mathsf{let}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({x_{0}^{v_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\vec{f}_{0}}}}\right\}\dots x_{n}^{v_{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\vec{f}_{n}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L_{0}}}

with
l_{0}=n
and
l_{1},\dots,l_{k}
,
\vec{f}^{\prime}_{0},\dots,\vec{f}^{\prime}_{n}
as in rule
\mathsf{let}
in
fig.

3

Figure 4:
Alternative formulation as inference rules of the translation rules in
fig.

3
for the interpretation of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as λ-ap-ho-term-graphs.

§ 5.12

(inference rule formulation of
R
)
.

See also
fig.

4
for inference rules that correspond to the deconstruction of prefixed terms in
R
.

Proposition 5.13

(λ-ap-ho-term-graph translation of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms)
.

Let
L
be a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term. Suppose that a term graph
G
over
\Sigma^{λ}_{\bullet}
, and an abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P}}
are
R
-generated from
L
. Then
{\mathrm{P}}
is a correct abstraction-prefix function for
G
, and consequently,
G
and
{\mathrm{P}}
together form a λ-ap-ho-term-graph.

§ 5.14

(non-determinism in
R
)
.

There are two sources of non-determinism in this translation: The
\mathsf{S}
-rule for shortening prefixes can be applicable at the same time as other rules. And the
\mathsf{let}
-rule does not fix the lengths
l_{1},\dots,l_{k}
of the abstraction prefixes used in the translations of the function definitions of the
\mathsf{let}
-expression. Neither kind of non-determinism affects the underlying term graph that is produced, but induces different abstraction-prefix functions, and thus different λ-ap-ho-term-graphs.

1
Interpretation as eager-scope λ-ap-ho-term-graphs

§ 5.15

(eager-scope closure induces a higher degree of sharing)
.

Of the different translations due to
5.14
we are most interested in the one using the shortest possible abstraction prefixes, thus the translation yielding eager-scope λ-ap-ho-term-graphs (
definition

10.5
). The reason for this choice is illustrated in
fig.

5
: eager-scope closure allows for more sharing.

\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}

\not{\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}

Figure 5:
Translation of
λ{a}.\,{{(λ{b}.\,{λ{c}.\,{{a}\;{c}}})}\;{(λ{d}.\,{{a}\;{d}})}}
with eager scope-closure (left), and with lazy scope-closure (right). While on the left four vertices can be shared, on the right only a single variable occurrence can be shared.

Also, we will call a
translation process
‘eager-scope’ if it resolves the non-determinism in
R
in such a way that it always yields eager-scope λ-ap-ho-term-graphs. In order to obtain an eager-scope translation we have to consider the following aspects.

§ 5.16

(garbage removal)
.

In the presence of garbage – unused function bindings – a translation process cannot be eager-scope. Consider the term
λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=x}\;\mathsf{in}\;{y}}}
. The variable
x
occurs solely in the unused binding
f=x
, which prevents the application of the
\mathsf{S}
-rule, and hence the closure of the scope of
λ{x}
, directly below
λ{x}
. Therefore we henceforth assume that
all unused function bindings are removed
prior to applying the rules
R
.

§ 5.17

(short enough prefix lengths in the
\mathsf{let}
-rule)
.

For obtaining an eager-scope translation we will stipulate that the
\mathsf{S}
-rule is applied eagerly, i.e. it is given precedence over the other rules. This is clearly necessary for keeping the abstraction prefixes minimal. But how do we choose the prefix lengths
l_{1},\dots,l_{k}
in the
\mathsf{let}
-rule? The prefix lengths
l_{i}
determine at which position a binding
f_{i}=L_{i}
is inserted into the abstraction prefixes. Therefore
l_{i}
may not be chosen too short; otherwise a function
f
depending on a function
g
may end up to the right of g, and hence may be removed from the prefix by the
\mathsf{S}
-rule prematurely, preventing completion of the translation. Yet simply choosing
l_{i}=n
may prevent scopes from being minimal. For example, when translating the term
λ{a}.\,{λ{b}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=a}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{{{a}\;{a}}\;{({f}\;{a})}}\;{b}}}}
, it is crucial to allow shorter prefixes for the binding than for the body. This is illustrated in
fig.

6
where the graph on the left does not have eager scope-closure even if the
\mathsf{S}
-rule is applied eagerly. Consequently the opportunity for sharing the lower application vertices is lost.

Figure 6:
Translation of
λ{a}.\,{λ{b}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=a}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{{{a}\;{a}}\;{({f}\;{a})}}\;{b}}}}
with equal (left) and with minimal prefix lengths (right) in the
\mathsf{let}
-rule. See also
.

§ 5.18

(required variable analysis)
.

For choosing the prefixes in the
\mathsf{let}
-rule correctly, the translation process must know for each function binding which λ-variable are ‘required’ on the right-hand side of its definition. For this we use an analysis obtaining the required variables for positions in a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term as employed by algorithms for λ-lifting
[
32
,
14
]
. The term ‘required variables’ was coined by Morazán and Schultz
[
42
]
. A λ-variable
x
is called
required at a position
p
in a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
if
x
is bound by an abstraction above
p
, and has a free occurrence in the complete unfolding of
L
below
p
(also function variables from above
p
are unfolded). The required variables at position
p
in
L
can be computed as those λ-variable with free occurrences that are reachable from
p
by a downwards traversal with the stipulations: on encountering a
\mathsf{let}
-expression the body is entered; when encountering a function variable the traversal continues at the right-hand side of the corresponding function binding (even if it is defined above
p
).

With the result of the required variable analysis at hand, we now define properties of the translation process that can guarantee that the resulting λ-ap-ho-term-graph is eager-scope.

Definition 5.19

(eager-scope and minimal-prefix generated)
.

Let
L
be a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term, and let
G
be a λ-ap-ho-term-graph.

We say that
G
is
eager-scope

R
-generated from
L
if
G
is
R
-generated from
L
by a translation process with the following property: for every translation box reached during the process with label
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({λ{\vec{p}\,x^{v}[f]}}}}\right)\;{P}
, where
P
is a subterm of
L
at position
q
, it holds that if
x
is not a required variable at
q
in
L
, then in the next translation step performed to this box either one of the rules
f
or
\mathsf{let}
is applied, or the prefix is shortened by the
\mathsf{S}
-rule.

We say that
G
is
R
-generated
with minimal prefixes
from
L
if
G
is
R
-generated from
L
by a translation process in which minimal prefix lengths are achieved by giving applications of the
\mathsf{S}
-rule precedence over applications of all other rules, and by always choosing prefixes minimally in applications of the
\mathsf{let}
-rule.

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
G
be a λ-ap-ho-term-graph that is
R
-generated from a garbage-free
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
. The following statements hold:

(i)

If
G
is eager-scope
R
-generated from
L
, then
G
is eager-scope.

(ii)

If
G
is
R
-generated with minimal prefixes from
L
, then
G
is eager-scope
R
-generated from
L
, hence by
item

i

G
is eager-scope.

Definition 5.21

(higher-order term graph semantics)
.

The semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as λ-ap-ho-term-graphs is defined as
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}:{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)\textrightarrow\mathcal{H}
,
L↦\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}:=\,
the λ-ap-ho-term-graph that is
R
-generated with minimal prefixes from a garbage-free version of
L
.

Proposition 5.22
.

For every
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
,
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
is eager-scope.

In preparation of proving
item

1
in
section

6
, we establish that the semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
is correct with respect to the unfolding semantics of
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
.

Theorem 5.23

(correctness of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
)
.

\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
if and only if
\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
, for all
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
.

Sketch of Proof.

For the proof we introduce a class of λ-ap-ho-term-graphs that have tree form, i.e. they only contain variable backlinks but no other backlinks. We denote that class by
\mathcal{H}_{T}\subset\mathcal{H}
. Every
\mathcal{G}∈\mathcal{H}
has a unique ‘tree unfolding’
{\mathrm{Tree}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{G}}}\right)∈\mathcal{H}_{T}
. We make use of the following statements. For all
L,L_{1},L_{2}∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)
,
M,M_{1},M_{2}∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}\right)
,
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{2}∈\mathcal{H}
, and
T,T_{1},T_{2}∈\mathcal{H}_{T}
it can be shown that:

\displaystyle L_{1}\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}L_{2}\penalty\ ⇒\penalty\ \llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}

(1)

\displaystyle L\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-6.28612pt\textrightarrow}_{\triangledown}M\penalty\ \text{(hence $\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=M$)}\penalty\ ⇒\penalty\ \llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}

(2)

\displaystyle\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}∈\mathcal{H}_{T}

(3)

\displaystyle\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrel{\sim}\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}\penalty\ ⇒\penalty\ M_{1}=M_{2}

(4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}{\mathrm{Tree}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{G}}}\right)

(5)

\displaystyle T_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}T_{2}\penalty\ ⇒\penalty\ T_{1}\mathrel{\sim}T_{2}

(6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}\mathcal{G}_{2}\penalty\ ⇒\penalty\ {\mathrm{Tree}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{G}_{1}}}\right)\mathrel{\sim}{\mathrm{Tree}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{G}_{2}}}\right)

(7)

We can use
eq.

1
for proving
eq.

2
, and we can use
eq.

5
with
eq.

6
for proving
eq.

7
. Now for proving the theorem, let
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
be arbitrary
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms.

For “⇒”, suppose
\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
. Let
M
be the infinite unfolding of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, i.e.,
\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}=M=\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
. Then by
eq.

2
it follows
\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
, and hence
\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
.

For “⇐”, suppose
\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
. Then by
eq.

7
it follows that
{\mathrm{Tree}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}}}\right)\mathrel{\sim}{\mathrm{Tree}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}}}\right)
. Let
M_{1},M_{2}∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\bullet}}}\right)
be the infinite unfoldings of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, i.e.
M_{1}=\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
, and
M_{2}=\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
. Then
eq.

2
together with the assumption entails
\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
. Since
\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}},\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}∈\mathcal{H}_{T}
by
eq.

3
, it follows by
eq.

6
that
\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrel{\sim}\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
. Finally, by using
eq.

4
we obtain
M_{1}=M_{2}
, and hence
\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=M_{1}=M_{2}=\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}
.
∎

6
Interpretion of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as λ-term-graphs

§ 6.1

(overview)
.

We have found a way to model sharing in
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
by interpreting
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as eager-scope λ-ap-ho-term-graphs. We have also identified eager-scope λ-term-graphs as a class of first-order term graphs that faithfully implements (functional) bisimulation on λ-ap-ho-term-graphs. Since we have a higher-order term graph semantics for interpreting
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as λ-ap-ho-term-graphs, as well as translation from λ-ap-ho-term-graphs to λ-term-graphs and back, we seem to be able to turn our attention to the readback function as the last required ingredient of the methods described in
4.4
. However, we first need to add black holes to the formalisation of λ-term-graphs as done for λ-ap-ho-term-graphs. And then we also need to deal with a few intricacies of λ-term-graphs with regards to readback.

Analogously to
5.5
we extend the signature
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
and the correctness conditions in
definition

7.5
by a black-hole case.

Definition 6.2

(signature for λ-term-graphs with black holes)
.

By
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{S},\bullet}
we denote the signature
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{0}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2}}
extended by a black-hole symbol
\bullet
with arity
0
, thus
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{S},\bullet}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{@,λ,\mathsf{0},\mathsf{S},\bullet}}}\right\}
with
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(@}}\right)=2
,
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ}}\right)={\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{0}}}\right)=1
,
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{S}}}\right)=2
, and
{\mathrm{ar}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bullet}}\right)=0
.

Definition 6.3

(abstraction-prefix function for
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{S},\bullet}
-term-graphs)
.

As
definition

7.5
but over signature
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{S},\bullet}
and with the following condition added:

\displaystyle\penalty\ {\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bullet}}\right)=\epsilon

(black hole)

Definition 6.4

(λ-term-graph with black holes)
.

As
definition

6.3
, but over signature
\Sigma^{λ}_{\mathsf{S},\bullet}
and using
definition

6.3
.

Terminology 6.5

(λ-term-graph := λ-term-graph with black holes)
.

Henceforth, if we speak of λ-term-graphs, we refer to this specific variant with black holes and variable and scope-delimiter vertices with backlinks. All the relevant properties that hold for ordinary λ-term-graphs carry over.

Remark 6.6

(relaxed black-hole condition)
.

The condition from
definition

6.3
could also be relaxed to say
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bullet}}\right)=w
where
w
is an arbitrary word of vertices. In that case instead of one single shared function definition of the form
f=f
one would obtain multiple such definitions, but at most one for each scope.

§ 6.7

(
{\mathrm{\mathcal{HT}}}
)
.

{\mathrm{\mathcal{HT}}}
, the function that maps λ-ap-ho-term-graphs to their corresponding λ-term-graphs is defined as
{\mathrm{G}}_{1,2}
from
proposition

7.18
but amended to also translate black holes (in the obvious way).

§ 6.8

(two interpretations as λ-term-graphs)
.

We will consider in fact two interpretations of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as λ-term-graphs: first we define
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}
as the composition of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathrm{\mathcal{HT}}}
; then we define the semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
with more fine-grained
\mathsf{S}
-sharing, which is necessary for defining a readback with
item

3
.

By composing the interpretation
{\mathrm{\mathcal{HT}}}
of λ-ap-ho-term-graphs as λ-term-graphs with the λ-ap-ho-term-graph semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
, a semantics of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as λ-term-graphs is obtained. There is, however, a more direct way to define this semantics: by using an adaptation of the translation rules
R
in
fig.

3
, on which
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}
is based. For this, let
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{S}}
be the result of replacing the rule
\mathsf{S}
in
R
by the version in
fig.

7
. While applications of this variant of the
\mathsf{S}
-rule also shorten the abstraction-prefix, they additionally produce a delimiter vertex.

Here, at the end of the translation process, every loop on an indirection vertex with a prefix of length
n
is replaced by a chain of
n

\mathsf{S}
-vertices followed by a black hole vertex. Note that, while the system
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{S}}
inherits all of the non-determinism of
R
, the possible degrees of freedom have additional impact on the result, because now they also determine the precise degree of
\mathsf{S}
-vertex sharing.
By analogous stipulations as in
definition

5.19
we define the conditions under which a λ-term-graph is called
eager-scope

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{S}}
-generated, or
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{S}}
-generated
with minimal prefixes
, from a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term. For these notions, statements entirely analogous to
proposition

5.20
hold.

\mathsf{S}
:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,x^{v}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f_{1}^{v_{1}},\dots,f_{n}^{v_{n}}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

\overset{\text{\small$x∉{\mathrm{FV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L}}\right)$ }}{\underset{\text{\small$f_{i}∉{\mathrm{FV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L}}\right)$ }}{\Rightarrow}}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}}}}\right)\;{L}
\mathsf{S}
\scriptstyle({\mathrm{vs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{p}}}\right)\,v)
λ
\scriptstyle v
\scriptstyle v

Figure 7:
Delimiter-vertex producing version of the
\mathsf{S}
-rule from
fig.

3

Definition 6.9

(
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}
)
.

The semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}
for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as λ-term-graphs is defined as
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}:{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)\textrightarrow\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
,
L↦\llbracket L\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}:=\,
the eager-scope term graph that is
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{S}}
-generated with minimal prefixes from a garbage-free version
L
.

§ 6.10

(no
\mathsf{S}
-sharing)
.

For an example, see
example

6.18
below. In
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}
, ‘
\mathrm{min}
’ also indicates that λ-term-graphs obtained via this semantics exhibit minimal (in fact no) sharing (two or more incoming edges) of
\mathsf{S}
-vertices. This is substantiated by the next proposition, in the light of the fact that
{\mathrm{\mathcal{HT}}}
does not create any shared
\mathsf{S}
-vertices.

Proposition 6.11
.

\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}={{\mathrm{\mathcal{HT}}}}∘{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{H}}}
.

§ 6.12

(
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}
is partial)
.

Hence
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}
only yields λ-term-graphs without sharing of
\mathsf{S}
-vertices, and therefore its image cannot be all of
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
. As a consequence, we cannot hope to define a readback function
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}
with respect to
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}
that adheres to
item

3
, because that requires that the image of the semantics is
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
in its entirety.

§ 6.13

(eager-scope
R
-generated with maximal prefixes)
.

Therefore we provide an alternative λ-term-graph semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
with
{\mathrm{im}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}}}\right)=\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
. We achieve this by letting the
\mathsf{let}
-binding-structure of the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term influence the degree of
\mathsf{S}
-sharing as much as possible, while still remaining eager-scope.

By ‘letting the
\mathsf{let}
-binding-structure determine the degree of
\mathsf{S}
-sharing’ we mean to eliminate the freedom of choice with respect to closing scopes: one can either place a scope-delimiter vertex at the top of the translation of a function binding in which case it is shared, or right above every use site of that function in which case it is not. See
fig.

8
for illustration.

λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=x}\;\mathsf{in}\;{λ{y}.\,{{f}\;{({f}\;{y})}}}}

λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=x}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{f}\;{({f}\;{y})}}}}

λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=x}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{{f}\;{f}}\;{y}}}}

Figure 8:

In
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
the degree of
\mathsf{S}
-sharing is determined by the
\mathsf{let}
-structure as much as possible. Consider the above
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms and their term graph interpretation w.r.t.
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
.

left

:
f
is bound outside of
y
’s scope. Therefore it is not
f
’s responsibility to close
y
’s scope but the responsibility of
f
’s two use sites.

middle

:
f
is bound within
y
’s scope so it is
f
’s responsibility to close it.

right

:
f
is bound within
y
’s scope. Therefore it should be
f
’s responsibility to close
y
’s scope, but putting the scope delimiter underneath the lower application would violate eager scope-closure.

Definition 6.14

(eager-scope
R
-generated with maximal prefixes)
.

We say that a λ-ap-ho-term-graph
G
is
eager-scope
R
-generated with maximal prefixes
from a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
if
G
is
R
-generated from
L
by a translation process in which in applications of the
\mathsf{let}
-rule the prefixes are chosen maximally, but so that the eager-scope property of the process is not compromised. It can be shown that this condition fixes the prefix lengths per application of the
\mathsf{let}
-rule.

Definition 6.15

(
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
)
.

The semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms as λ-term-graphs is defined as
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}:{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)\textrightarrow\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
,
L↦\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}:=\,
the λ-term-graph that is eager-scope
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{S}}
-generated with maximal prefixes from a garbage-free version of
L
.

\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}

=\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}=\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}

=\llbracket L_{3}\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}=\llbracket L^{\prime}_{3}\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}

\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}

\llbracket L_{3}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}=\llbracket L^{\prime}_{3}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}

Figure 9:
Translation of the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms from
example

6.18
with the semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}
and
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
. For legibility some backlinks are merged.

Proposition 6.16
.

\llbracket L\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}^{S}\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}

holds for all
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms
L
.

Now due to this, and due to
theorem

7.20

item

iii
, the statement of
theorem

5.23
can be transferred to
T
, yielding
item

1
for
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
.

Theorem 6.17
.

For all
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms
L_{1}
and
L_{2}

the following holds:

\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}=\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.1pt{\boldsymbol{λ}}}

if and only if

\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
.

Example 6.18
.

Consider the following four
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms:

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;{I=λ{z}.\,{z}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=x}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{({({y}\;{I})}\;{({I}\;{y})})}\;{({f}\;{f})}}}}}

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle=λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{I=λ{z}.\,{z}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=x}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{({({y}\;{I})}\;{({I}\;{y})})}\;{({f}\;{f})}}}}}

\displaystyle L_{3}

\displaystyle=λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{I=λ{z}.\,{z},\,f=x}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{({({y}\;{I})}\;{({I}\;{y})})}\;{({f}\;{f})}}}}

\displaystyle L^{\prime}_{3}

\displaystyle=λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{I=λ{z}.\,{z}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=x,\ g=I}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{({({y}\;{g})}\;{({g}\;{y})})}\;{({f}\;{f})}}}}}

The three possible fillings of the dashed area in
fig.

9

depict the translations
\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
,
\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
, and
\llbracket L_{3}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}=\llbracket L^{\prime}_{3}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
. The translations of the four terms with
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}
are identical:

\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}=\llbracket L_{2}\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}=\llbracket L_{3}\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}=\llbracket L^{\prime}_{3}\rrbracket^{\mathrm{min}}_{\mathcal{T}}=\llbracket L_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
.

See
4.3
for a fully-worked out stepwise translation of
L_{2}
and also
.

7
Readback of λ-term-graphs

§ 7.1

(overview)
.

In this section we describe how from a given λ-term-graph
G
a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
that represents
G
(i.e. for which
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}=G
holds) can be ‘read back’. For this purpose we define a process based on synthesis rules. It defines a readback function from λ-term-graphs to
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. We illustrate this process by an example, formulate its most important properties, and sketch the proof of
item

3
.

§ 7.2

(
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
is not invertible)
.

Note that we state as the desired
item

3
of the readback
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}
to be a right-inverse and not a ‘full’ inverse of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
. This is because
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
is not injective. Consider the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms
λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=x}\;\mathsf{in}\;{λ{y}.\,{f}}}
and
λ{x}.\,{λ{y}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=x}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}}}
. They only differ in the position of the function definition; this information is lost in the translation;
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
maps them to the same term graph. In particular, this kind of relocation of function-bindings (called
let-floating

[
17
]
) preserves the λ-term-graph interpretation.

§ 7.3

(approach)
.

The idea underlying the definition of the readback procedure is the following: For a given λ-term-graph
G
, a spanning tree
T
for
G
(augmented with a dedicated root node) is constructed that severs cycles due to recursive bindings and variable and scope-delimiter backlinks. Now the spanning tree
T
facilitates an inductive bottom-up (from the leafs upwards) synthesis process along
T
, which labels the edges of
G
with prefixed
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. For this process we use local rules (see
fig.

11
) that synthesise labels for incoming edges of a vertex from the labels of its outgoing edges. Eventually the readback of
G
is obtained as the label for the edge that singles out the root of term graph.

§ 7.4

(placement of function definitions)
.

In the design of the readback rules, there is some freedom in where to place the function bindings in the synthesised term (
7.2
).
Here, function bindings will be put into a
\mathsf{let}
-expression placed as high up in the term as possible: a binding arising from the term synthesised for a shared vertex
v
is placed in a
\mathsf{let}
-expression that is created at the enclosing λ-abstraction of
v
(the rightmost vertex in the abstraction-prefix
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
of
v
).

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=λ{y}.\,{{{f}\;{x}}\;{y}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{f}\;{f}}}}
\top
λ
\scriptstyle\penalty\ x
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=λ{y}.\,{{{f}\;{x}}\;{y}}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{f}\;{f}}}}
\scriptstyle()
@
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=λ{y}.\,{{{f}\;{x}}\;{y}}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{{f}\;{f}}
\scriptstyle()
|
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=λ{y}.\,{{{f}\;{x}}\;{y}}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{f}
\scriptstyle(x)
\scriptstyle\penalty\ f
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=\text{?}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{f}
λ
\scriptstyle\penalty\ y
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=\text{?}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{λ{y}.\,{{{f}\;{x}}\;{y}}}
\scriptstyle(x)
@
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=\text{?}}}}\right\}\,y\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{{{f}\;{x}}\;{y}}
\scriptstyle(x)
\mathsf{S}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=\text{?}}}}\right\}\,y\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{{f}\;{x}}
\scriptstyle(x\,y)
@
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=\text{?}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{{f}\;{x}}
\scriptstyle(x\,y)
\mathsf{0}
\scriptstyle(x)
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{x}
\scriptstyle(x)
\mathsf{0}
\scriptstyle(x\,y)
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,y\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{y}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=\text{?}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{f}

Figure 10:
Example of the readback synthesis from a λ-term-graph.

Definition 7.5

(readback of λ-term-graphs)
.

Let
G∈\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
be an eager-scope λ-term-graph. The process of computing the readback of
G
(a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term) consists of the following five steps, starting on
G
:

(Rb-1)

Determine the abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P}}
for
G
by performing a traversal over
G
, and associate with every vertex
v
of
G
its abstraction-prefix
{\mathrm{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
.

(Rb-2)

Add a new vertex on top with label
\top
, arity 1, and empty abstraction prefix. Let
G^{\prime}
be the resulting term graph, and
{\mathrm{P}}^{\prime}
its abstraction-prefix function.

(Rb-3)

Introduce indirection vertices to organise sharing: For every vertex
v
of
G^{\prime}
with two or more incoming non-variable-backlink edges, add an indirection vertex
v_{0}
, redirect the incoming edges of
v
that are not variable backlinks to
v_{0}
, and direct the outgoing edge from
v_{0}
to
v
. In the resulting term graph
G^{\prime\prime}
only indirection vertices are shared ; their names will be used. Extend
{\mathrm{P}}^{\prime}
to an abstraction-prefix function
{\mathrm{P}}^{\prime\prime}
for
G^{\prime\prime}
so that every indirection vertex
v_{0}
gets the prefix of its successor
v
.

(Rb-4)

Construct a spanning tree
T^{\prime\prime}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
by using a depth-first search (DFS) on
G^{\prime\prime}
. Note that all variable backlinks and
\mathsf{S}
-backlinks, and some of the recursive backlinks, of
G^{\prime\prime}
, are not contained in
T^{\prime\prime}
, because they are back-edges of the DFS.

(Rb-5)

Apply the readback synthesis rules from
fig.

11
to
G^{\prime\prime}
with respect to
T^{\prime\prime}
. By this a complete labelling of the edges of
G^{\prime\prime}
by prefixed
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms is constructed. The rules define how the labelling for an incoming edge (on top) of a vertex
v
is synthesised under the assumption of an already determined labelling of an outgoing edge of (and below)
v
. If the outgoing edge in the rule does not carry a label, then the labelling of the incoming edge can happen regardless. Note that in these rules:

∘

full (dotted) lines indicate spanning tree (non-spanning tree) edges; broken lines match either kind;

∘

abstraction prefixes of vertices are crucial for the
\mathsf{0}
-vertex, and the second indirection vertex rule, where the prefixes in the synthesised terms are created; in the other rules the prefix of the assumed term is used; for indicating a correspondence between a term’s and a vertex’s abstraction prefix we denote by
{\mathrm{vs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{p}}}\right)
the word of vertices occurring in a term’s prefix
\vec{p}
;

∘

the rule for indirection vertices with incoming non-spanning tree edge introduces an unfinished function binding
f=\text{?}
for
f
; unfinished bindings are to be filled in later;

∘

the @-vertex rule applies only if
{\mathrm{vs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{p}_{0}}}\right)={\mathrm{vs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{p}_{1}}}\right)
; the operation
\mathrel{\vec{\cup}}
used in the synthesised term’s prefix builds the union per prefix variable of the pertaining bindings; if the prefixed terms
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}_{0}}}}\right)\;{L_{0}}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}_{1}}}}\right)\;{L_{1}}
assumed in this rule contain a yet unfinished function binding
f=\text{?}
and a completed binding
f=P
at a λ-variable
z
, the synthesised term contains the completed binding
f=P
at
z
;

If this process yields the label
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}
at the root of
G^{\prime\prime}
, we call
L
the
readback of
G
.

\top
\scriptstyle\star
\scriptstyle()
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

\top
(
B≠\emptyset
)
\scriptstyle\star
\scriptstyle()
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{B}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

λ
\scriptstyle v_{n}
\scriptstyle({\mathrm{vs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{p}}}\right))
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}}}}\right)\;{λ{v_{n}}.\,{L}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,v_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

λ
(
B≠\emptyset
)
\scriptstyle v_{n}
\scriptstyle({\mathrm{vs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{p}}}\right))
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}}}}\right)\;{λ{v_{n}}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,v_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{B}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

@
\scriptstyle(\vec{v})
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}_{0}\mathrel{\vec{\cup}}\vec{p}_{1}}}}\right)\;{{L_{0}}\;{L_{1}}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}_{0}}}}\right)\;{L_{0}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}_{1}}}}\right)\;{L_{1}}

\mathsf{0}
\scriptstyle(v_{1}\,\dots\,v_{n})
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,v_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\hskip 1.0pt\cdots\hskip 2.0ptv_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{v_{n}}
λ
\scriptstyle v_{n}

\mathsf{S}
\scriptstyle({\mathrm{vs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{p}}}\right)\,v_{n})
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,v_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}
λ
\scriptstyle v_{n}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}}}}\right)\;{L}

\bullet
\scriptstyle()
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=f}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{f}

|
\scriptstyle({\mathrm{vs}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\vec{p}}}\right)\,v_{n+1})
\scriptstyle f
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,v_{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{B,f=L}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{f}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,v_{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{B,(f=\text{?})}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

|
\scriptstyle(v_{1}\,\dots\,v_{n}\,v_{n+1})
\scriptstyle f
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,v_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\hskip 1.0pt\cdots\hskip 2.0ptv_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}\,v_{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=\text{?}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{f}

Figure 11:
Readback synthesis rules for computing a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term from a λ-term-graph. For explanations, see
definition

7.5

item

5
.

§ 7.6

(readback is deterministic)
.

For every edge
e
the synthesis rule to be applied is uniquely determined by the label of the target vertex
v
of
e
together with side conditions for
λ
,
\top
, and
|
. In the last case it depends on whether
e
is a spanning-tree edge or not.

Proposition 7.7

(readback function)
.

For every λ-term-graph
G
the process from
definition

7.5
produces a complete edge labelling of the (modified) term graph, with label
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}
for the root edge, where
L
is a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term. Hence it yields
L
as the readback of
G
. Thus
definition

7.5
defines a function
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}:\mathcal{T}\textrightarrow{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)
, the
readback function
.

Example 7.8
.

See
fig.

10
for the illustration of the synthesis of the readback from an example λ-term-graph. Full lines depict spanning tree edges, dotted lines depict non-spanning-tree edges.

The following theorem validates
item

3
.

Theorem 7.9
.

For all
G∈\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
it holds:
((\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}∘{{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}})})(G)=\llbracket{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}\mathrel{\sim}G
, i.e.,
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}
is a right-inverse of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
, and
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
a left-inverse of
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}
, up to
\mathrel{\sim}
. Hence
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}
is injective, and
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
is surjective, thus
{\mathrm{im}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}}}\right)=\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{\mathcal{T}}
.

f
:

|
\scriptstyle w

\overset{\text{$w$ already has an}}{\underset{\text{outgoing edge}}{⇐}}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,x^{v}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\dots,f^{w}=L,\dots}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{f}

\overset{\text{no outgoing edge}}{\underset{\text{for $w$ yet}}{⇒}}

|
\scriptstyle w
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,x^{v}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\dots,f^{w}=L,\dots}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

:

Figure 12:
Augmented version of two of the translation rules from
fig.

3
for an alternative definition of the λ-term-graph semantics of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms.

\mathrm{rec}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,x^{v}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\dots,f^{w}=L,\dots}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{f}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,x^{v}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\dots,f^{w}=L,\dots}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

\mathrm{rec}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{\small($w$ is fresh)}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,x^{v}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\dots,f=L,\dots}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{f}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,x^{v}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{B,f_{1}=L_{1},\dots,f_{k}=L_{k}}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L_{0}}

\mathsf{let}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\vec{p}\,x^{v}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{B}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{\mathsf{let}\;{f_{1}=L_{1},\dots,f_{k}=L_{k}}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L_{0}}}

Figure 13:
Formulation of the local translation rules in
fig.

12
in the form of inference rules.

Proof Idea.

Graph translation steps can be linked with corresponding readback steps in order to establish that the former roughly reverse the latter. Roughly, because e.g. reversing a λ-readback step necessitates both a λ- and a
\mathsf{let}
-translation step. Therefore, this correspondence holds only for a modification of the translation rules
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{S}}
from
fig.

3
, shown in
fig.

7
where the rules
\mathsf{let}
(for
\mathsf{let}
-expressions) and
f
(for occurrences of function variables) are replaced by the locally-operating versions in
fig.

12
. Moreover, the translation rules need to store entire function bindings in the prefix (not just function variables) as the readback rules do. To the augmented rules we also add a initiating rule

\top
:

\overset{}{\underset{}{\Rightarrow}}

\top
\scriptstyle\star
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

(start of translation of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
)

for creating a top vertex. Now the translation of a
\mathsf{let}
-expression does no longer directly spawn translations of the bindings, but the bindings will only be translated later once their calls have been reached during the translation process of the body, or of the definitions of other already translated bindings. Note that in the
\mathsf{let}
-rule in
fig.

12
function bindings are associated with the rightmost variable in the prefix, which corresponds to choosing
l_{i}=n
in the
\mathsf{let}
-rule in
fig.

3
. While such a stipulation does not guarantee the eager-scope translation of every term, it actually does so for all
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms that are obtained by the readback (on these terms the such defined translation coincides with
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
from
definition

5.21
).

Please find in
fig.

14
a graphical argument for the stepwise reversal of readback steps through (augmented) translation steps. This establishes that graph translation steps reverse readback steps, and is the crucial step in the proof of the theorem. The proof uses induction on access paths, and an invariant that relates the eager-scope property localised for a vertex
v
with the applicability of the
\mathsf{S}
-rule to the readback term synthesised at
v
. Note that in most cases the sets of function bindings on the left-hand side (
\bar{B}_{i}
) and the right-hand side (
B_{i}
) differ, due to the freedoms of the function definition’s positions in the translated
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term (see
7.2
).

Note that
\cupdot
denotes a join of two sets of function definitions where a defined function always overrules an undefined function, such that for instance the following holds:
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=x,g=?}}}\right\}\cupdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=?,g=?}}}\right\}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{f=x,g=?}}}\right\}
.
∎

\top
\scriptstyle()
\scriptstyle\star
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}
\Longleftarrow

\top
\scriptstyle\star
\scriptstyle()
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\star\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{}}}\right\}}}}\right)\;{L}

(
L
not a

\mathsf{let}
-expression)

(
L
not a

\mathsf{let}
-expression)

8
Complexity analysis

Here we report on the complexity for the individual operations from the previous sections used form implementing maximal sharing and determining unfolding-equivalence.

In the lemma below,
item

ii
and
item

v
justify
item

4
of our methods. Items
item

iii
and
item

iv
detail the complexity of standard methods when used for computing bisimulation collapse and bisimilarity of λ-term-graphs. Note that first-order term graphs can be modelled by deterministic process graphs, and hence by DFAs
[
28
]
. Therefore bisimilarity of term graphs can be computed via language equivalence of the corresponding DFAs (in time
{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(nα(n)}}\right)

[
43
]
, where
α
is the quasi-constant
inverse Ackermann function
) and bisimulation collapse via state minimisation of DFAs (in time
{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n\log n}}\right)
)
[
27
]
.

By
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{L}}}\right|
we denote the size (number of symbols) of a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
. Also we denote by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{G}}}\right|
the size (number of vertices) of a term graph
G
.

Lemma 8.1
.

(i)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}}}}\right|∈{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{L}}}\right|^{2}}}\right)
for
L∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)
.

(ii)

Translating
L∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)
into
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}∈\mathcal{T}
takes time
{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{L}}}\right|^{2}}}\right)
.

(iii)

Collapsing
G∈\mathcal{T}
to
{G}\hskip 0.73193pt{\text{\small\textbar}\hskip-3.14302pt\downarrow}
is in
{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{G}}}\right|\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{G}}}\right|}}\right)
.

(iv)

Deciding bisimilarity of
G_{1},G_{2}∈\mathcal{T}

requires time
{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(nα(n)}}\right)
for
n=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{G_{1}}}}\right|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{G_{2}}}}\right|}}}\right\}
.

(v)

Computing the readback

{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
for a given
G∈\mathcal{T}
requires time

{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n\log n}}\right)
, for
n=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{G}}}\right|
.

See
fig.

15
for an example that the size bound in item
item

i
of the lemma is tight.

Proposition 8.2
.

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}}}}\right|∈{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{L}}}\right|^{2}}}\right)
for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms
L
.

Consider the finite λ-terms
M_{n}
with
n
occurrences of bindings
λ{x_{2}}
:

λ{x_{0}}.\,{λ{x_{1}}.\,{{{x_{0}}\;{x_{1}}}\;{λ{x_{2}}.\,{{{x_{0}}\;{x_{1}}}\;{λ{x_{1}}.\,{{{x_{0}}\;{x_{2}}}\;{λ{x_{2}}.\,{{{x_{0}}\;{x_{1}}}\;{\dots{λ{x_{2}}.\,{{{x_{0}}\;{x_{1}}}\;{x_{2}}}}}}}}}}}}}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{M_{n}}}}\right|∈{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n}}\right)
. But both the transformation of
M_{n}
into de-Bruijn notation

λ{}.\,{λ{}.\,{{{\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{0})}\;{\mathsf{0}}}\;{λ{}.\,{{{\mathsf{S}^{2}(\mathsf{0})}\;{\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{0})}}\;{λ{}.\,{{{\mathsf{S}^{3}(\mathsf{0})}\;{\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{0})}}\;{λ{}.\,{{{\mathsf{S}^{4}(\mathsf{0})}\;{\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{0})}}\;{\dots{λ{}.\,{{{\mathsf{S}^{2n}(\mathsf{0})}\;{\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{0})}}\;{\mathsf{0}}}}}}}}}}}}}

and the rendering of
M_{n}
with respect to the eager scope-delimiting strategy:

λ{}.\,{λ{}.\,{{{\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{0})}\;{\mathsf{0}}}\;{λ{}.\,{{\mathsf{S}({\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{0})}\;{\mathsf{0}})}\;{λ{}.\,{{\mathsf{S}({\mathsf{S}^{2}(\mathsf{0})}\;{\mathsf{0}})}\;{λ{}.\,{{\mathsf{S}({\mathsf{S}^{3}(\mathsf{0})}\;{\mathsf{0}})}\;{\dots{λ{}.\,{{\mathsf{S}({\mathsf{S}^{2n-1}(\mathsf{0})}\;{\mathsf{0}})}\;{\mathsf{0}}}}}}}}}}}}}

have size
{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n^{2}}}\right)
.

Figure 15:
Example of a sequence
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{M_{n}}}}\right\}_{n}
of finite λ-terms
M_{n}
whose translation into λ-term-graphs grows quadratically in the size of
M_{n}
.

Based on this lemma, and on further considerations, we obtain the following
complexity statements for our methods.

Theorem 8.3
.

(i)

The computation, for a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{L}}}\right|=n
,
of a maximally compactified form

{({\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}\circ\text{\small\textbar}\hskip-3.14302pt\downarrow\circ\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}})}(L)

requires time
{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n^{2}\log n}}\right)
.

By using an
\mathsf{S}
-unsharing operation
\textsf{unsh}_{\mathsf{S}}
after the collapse,
a (typically smaller)
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
(({\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}∘{{\textsf{unsh}_{\mathsf{S}}}∘{\text{\small\textbar}\hskip-3.14302pt\downarrow}}∘{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}})}){L}

of size
{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n\log n}}\right)
can be obtained,
with the same time complexity.

(ii)

The decision of whether two
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are unfolding equivalent
requires time
{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n^{2}}}\right)α(n)
for
n=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{L_{1}}}}\right|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{L_{2}}}}\right|}}}\right\}
.

9
Implementation

We have implemented our methods in Haskell using the
Utrecht University Attribute Grammar System
. The implementation is available at
http://hackage.haskell.org/package/maxsharing/
. Output produced for a number of examples from this thesis can be found in
chapter

5
. The output includes translations into λ-term-graphs (in DFA-form) according to different semantics, complete derivations w.r.t. these semantics, the bisimulation collapse of the λ-term-graphs and the readback thereof.

10
Modifications, extensions and applications

We conclude by describing straightforward modifications, extensions, and promising areas of application for our methods.

1
Modifications

§ 10.1

(implicit sharing of λ-variable)
.

Our method introduces explicit sharing via
\mathsf{let}
for multiple occurences of the same λ-variable. For instance the term
λ{x}.\,{{x}\;{x}}
is compactified into
λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{f=x}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{f}\;{f}}}
. Such explicit sharing of abstraction variables is excessive for many applications. This is easily resolved, by unsharing variable vertices before applying the readback, or by preventing the readback from introducing function bindings when only a variable vertex is shared.

§ 10.2

(avoiding aliases produced by the readback)
.

The readback function in
section

7
is sensitive to the degree of sharing of
\mathsf{S}
-vertices in the given λ-term-graph: it maps two λ-term-graphs that only differ in what concerns sharing of
\mathsf{S}
-vertices to different
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms. Typically, for λ-term-graphs with maximal sharing of
\mathsf{S}
-vertices this can produce function bindings that are just ‘aliases’, such as
g
is alias for
I
in
L^{\prime}_{3}
from
example

6.18
. This can be avoided in two ways: by slightly adapting the readback function, or by performing maximal unsharing of
\mathsf{S}
-vertices before applying the readback as defined.

§ 10.3

(preventing disadvantageous sharing)
.

Introducing sharing at compile-time can cause ‘space leaks’, i.e. a needlessly high memory footprint, at run-time, because ‘a large data structure becomes shared [
\dots
], and therefore its space which before was reclaimed by garbage collection now cannot be reclaimed until its last reference is used’
[
50
]
. For this reason, realisations of CSE
[
11
]
prevent the introduction of such undesired sharing by suitable conditions that account for the type of potentially shared subexpressions, and their strictness in the program.

As our approach generalises CSE, it inherits this weakness, and the introduction sharing needs to be restricted in a similar fashion. Technically this can easily achieved by adding additional backlinks to prevent parts of the λ-term-graph from collapsing.

§ 10.4

(a more general notion of readback)
.

item

3
is rather rigorous in that it imposes sharing structure on
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
that is specific to λ-term-graphs (degrees of
\mathsf{S}
-sharing). For a weaker definition of
item

3
with
\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}^{\mathsf{S}}
in place of isomorphism, a readback does not have to be injective and independently of the degree of
\mathsf{S}
-sharing a readback function always exists.

§ 10.5

(scope-closure strategies)
.

We focused on eager-scope translations since they maximise sharing. However, every scope-closure strategy
[
19
]
induces a translation and its own notion of maximal sharing.

2
Extensions

§ 10.6

(full functional languages)
.

In order to support programming languages that are based on
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
like Haskell, additional language constructs need to be supported. Such languages can typically be desugared into a core language, which comprises only a small subset of language constructs such as constructors, case statements, and primitives. These constructs can be represented in an extension of
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
by additional function symbols. In conjunction with a desugarer our methods are applicable to full programming languages.

§ 10.7

(other programming languages, and calculi with binding constructs)
.

Most programming languages feature constructs for grouping definitions that are similar to
\mathsf{letrec}
. We therefore expect that our methods can be adapted to many imperative languages in particular, and may turn out to be fruitful for optimising compilers. Our methods for achieving maximal sharing certainly generalise to theoretical frameworks and calculi with binding constructs, such as the
\pi
-calculus
[
41
]
, and higher-order rewrite systems (e.g. CRSs and HRSs,
[
51
]
) as used here for the formalisation of
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
.

§ 10.8

(fully-lazy λ-lifting)
.

There is a close connection between our methods and fully-lazy λ-lifting
[
30
,
33
]
. In particular, the required-variable and scope analysis of a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
L
on which the λ-term-graph translation
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
is based is analogous to the one needed for extracting from
L
the supercombinators in the result
\hat{L}
of fully-lazy λ-lifting
L
. Moreover, the fully-lazy λ-lifting transformation can even be implemented in a natural way on the basis of our methods. Namely as the composition
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}_{\textit{LL}}∘\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
of the translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathcal{T}}
into λ-term-graph, where
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}_{\textit{LL}}
is a variant readback function that, for a given λ-term-graph, synthesises the system
\hat{L}
of supercombinators, instead of the
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term
{\mathrm{\mathit{rb}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L}}\right)
.

§ 10.9

(maximal sharing on supercombinator translations of
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms)
.

λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms
L
correspond to supercombinator systems
S
, the result of fully-lazy lambda-lifting
L
: the combinators in
S
correspond with ‘extended scopes’ in
L
, and supercombinator reduction steps on
S
correspond with weak β-reduction steps
L
. In the case of λ-calculus this has been established by Balabonski
[
5
]
. Via this correspondence the maximal-sharing method for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms can be lifted to obtain a maximal-sharing method systems of supercombinators obtained by fully-lazy lambda-lifting.

§ 10.10

(non-eager scope-closure strategies)
.

We focused on eager-scope translations, because they facilitate maximal sharing, and guarantee that interpretations of unfolding-equivalent
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms are bisimilar. Yet every scope-closure strategy induces a translation and its own notion of maximal sharing. For adapting our maximal sharing method it however necessary to modify the translation into first-order term graphs in such a way that the image class obtained is closed under functional bisimulation (
{\mathcal{T}}_{{1},{2}}
is not closed under functional bisimulation, unlike its subclass
\prescript{\smash{\mathrm{eag}}}{}{{\mathcal{T}}_{{1},{2}}}=\mathcal{T}
). This can be achieved by using delimiter vertices also below variable vertices to close scopes that are still open
[
24
, report]
.

§ 10.11

(weaker notions of sharing)
.

The presented methods deal with sharing as expressed by
\mathsf{letrec}
that is horizontal, vertical, or twisted (see
remark

3.4
). By contrast, the μ-construct
[
8
,
20
]
expresses only vertical, and the non-recursive
\mathsf{let}
only horizontal, sharing (see
remark

3.4
). By restricting bisimulation (either artificially or by adding special backlinks), our methods can be adapted to the λ-calculus with μ
[
20
]
, or with
\mathsf{let}
.

3
Applications

§ 10.12

(maximal sharing at run-time)
.

Maximal sharing can be applied repeatedly at run-time in order to regain a maximally shared form, thereby speeding up evaluation. This is reminiscent of ‘collapsed tree rewriting’
[
49
]
for evaluating first-order term graphs represented as maximally shared dags. Since the state of a program in the memory at run-time is typically represented as a supercombinator graph, compactification by bisimulation collapse can take place directly on that graph (see
section

2
), no translation is needed. Compactification can be coupled with garbage collection as bisimulation collapse subsumes some of the work required for a mark and sweep garbage collector. However, a compromise needs to be found between the costs for the optimisation and the gained efficiency.

§ 10.13

(additional prevention of disadvantageous sharing)
.

While static analysis methods for preventing sharing that may be disadvantageous at run-time can be adapted from CSE to the maximal-sharing method (see
section

1
), this has yet to be investigated for binding-time analysis
[
48
]
and a sharing analysis of partial applications
[
16
]
.
for fine-tuning sharing of partial applications in supercombinator translations
[
16
]
.

§ 10.14

(compile-time optimisation)
.

Increasing sharing facilitates potential gains in efficiency. Our method generalises common subexpression elimination, but therefore it also inherits its shortcomings: the cost of sharing (e.g. of very small functions) might exceed the gain. In non-strict functional languages, sharing can cause ‘memory leaks’
[
11
]
. Therefore, similar as for CSE, additional dynamic analyses like binding-time analysis
[
48
]
, and heuristics to restrict sharing in cases when it is disadvantageous
[
33
,
16
]
are in order.

§ 10.15

(code improvement)
.

In programming it is generally desirable to avoid duplication of code. As an extension of CSE, our method is able to detect code duplication. The bisimulation collapse of the term graph interpretation of a program can, together with the readback, provide guidance on how code can be written more compactly with less duplication. This optimisation has to be fine-tuned to avoid excessive behaviour like the explicit sharing of variable occurrences (see
section

1
). Yet for this only lightweight additional machinery is needed, such as size constraints or annotations to restrict the bisimulation collapse.

§ 10.16

(function equivalence)
.

Recognising whether two programs are equivalent in the sense that they implement the same function is undecidable. Still, this problem is tackled by proof assistants, and by automated theorem provers used in type-checkers of compilers for dependently-typed programming languages such as Agda. For such systems coinductive proofs are more difficult to find than inductive ones, and require more effort by the user. Our method for deciding unfolding-equivalence could contribute to finding coinductive proofs.
