Chapter 5
Implementation Showcase

To demonstrate the realisability of our method, we have implemented the methods from
chapter

3
. The implementation is called
maxsharing
and is available on Hackage:
http://hackage.haskell.org/package/maxsharing/
. It is written in Haskell and therefore requires the
Haskell Platform
to be installed. Then, maxsharing can be installed via
cabal-install
using the commands
cabal update
and
cabal install maxsharing
from the terminal. Invoke the executable
maxsharing
in your cabal-directory with a file as an argument that contains a
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term. Run
maxsharing -h
for help on run-time flags.

For further illustration we provide the output of the
maxsharing
tool
for a number of examples from the thesis. Since the volume of the output is too large to include here it is supplied as an external appendix available at
http://rochel.info/thesis/
.

Chapter 6
Confluence of unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms

Here we present a proof of
proposition

7.8
which is an adaptation of
[
18
]
where we prove the confluence of a precursor of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
. Here the proof below is written out for
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
, but works for
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
as well if the critical pairs involving the rules
\varrho^{\mathrm{tighten}}
and
\varrho^{\bullet}
are left out.

Proof of
proposition

7.8
.

First of all, we cannot use Newman’s Lemma to prove confluence, because
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
is not terminating. To show confluence of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
we use the method of ‘decreasing diagrams’
[
45
, Section 2.3]

[
51
, Section 14.2]
. We use it however not to prove confluence of the rewriting relation
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
induced by
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
directly, but of the abstract reduction system
\mathcal{A}=({\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right),\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathrel{\hskip 4.30554pt||\hskip-12.05553pt\longrightarrow}_{{\rho}_{d}}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {(d,\rho)\in ℕ\times R}}}\right\})
with
R
as the set of rules of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
where
\mathrel{\hskip 4.30554pt||\hskip-12.05553pt\longrightarrow}_{{\rho}_{d}}
denotes the parallel rewriting relation on
{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)
induced by rule
\rho
at
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth
d
. As a precedence order we consider the order induced by the
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth:

\mathrel{\hskip 4.30554pt||\hskip-12.05553pt\longrightarrow}_{{\rho}_{d}}\penalty\ ≥\penalty\ \mathrel{\hskip 4.30554pt||\hskip-12.05553pt\longrightarrow}_{{\sigma}_{d^{\prime}}}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ d\geq d^{\prime}

The
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth of a redex in
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term denotes the number of
\mathsf{let}
-nodes passed on the path from the root of the term tree to the corresponding position. We write
\mathrel{\to}_{{\rho}_{d}}
to denote the relation induced by applying rule
\rho
contracting a redex at
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth
d
.

Let us denote the rewriting relation induced by
A
by
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathcal{A}}
:

\mathrel{\to}_{\mathcal{A}}=\bigcup\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathrel{\hskip 4.30554pt||\hskip-12.05553pt\longrightarrow}_{{\rho}_{d}}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {(d,\rho)\in ℕ\times R}}}\right\}

If
\mathrel{\to}_{\mathcal{A}}
is confluent then
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
is confluent because it holds:
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}\subseteq\mathrel{\to}_{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\triangledown}
or equivalently
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\triangledown}
(see also
[
45
, Lemma 2.2.5]
).

We use parallel steps because the preceding attempt to prove confluence of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
-steps themselves by decreasing diagrams was unsuccessful. As a precedent order we considered an ordering on the rules and lexicographic extensions of such orderings with the
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth of the contracted redex. We came to the conclusion that no such order could ensure decreasingness of the elementary diagrams of both the critical pairs as well as the strictly nested redexes. This was due to redex duplication induced by the diverging steps, so that joining the diagram required a many-step that disrupted decreasingness. In order to resolve this problem we considered parallel steps such that the problematic multi-step would become a single parallel step. This led to more intricate diagrams but turned out to be a viable solution.

We will show that two diverging parallel steps in
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
can be joined in an elementary diagram of the following form with
d\leq e
.

||
{\rho}_{d}
=
{\sigma}_{e}
||
{\sigma}_{e-1}
||
{\rho}_{d}
=
{\sigma}_{e}
=
{\sigma}_{e-1}

Figure 1:
Elementary diagram

If we pick as the precedence order on the steps the order that is induced by their
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth, the diagram is decreasing. Note that in all the diagrams we implicitly assume the reflexive closure for all arrows. The rest of the proof is structured as follows. To justify the diagram we distinguish the cases
d=e
and
d<e
, for which we construct diagrams that are instances of the diagram in
fig.

1
.

d
. We construct the diagram below which is an instance of the diagram above where the diverging parallel steps are in sequentialised form. We write terms as fillings of a multihole context

\mathsf{letrec}

such that the contracted

{\rho}_{d}
- and
{\sigma}_{d}

Only the tiles on the diagonal require closer attention because for all other tiles the vertical and horizontal steps take place in different holes of the context, therefore they are disjoint and consequently commute. In the tiles on the diagonal the diverging steps may be either due to a critical pair or to identical steps. In the latter case the diagram is easily joined. In case of a critical pair, since all steps take place at the same
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth any such critical pair must arise from a root overlap. Exhaustive scrutiny of all these critical pairs reveals that they can be joined in a way that conforms to the tiles on the diagonal. Note that the
\mathsf{letrec}
-depths of the steps have to be increased by
d
according to the lifting into a context with its hole at
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth
d
.

.

\mathsf{let}
-expression. This is merely to save page space and can be easily generalised.

Every hole on the diagonal is filled with at most one
{\rho}_{d}
-redex (at the root of the context hole fillings) but because of
d<e
with possibly many
{\sigma}_{e}

{\rho}_{d}
-step and a
{\sigma}_{e}
-step, but there can be at most one, which is due to the rules of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
.

Therefore
{\sigma}_{e}
contracts either an overlap and a number of nested redexes, or only nested redexes without an overlap. These constellations are depicted on the figure below. There is one
{\rho}_{d}
-redex and three
{\sigma}_{e}
-redexes. On the left, one of the
{\sigma}_{e}
-redexes overlaps with the
{\rho}_{d}
-redex while on the right all
{\sigma}_{e}
-redexes are strictly nested inside the
{\rho}_{d}
-redex.

When lifted into a context of
\mathsf{letrec}
-depth
d
both of the diagrams comply to the shape necessary for the diagonal tiles, but we need to be able to handle situations as on as on the left of the above figure, where both nested redexes as well as the overlapping redex are contracted. Firstly, since all
\sigma

\mathsf{letrec}
-depth, it must hold that
d=0
and

, which is due to the rules of
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
. Secondly, none of the involved redex contractions affect any of the nested redexes except for duplicating or erasing them, which means that the residuals of the
\sigma

{\sigma}_{e^{\prime}}

The diagram is composed from the previous two diagrams. A parallel version of the right one constitutes the top part, while the bottom part is an exact replica of the left one. The top part settles the portion arising from the nested redexes, the bottom part settles the portion arising from the overlapping redex.

At last in order to fit that diagram into the scheme of the diagonal tiles the steps on the right have to be reordered such that
{\sigma}_{e_{i}}
-steps with
e_{i}=1
preceed
{\sigma}_{e_{i}}
-steps with
e_{i}=0
. The reordering is viable because every
{\sigma}_{e_{i}}
-step takes place in its own residual of the
{\sigma}_{1}
-step from the left.

We conclude the proof by a comprehensive analysis all critical pairs that arise from non-root overlaps in
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{{{\triangledown\hskip-1.0889pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{$\bullet$ }}\hskip-2.33331pt}}}
as well as the diagrams for joining nested redexes.

{\sigma}_{e}
{\mathrm{tighten}}_{0}

\mathsf{let}\;{B,f=f}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}
\mathsf{let}\;{{B}[{f}:={\bullet}]}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{L}[{f}:={\bullet}]}
\mathsf{let}\;{B^{\prime},f=g}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L^{\prime}}
\mathsf{let}\;{{B^{\prime}}[{f}:={\bullet}]}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{L^{\prime}}[{f}:={\bullet}]}
{\mathrm{tighten}}_{0}

Chapter 7
Unfolding with a Single Rule

Here, we present an alternative rewriting system
{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{1}}}
for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms, which has only a single rule, similar to μ-unfolding. We will briefly argue that the system unfolds to the same λ-terms as
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
. For simplicity we will not address the problem of meaningless bindings, which can be resolved with black holes and appropriate rewriting rules, as done for
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
.

Notation 7.1
.

Hereinafter,
B
stands for
f_{1}=L_{1},\dots,f_{n}=L_{n}
.
Furthermore
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f_{i}}:={L_{i}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {1≤i≤n}}}\right]
stands for the substitution
[f_{1}:=L_{1},\dots,f_{n}:=L_{n}]
.

Definition 7.2

(One-rule rewriting system for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
)
.

The rewriting system
{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{1}}}
consists of the single rule:

\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}\penalty\ \textrightarrow\penalty\ L\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f_{i}}:={L_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f_{j}}:={\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f_{j}}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ 1≤j≤n}}\right]}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ 1≤i≤n}}\right]

In CRS notation:

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L_{1}(\vec{f}),\dots,L_{n}(\vec{f})),L_{0}(\vec{f})}}\right)}}}\right)\textrightarrow L_{0}(L_{1}(\vec{B}),\dots,L_{n}(\vec{B}))

\displaystyle\text{where $\vec{B}$ stands for\penalty\ }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L_{1}(\vec{f}),\dots,L_{n}(\vec{f}),L_{1}(\vec{f})}}\right)}}}\right),\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \dots,\\
\mathsf{let}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([{\vec{f}}]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathsf{in}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L_{1}(\vec{f}),\dots,L_{n}(\vec{f}),L_{n}(\vec{f})}}\right)}}}\right)\end{array}}}\right)

§ 7.3

(an even simpler approach?)
.

One might wonder why the nested substitutions are necessary, why a simpler rule as follows would not be adequate:

\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}\textrightarrow L\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f_{i}}:={\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f_{i}}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ 1≤i≤n}}\right]

This rule is not sufficient because it cannot unfold a term
\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}
correctly if
L
is a variable bound in
B
; it reduces a term like
\mathsf{let}\;{f=x}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f}
to itself.

Proposition 7.4
.

{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{1}}}
is confluent. (
Proof:

{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{1}}}
is orthogonal.)

In order to argue that
{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{1}}}
reduces
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms to the same infinite normal forms as
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
(let us write this as
{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{1}}}
≡
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
), we introduce an intermediate system
{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}
consisting of the rule from
7.3
, and one additional rule from
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}
to handle the problematic case.

Definition 7.5

(Two-rule rewriting system for unfolding
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
)
.

The rewriting system
{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}
consists of the following two rules:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}\textrightarrow L\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f_{i}}:={\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f_{i}}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ 1≤i≤n}}\right]\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{\small(if $L$ is not a variable bound in $B$)}\\
\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f_{i}}\textrightarrow\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L_{i}}\end{array}

Proposition 7.6

(
{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}
is a refinement of
{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{1}}}
)
.

\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{1}}}\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}

Proof.

Proposition 7.7

(
{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}

∀M,N∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)\penalty\ (M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}N\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{1}}}.\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{1}}}N)

Proposition 7.9

(

Proposition 7.10

(
\textit{{R}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{\triangledown}}≡{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}
)
.

∀L∈{\mathrm{Ter}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}}}\right)\penalty\ L\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-6.28612pt\textrightarrow}_{\triangledown}^{!}L^{\prime}\penalty\ \iff\penalty\ L\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-6.28612pt\textrightarrow}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}^{!}L^{\prime}

Proof idea.

Only a rudimentary proof idea is given here, i.e. that the property

\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}.\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}

can be used to construct a coinductive proof. In order to convince ourselves that the property holds we only look at three cases here. In each of these cases the leftmost term reduces via
\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown}
to the rightmost term.

Case:
λ

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{λ{x}.\,{L}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}

\displaystyle(λ{x}.\,{L}){\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f_{i}}:={\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f_{i}}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ 1≤i≤n}}\right]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle λ{x}.\,{L\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f_{i}}:={\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f_{i}}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ 1≤i≤n}}\right]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}λ{x}.\,{\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}}

Case:
@

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{{L}\;{P}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}

\displaystyle({L}\;{P}){\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f_{i}}:={\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f_{i}}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ 1≤i≤n}}\right]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f_{i}}:={\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f_{i}}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ 1≤i≤n}}\right]}}\right)\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f_{j}}:={\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f_{j}}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ 1≤j≤n}}\right]}}\right)\end{array}\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}}}\right)\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{P}}}\right)\end{array}

Case:
\mathrm{letrec}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{\mathsf{let}\;{C}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{let}\;{C}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f_{i}}:={\mathsf{let}\;{B}\;\mathsf{in}\;{f_{i}}}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ 1≤i≤n}}\right]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\leftarrow}_{\triangledown_{\hskip-0.8pt\mathbf{2}}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;{B,C}\;\mathsf{in}\;{L}

Chapter 9
Samenvatting in het Nederlands (Summary in Dutch)

Dit proefschrift bevat resultaten uit onderzoek van de ongetypeerde λ-calculus (lees: ,,lambda calculus”). De λ-calculus is een formeel systeem dat in de jaren 1930 is ontworpen en is sindsdien onderzoeksonderwerp in informatica en filosofie:

∘

Filosofie
>
Formele Logica
>
Herschrijven
>
λ-Calculus

∘

Informatica
>
Programmeertalen en Compilerconstructie
>
Functionele Programmeertalen
>
λ-Calculus

Het onderzoek is voornamelijk gemotiveerd door de rol van de λ-calculus als basis van functionele programmeertalen. Het doel van het onderzoeksproject was om de uitvoering van in functionele programeertalen geschreven programma’s efficiënter te maken door het verhogen van
sharing
. Het Engelse begrip sharing duidt het fenomeen aan dat een berekening
gedeeld
wordt, dat wil zeggen dat een waarde die op meerderen plekken nodig is niet iedere keer opnieuw wordt uitgerekend, maar dat die waarde slechts één keer wordt uitgerekend en dat het resultaat vervolgens op deze plekken wordt hergebruikt. Om dit te bereiken worden functionele talen doorgaans als graafherschrijfsysteem geïmplementeerd. In een graaf kan een knoop meerdere inkomende kanten hebben. Op die manier wordt die knoop en zijn opvolgers gedeeld. Maar vaak moet tijdens de uitvoering een gedeelde subgraaf worden ,,ontdeeld” of ,,ontvouwd”. Hoe later en hoe voorzichtiger het ontdelen kan worden doorgevoerd des te hoger is de graad van sharing en de hierdoor bespaarde berekeningskosten. Er zijn verschillende evaluatiemodellen met verschillende graden van sharing. In dit proefschrift focussen wij niet op het dynamische delen tijdens de uitvoering maar op het statische delen
voor
de uitvoerig van het programma. Het delen tijdens de uitvoering is namelijk gebaseerd op de initiële graad van sharing van de graaf die door de compiler van een programma wordt geconstrueerd. Deze graaf wordt vaak in een taal beschreven die gebaseerd is op de
λ-calculus met
\mathsf{letrec}
(of kort:
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
). Zo noemen we de λ-calculus waarvan de termen niet alleen normale
\lambda
-termen zijn maar ook termen met voorkomens van de zogenoemde
\mathsf{letrec}
-constructie. De
\mathsf{letrec}
-constructie maakt het mogelijk om een graafstructuur en dus sharing direct in de programmeercode uit te drukken.

In dit proefschrift bestuderen we sharing in
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
. Het centrale begrip voor hoe we een
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term beschouwen is de
ontvouwingssemantiek
: We beschouwen een
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term als een representatie van een (potientieel) oneindige term. De rechtvaardiging hiervoor is dat dit overeenkomt met hoe functionele talen geëvalueerd worden: voordat een β-reductie plaatsvindt wordt het deel van de graaf dat de redex bevat eerst ontvouwd en het ontvouwen van een
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term heeft (in de limiet) een (potentieel oneindige) λ-term als resultaat. We houden ons niet verder bezig met β-reductie maar onderzoeken uitsluitend ontvouwing van
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-termen.

Hoofdstuk
0
introduceert het
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-formalisme samen met een herschrijfsysteem om
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-termen te ontvouwen. In de volgende drie hooftstukken bestuderen we dit herschrijfsysteem en beantwoorden o. a. de volgende vragen:

Welke oneindige λ-termen kunnen als eindige
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-termen worden uitgedrukt? Met anderen woorden: hoe expressief is de taal
\boldsymbol{λ}_{\mathsf{letrec}}
? In hoofdstuk
1
karakteriseren we de verzameling van
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-uitdrukbaren λ-termen, dus die λ-termen die de ontvouwing van eindige
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-termen zijn.

Wat zijn goede graafrepresentaties voor
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-uitdrukbare termen? De
\mathsf{letrec}
-constructie dient ertoe om een graafstructuur uit te drukken. In hoofdstuk
2
vragen we ons af hoe precies deze grafen kunnen worden geformaliseerd en we identificeren een formalisatie die voor de volgende vragen nuttig blijkt.

Hoe kunnen we bepalen of twee
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-termen dezelfde ontvouwing hebben? Hoe kunnen we van een
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term een equivalente maar zo compact mogelijke variante berekenen? Bestaat er zoiets als een zo compact mogelijke variant? In hoofdstuk
3
tonen we aan dat er voor iedere klasse van equivalente
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-termen een maximaal compacte vorm bestaat. We ontwikkelen praktische en efficiënte methoden om deze vorm te berekenen en om te bepalen of twee
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-termen dezelfde ontvouwing hebben.

Chapter 10
Lay Summary

This summary is intended to give the casual reader an idea what this thesis is about.

In this thesis I study a very specific subject on the intersection of the fields of computer science and philosophy, namely a formal system called the λ-calculus (read ‘lambda calculus‘). The λ-calculus can be placed into these two fields of science as follows:

∘

Philosophy
>
Formal Logic
>
Rewriting Systems
>
λ-Calculus

∘

Computer Science
>
Programming Languages and Compiler Construction
>
Functional Programming Languages
>
λ-Calculus

In order to convey the meaning of the thesis title, let us first establish what computers and programming languages are all about.

A computer is a machine for processing information. It has input channels (like a touchpad or a microphone) to receive data; it processes the data and performs computations; and it has output channels (like a display or a connector to another device) to convey the result of the computation, by making the result visible, or carrying out some mechanical activity.

The behaviour of computers is determined by their programming. They are
programmable
, meaning their behaviour can be changed by a programmer. But moreover, a computer
requires
programming to function; there is no intelligence inherent to the device itself; it is the programmer that imbues the machine with his ideas through programming.

The programmer’s ideas (say, a method to increase the contrast in a photograph) have to be expressed such that it can be understood by the computer. The language that a computer understands is its
machine language
which is usually a very simple language and which is different for each type of computer. It consists of a collection of basic instructions that, when carried out by the computer, each make very small changes to the computer’s memory. It is difficult and cumbersome for a programmer to express sophisticated ideas using a machine language.

Today, programmers do not need to program directly with machine languages, but have a vast number of
programming languages
at their disposal. Programming languages are languages designed to make the task of programming more efficient and convenient, by giving programmers useful, more powerful means to encode their ideas. A program called
compiler
then translates the code written in a programming language into machine language, which subsequently can be executed by the machine.
A very simple compiler that uses the most straightforward way to translate a programming language into machine language produces very inefficient machine language, which leads to a slower execution of the program. This is especially the case for more complex programming languages, which provide the programmer with a higher level of convenience. Modern compilers produce more efficent machine language by analysing the original program and then translating it in a more sophisticated way. Such a compiler is called an
optimising
compiler.

This thesis focusses on one specific sub-class of programming languages called
functional programming languages
. They differ from
imperative programming languages
, which form the mainstream of today’s programming languages. Code written in an imperative programming language can be regarded as a list of instructions which are executed sequentially, one by one. Code written in a functional programming languages, on the other hand, more resembles a set of mathematical equations, by which the result of the computation is defined.

While functional programming has for decades led a niche existence mostly limited to the academic world, its merits and its elegance are being ever more recognised, and is steadily gaining ground. Today functional programming languages are employed in commerce and in industry, and modern imperative programming languages incorporate more and more features from functional programming languages.

Functional programming has its roots in the λ-calculus, a formal system developed in the 1930s. It is a
rewriting system
; that means it acts on a specific kind of formal expressions (λ-terms) which can be rewritten in a rewriting step to a different term by a fixed set of rewriting rules. It is a sort of minimalistic programming language; the algorithm and the input are expressed as a λ-term; the computation consists of repeatedly rewriting the term according to the rewriting rules of the λ-calculus until it cannot be rewritten any further; the term one obtains in the end is the result of the computation.

There has been a multitude of extensions of the λ-calculus. One such extension is the inclusion of a language construct called
\mathsf{letrec}
, which we call the ‘λ-calculus with
\mathsf{letrec}
’ or in short
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
. The
\mathsf{letrec}
is a syntactic element which allows for function definitions. Function definitions are equations which bind terms to names. A bound term can be referenced by its name and thus used multiple times at different places, thereby introducing ‘sharing’. In the course of performing a computation in
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
the definitions need to be
unfolded
. Unfolding is the process of replacing occurrences of function names by their definition. As function names may occur within their own definition unfolding may go on forever resulting an infinite term. This thesis is all about the
\mathsf{letrec}
-construct and unfolding; it tackles questions like:

∘

Which are the infinite terms that can be obtained by unfolding of finite
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms?

∘

When do two given
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms have the same unfolding?

∘

How can we find a maximally compact form of a given
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-term?

∘

What is a suitable graph representation for
λ_{\mathsf{letrec}}
-terms?

These results provide compilers with further opportunities for analysis and for a more optimised translation which might speed up the execution of programs written in a functional programming languages. Furthermore they may also help reasoning about programs and thus promote further research about functional programming languages.

Chapter 11
